The straightening law in a pseudo-ASL results in divisibility of standard monomials not always being as clear-cut as divisibility of ordinary monomials in a polynomial ring; put another way, because of the straightening law, many pseudo-ASLs (and ASLs) of interest are not monomial pseudo-ASLs. However, because of the importance of monomial ideals and divisibility of monomials in Gröbner basis theory, we find it useful to have an auxiliary monomial pseudo-ASL
A_{lt}
associated to any pseudo-ASL
A
, in which the leading terms will live, and where divisibility in the auxiliary algebra will govern what we mean for one standard monomial to “divide” another. This approach is similar to the “graded structures” approach developed by Robbiano
[
Rob86
]
and Mora
[
Mor88
,
Mor94
]
for Gröbner bases in other settings. We encapsulate our desired monomial pseudo-ASL in the following definition, and we give two key examples below, that exist relative to any pseudo-ASL term order.

Definition 4.23

(store=defalt, note=Algebra of leading terms)
.

Given an
R
-pseudo-ASL
(A,H,\Sigma)
with pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq
, we say that an algebra
A_{lt}
is an
algebra of leading terms
for
(A,H,\Sigma,\preceq)
, if

(1)

A_{lt}
is also an
R
-pseudo-ASL governed by the same
(H,\Sigma)
, and

(2)

if
\pi_{lt}
denotes the unique
R
-linear bijection
A\to A_{lt}
that restricts to the identity map on standard monomials, then for all standard monomials
m
,
m^{\prime}
,

\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})=0,\qquad\text{or}\qquad\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime})).

Lemma 4.24
.

If
A_{lt}
is an algebra of leading terms for some
(A,\preceq)
, then
A_{lt}
is a monomial pseudo-ASL.

Proof.

Suppose
m_{1},m_{2}\in A_{lt}
are standard monomials. If
m_{1}m_{2}=0
we are done. Otherwise, let
\overline{m}_{i}=\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m_{i})
. Then by assumption
\pi_{lt}(\overline{m}_{1})\pi_{lt}(\overline{m}_{2})=m_{1}m_{2}\neq 0
. Thus, by Definition
4.1.3
,

\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\overline{m}_{1}\overline{m}_{2}))=\pi_{lt}(\overline{m}_{1})\pi_{lt}(\overline{m}_{2})=m_{1}m_{2}.

Since the left-hand side here is
\pi_{lt}
applied to a standard term, it is, itself, a standard term, and we are done.
∎

Notation 4.25
.

Henceforth when we write
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
it is assumed that
A
is an
R
-pseudo-ASL,
\preceq
is a pseudo-ASL term order, and
A_{lt}
is an algebra of leading terms for
(A,\preceq)
.

Observation 4.26
.

If
A
is an ASL on
(H,\leq,\Sigma)
, and
\preceq
is a pseudo-ASL term order on
A
, then any algebra of leading terms
A_{lt}
relative to
(A,\preceq)
is an ASL on the same
(H,\leq,\Sigma)
.

Proof.

Since the only additional structure on an ASL relative to a pseudo-ASL is the partial order
\leq
on
H
, and the fact that the straightening laws satisfy the ASL condition, that is all we need to show. However, since the monomial occurring on the RHS of a (pseudo-)straightening law in
A_{lt}
is among the monomials occurring on the RHS of the straightening law in
A
, the ASL condition is automatically satisfied.
∎

Definition 4.27

(
A_{gen}
and
A_{disc}
— two
extreme
algebras of leading terms)
.

(1)

(cf. discrete ASL
[
dCEP82
]
) Given
(H,\Sigma)
, the
discrete (pseudo-)ASL
is defined as
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\langle\Sigma\rangle$}\right.}
; we denote this by
A_{disc}
.

(2)

Given
(A,H,\Sigma,\preceq)
, we define the
generic algebra of leading terms
, denoted
A_{gen}
, as the
R
-algebra with
R
-basis the standard monomials
\W^{H}\backslash\Sigma
, and with multiplication defined as follows. Let
\pi_{lt}\colon A\to A_{gen}
be the
R
-linear bijection that is the identity on standard monomials. Given two standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A
, we define multiplication in
A_{gen}
by

(4.5)

\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime}))

for all standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A
, and then extending by linearity.

The definition of
A_{gen}
amounts to taking the straightening rules in
A
and “truncating” them after the leading term on their right-hand sides. That is, if
m
is an element of
\Sigma
, then the rewriting rule for
m
in
A_{gen}
is defined to be
m=\text{LT}_{sm}(m)
; note that in the definition of
A_{gen}
we include (
4.5
) for
all

mm^{\prime}\in\Sigma
, not just the generators of
\Sigma
. The fact that we have to impose (
4.5
) for all
mm^{\prime}\in\Sigma
and not just generators is closely related to the fact that, even in an ASL, the straightening rules only for the generators of
\Sigma
need not give a full set of defining relations for the ASL
[
Tru91
,
GP92
]
, though they do in the case of graded ASLs
[
dCEP82
, Prop. 1.1]
.

Proposition 4.28
.

Let
(A,H,\Sigma)
be a pseudo-ASL with a pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq
. Then the discrete pseudo-ASL
A_{disc}
and the generic algebra of leading terms
A_{gen}
are both algebras of leading terms for
(A,H,\Sigma,\preceq)
.

It follows from Observation
4.1.3
that if
A
is an ASL governed by
(H,\leq,\Sigma)
, then
A_{disc}
and
A_{gen}
are ASLs governed by the same
(H,\leq,\Sigma)
as well. Whereas
A_{disc}
was already considered in
[
dCEP82
]
as a deformation of an ASL to one that is a quotient of a polynomial ring by a monomial ideal,
A_{gen}
provides an ASL that is similarly related to the original ASL
A
, but now is a quotient of
R[H]
by a binomial ideal. (We also note that
A_{disc}
is always an ASL, even if
A
is not an ASL; we do not know whether this holds for
A_{gen}
.)

Proof of Proposition
4.1.3
.

[A_{disc}{}]
: Since the standard monomials are precisely those not in
\Sigma
, and the monomials in
\Sigma
are the only monomials occurring in the
R[H]
-ideal
\langle\Sigma\rangle
, the standard monomials certainly form an
R
-basis for
A_{disc}
. It remains to show that
A_{disc}
satisfies the multiplicative property of Definition
4.1.3
. Towards that end, let
m,m^{\prime}\in A
be two standard monomials. If
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})=0
, then we are done. If
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})\neq 0
, then by definition of
A_{disc}
as
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\langle\Sigma\rangle$}\right.}
, we must have that
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})
does not lie in
\Sigma
, hence is a standard term. Thus, since the product
mm^{\prime}
in
R[H]
is not in
\Sigma
, we have that in
A
, no straightening occurs in the product
mm^{\prime}
; thus we have that
mm^{\prime}
is a standard term in
A
as well, and in fact must be the same standard term in
A
as it is in
A_{disc}
. Since
mm^{\prime}
is a standard term, we have
mm^{\prime}=\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime})
, and since it is the same standard term as in
A_{disc}
, we have
\pi_{lt}(mm^{\prime})=\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})
. Putting these together, we get
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime}))
, as desired.

[A_{gen}]
: First, we show that
A_{gen}
is in fact an associative algebra. For this, it suffices to prove associativity for standard monomials, as then full associativity follows by bilinearity of the product, since the standard monomials span (in fact are a basis for)
A_{gen}
by construction. Towards this end, let
m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}\in A
be standard monomials. We have

\displaystyle(\pi_{lt}(m_{1})\pi_{lt}(m_{2}))\pi_{lt}(m_{3})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1}m_{2}))\pi_{lt}(m_{3})

[by definition of
A_{gen}
]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1}m_{2})m_{3}))

[by definition of
A_{gen}
]

(4.6)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1}m_{2})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3})))

\displaystyle\text{[since $m_{3}$ is standard]}.

Similarly, we get

(4.7)

\pi_{lt}(m_{1})(\pi_{lt}(m_{2})\pi_{lt}(m_{3}))=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{2}m_{3}))).

So it is necessary and sufficient to show that

\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1}m_{2})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3}))=\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{2}m_{3})).

If
\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{2})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3})\neq 0
, then by (
4.4
), both of the preceding are equal to
\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{2})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3}))
, as desired.

On the other hand, if
\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{2})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3})=0
, we use the following lemma:

Lemma 4.29
.

Given
(A,\preceq)
, if
m_{1},m_{2}.m_{3}
are three standard monomials in
A
then

m_{1}m_{2}m_{3}=0\Rightarrow\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1}m_{2})m_{3}=0.

Proof.

If
m_{1}m_{2}=0
, then
\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1}m_{2})=0
and we are done. Otherwise, let
m_{1}m_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}m^{\prime}_{i}
for nonzero
r_{i}\in R
and standard monomials
m^{\prime}_{i}
, with

\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1}m_{2})=m^{\prime}_{1}\succ m^{\prime}_{2}\succ\dotsb\succ m^{\prime}_{k}.

By assumption we have

\sum r_{i}m^{\prime}_{i}\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3})=\sum r_{i}m^{\prime}_{i}m_{3}=0.

But by
(ATO-2)
, if
m^{\prime}_{1}m_{3}\neq 0
, then we have
\text{LT}_{sm}(m^{\prime}_{1}m_{3})\succ\text{LT}_{sm}(m^{\prime}_{i}m_{3})
for all
i>1
such that
m^{\prime}_{i}m_{3}\neq 0
, and thus no other terms could cancel out
\text{LT}_{sm}(m^{\prime}_{1}m_{3})
. Thus the term
\text{LT}_{sm}(m^{\prime}_{1}m_{3})
must vanish since
\sum r_{i}m^{\prime}_{i}m_{3}=0
, hence we have
m^{\prime}_{1}m_{3}=0
, as claimed.
∎

Returning to
A_{gen}
, it remains to handle the case that
\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{2})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3})=0
. Applying Lemma
4.1.3
once as written and once with indices permuted, we then get

\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1}m_{2})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3})=0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1})\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{2}m_{3})=0.

Thus, in this case, both (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) are equal to 0, and therefore multiplication in
A_{gen}
is associative.

So far, we have that
A_{gen}
is an associative
R
-algebra with
R
-basis given by the standard monomials. By construction, if
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})\neq 0
, we have
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime}))
, so
A_{gen}
satisfies the equation required in Definition
4.1.3
, and is thus an algebra of leading terms for
(A,\preceq)
.
∎

In other situations, such as the Weyl algebra
[
SST13
, Section 1.1]
, the role of the algebra of leading terms is played by the associated graded ring. In fact, although we will not need it in the sequel, we show that
A_{gen}
is the associated graded algebra, when
A
is graded in the natural way by the monoid of standard monomials:

Proposition 4.30
.

Every algebra of leading terms is a graded pseudo-ASL, graded by the monoid
\mathcal{M}
of standard monomials (as in Remark
4.1.2
). Furthermore, given
(A,\preceq)
,
A
is filtered by
\mathcal{M}
, and
A_{gen}
is the associated graded ring of this filtration.

Proof.

Given
(A,\preceq)
, let
\mathcal{M}
be the monoid of standard monomials associated to
A_{gen}
as in Remark
4.1.2
. Since the
m
-graded piece consists precisely of the
R
-multiples of
m
, and the standard monomials form an
R
-basis of
A
because
A
is a pseudo-ASL, we have that

A=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathcal{M}}Rm=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathcal{M}}A_{m},

where here
A_{m}
denotes the
m
-graded piece (and not, say, the localization); and the same direct sum decomposition holds for any
A_{lt}
. By construction of the monoid of standard monomials, in
A_{lt}
we have
(A_{lt})_{m}\cdot(A_{lt})_{m^{\prime}}\subseteq(A_{lt})_{mm^{\prime}}
, so
A_{lt}
is
\mathcal{M}
-graded (here we write the subscripts of our grading multiplicatively rather than additively), where if
mm^{\prime}=0
in
A
(and hence also in
\mathcal{M}
), we understand that
(A_{lt})_{mm^{\prime}}
on the RHS of the preceding equation to simply mean
(A_{lt})_{0}:=0
.

In
A
, since
\mathcal{M}
was defined by taking the leading term of
mm^{\prime}
, we have
A_{m}\cdot A_{m^{\prime}}\subseteq A_{\preceq mm^{\prime}}
, so
A
is
\mathcal{M}
-filtered. Furthermore, we have
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A_{\preceq mm^{\prime}}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$A_{\prec mm^{\prime}}$}\right.}\cong A_{mm^{\prime}}
, and it immediately follows that
A_{gen}
is the associated graded of
A
as an
\mathcal{M}
-filtered pseudo-ASL.
∎

Remark 4.31
.

In fact,
A_{gen}
is in some sense the “universal” algebra of leading terms, in that any algebra of leading terms for
(A,\preceq)
is obtained from
A_{gen}
by further forcing certain products of standard monomials to be 0. Similarly, we see that
A_{disc}
is the “opposite extreme,” in the sense that starting from any
A_{lt}
, one can recover
A_{disc}
by forcing certain additional products of standard monomials to be 0. For a conjectural geometric version of this picture in terms of flat deformations, see Conjecture
9.2
.

Proposition 4.32
.

If
\preceq
is a pseudo-ASL term order on the pseudo-ASL
A
, and
A_{lt}
is an algebra of leading terms for
(A,\preceq)
, let
\preceq_{lt}
denote the order on the standard monomials of
A_{lt}
that agrees with
\preceq
, i.e.

m\preceq_{lt}m^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)\preceq\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m^{\prime}).

Then
\preceq_{lt}
is a pseudo-ASL term order on
A_{lt}
.

Proof.

For any standard monomial
m\in A_{lt}
, we have

\displaystyle 1_{A}\preceq\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)\qquad

[by Axiom
(ATO-1)
on
A
]

\displaystyle\qquad\iff

\displaystyle\pi_{lt}^{-1}(1_{A_{lt}})\preceq\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)\qquad

[by definition of
\pi_{lt}
]

\displaystyle\qquad\iff

\displaystyle 1_{A_{lt}}\preceq_{lt}m\qquad

\displaystyle\text{[by definition of $\preceq_{lt}$]},

thus establishing Axiom
(ATO-1)
for
(A_{lt},\preceq_{lt})
.

To verify Axiom
(ATO-2)
for
\preceq_{lt}
, suppose
f,g,h,k
are standard monomials in
A_{lt}
with
f\prec_{lt}g
and
h\preceq_{lt}k
, and
fh
and
gk
both nonzero. Let
f^{\prime}=\pi_{lt}^{-1}(f)
, and similarly for
g^{\prime},h^{\prime},k^{\prime}
. By the definition of
\preceq_{lt}
, we have
f^{\prime}\prec g^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}\preceq k^{\prime}
.

Next, we claim that
f^{\prime}h^{\prime}\neq 0
and
g^{\prime}k^{\prime}\neq 0
. We show this for
f^{\prime}h^{\prime}
, the argument for
g^{\prime}k^{\prime}
being similar,
mutatis mutandis
. By Definition
4.1.3
, since

0\neq fh=\pi_{lt}(f^{\prime})\pi_{lt}(h^{\prime}),

we have that

\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f^{\prime}h^{\prime}))=\pi_{lt}(f^{\prime})\pi_{lt}(h^{\prime})=fh\neq 0.

And since
\pi_{lt}
applied to the leading term of
f^{\prime}h^{\prime}
is nonzero,
f^{\prime}h^{\prime}
itself must be nonzero.

Finally, since
\preceq
is a pseudo-ASL term order, we have
\text{LT}_{sm}(f^{\prime}h^{\prime})\prec\text{LT}_{sm}(g^{\prime}k^{\prime})
. But now, by Definition
4.1.3
, applying
\pi_{lt}
to both sides we get

\displaystyle\pi_{lt}\left(\text{LT}_{sm}(f^{\prime}h^{\prime})\right)\preceq_{lt}\pi_{lt}\left(\text{LT}_{sm}(g^{\prime}k^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\pi_{lt}(f^{\prime})\pi_{lt}(h^{\prime})\prec_{lt}\pi_{lt}(g^{\prime})\pi_{lt}(k^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle fh\prec_{lt}gk,

as desired. Thus
\preceq_{lt}
is a pseudo-ASL term order on
A_{lt}
.
∎

Corollary 4.33

(of Proposition
4.1.3
)
.

Given
(A,\preceq)
and two distinct standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}
, if there exists a standard term
t
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(mt)=m^{\prime}
, then
m\prec m^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Then existence of such a
t
is equivalent to the divisibility
\pi_{lt}(m)\mid\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})
in
A_{gen}
. As
A_{gen}
is a monomial pseudo-ASL (Lemma
4.1.3
), and
\prec
induces a pseudo-ASL term order on
A_{gen}
by Proposition
4.1.3
, Lemma
4.1.2
applies, so we get that
\pi_{lt}(m)\prec\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})
. As the term order on
A
and
A_{gen}
give the same order on standard monomials, we get
m\prec m^{\prime}
.
∎

The following result is key to ensuring that proofs and algorithms may proceed by downward inductions that reduce the leading term of a given element of
A
, and be guaranteed to reach a base case in finitely many steps.

Proposition 4.34
.

Pseudo-ASL term orders are well-orders.

Interestingly, although the statement of this proposition does not mention monomial pseudo-ASLs nor algebras of leading terms, the proof uses them in a crucial way, in order to apply Lemma
4.1.2
and an analogue of the “usual” Noetherianity-based proof (e.g., in polynomial rings).

Proof outline.

Given a pseudo-ASL
A
and pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq
on
A
, by Proposition
4.1.3
, there exists at least one algebra of leading terms
A_{lt}
for
(A,\preceq)
. By Proposition
4.1.3
,
\preceq
is isomorphic to a pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq_{lt}
on the standard monomials in
A_{lt}
. So it suffices to show that
\preceq_{lt}
is a well-ordering. The “usual” proof from polynomial rings then works in
A_{lt}
because
A_{lt}
is a monomial pseudo-ASL, and Noetherian.
∎

Proof.

We follow the above outline, showing here that the usual proof works in
A_{lt}
. To show that
\preceq_{lt}
is a well-ordering, for the sake of contradiction suppose that

x_{1}\succ_{lt}x_{2}\succ_{lt}x_{3}\succ_{lt}\dotsb

is an infinite chain of standard monomials in
A_{lt}
that is strictly descending according to
\preceq_{lt}
. Let
I\subseteq A_{lt}
be the ideal generated by all the
x_{i}
. Since
A_{lt}
is Noetherian, there is a finite subset of the
x_{i}
, say
\{x_{i_{1}},\dotsc,x_{i_{k}}\}
, that generates
I
. Since
I
is generated by standard monomials, by Lemma
4.1.2
, a standard monomial
m
is in
I
iff it is divisible by one of
\{x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}},\dotsc,x_{i_{k}}\}
. Thus for all
j
, each
x_{j}
is divisible by at least one of
\{x_{i_{1}},x_{i_{2}},\dotsc,x_{i_{k}}\}
. Now consider
j>\max\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{k}\}
, and suppose without loss of generality, for simplicity of notation, that
x_{j}
is divisible by
x_{i_{1}}
. By Lemma
4.1.2
, we thus have
x_{i_{1}}\preceq x_{j}
. But since
j>i_{1}
, by our original assumption we also have
x_{i_{1}}\succ x_{j}
, contradicting the fact that
\prec
is asymmetric.
∎

4.1.4.
Leading ideals and pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases

Unlike the case of ordinary Gröbner bases, in the case of pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases, in order for the leading ideal to be a monomial ideal, we define the leading ideal to be an ideal of
A_{lt}
rather than of
A
.

Definition 4.35

(store=defleadingideal, note=Leading ideal)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
, for an ideal
I\subseteq A
, its
leading ideal
is

LI_{sm}^{lt}(I):=\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f)):f\in I\rangle\subseteq A_{lt}.

It follows immediately from the definition that the leading ideal is a standard monomial ideal. The next proposition shows that the only standard monomials in
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
are the “expected” ones, namely,
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))
for
f\in I
:

Proposition 4.36
.

Given an
R
-pseudo-ASL
A
, for any ideal
I\subseteq A
,

Span_{R}\{\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f)):f\in I\}=LI_{sm}^{lt}(I).

Proof.

Let
S=\{\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f)):f\in I\}
. We first show that
Span_{R}(S)
is an ideal. By definition
Span_{R}(S)
is an
R
-module. All the remains to show is that
Span_{R}(S)
is closed under products with arbitrary elements of
A_{lt}
. By
R
-bilinearity of the product, it suffices to show that
S\cup\{0\}
is closed under products with standard monomials in
A_{lt}
. Towards that end, let
m\in A_{lt}
be a standard monomial, and let
m’\in S
. If
mm’=0
we’re done. Otherwise, by Definition
4.1.3
we have
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m’)))=mm’
. Since
m’
is in
S
, there is an
f\in I
with
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))=m’
. Since
I
is an ideal,
\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)f
is also in
I
. Thus by definition of
S
,
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\pi^{-1}(m)f))
is also in
S
. Now we have

(4.8)

\displaystyle S\ni\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)f)

\displaystyle=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)\text{LT}_{sm}(f))

[by Proposition
4.1.1
]

(4.9)

\displaystyle=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m’))

(4.10)

\displaystyle=mm’.

Thus
mm’
is in
S
, so
S\cup\{0\}
is closed under multiplication by arbitrary standard monomials, and thus
Span_{R}(S)
is an ideal.
Finally, since
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
is generated by
Span_{R}(S)
, and
Span_{R}(S)
is an ideal, the two are equal.
∎

We shall now define our key notion, i.e. that of pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases.

Definition 4.37

(store=defaslgb,note=Pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
and an ideal
I\subseteq A
, a set
G\subseteq I
is a
pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis
for
I
with respect to
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
if

LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)=\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)):g\in G\rangle.

(The superscript
lt
is to emphasize that the leading ideal is contained in
A_{lt}
, and is not merely
\langle\text{LT}_{sm}(f):f\in I\rangle\subseteq A
.)

Theorem 4.38

(store=thmaslgbexistence, note=Existence of pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
, every ideal
I\subseteq A
has a finite pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis with respect to
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
.

Proof.

Let
I
be an ideal in
A
with leading ideal
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)\subseteq A_{lt}
. Define

S=\{\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f)):f\in I\}.

By definition,
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
is generated by
S
. Since
R
is Noetherian,
A_{lt}
is Noetherian, so
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
is generated by a finite subset of
S
, say
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}
. By definition of
S
, there exist
g_{i}\in I
such that
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i}))=m_{i}
for all
i
. Thus
\{g_{1},\dotsc,g_{k}\}
is a finite pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
.
∎

Proposition
4.1.4
below is an equivalent characterization of the definition of pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis in terms of divisibility of leading terms in
A_{lt}
. This divisibility characterization allows one to answer basic questions about ideals in a pseudo-ASL in an effective manner. For instance, given a pseudo-ASL
A
and an ideal
I
in
A
, using a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis of
I
with respect to any
A_{lt}
, we can check if any
f\in A
belongs to
I
in a simple mechanical way just by using Proposition
4.1.4
.

Definition 4.39

(Divisibility of leading terms)
.

Let
A_{lt}
be an algebra of leading terms for
(A,\preceq)
, and let
f,g\in A
. If
\pi_{lt}(f)\mid\pi_{lt}(g)
(in
A_{lt}
), we write
f\mid_{lt}g
. If
m,m^{\prime}\in A
are standard terms such that
m\mid_{lt}m^{\prime}
, we may further write
\frac{\,m^{\prime}\,}{\,m\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}
for the unique (by Lemma
4.1.2
) standard term
t\in A_{lt}
such that
\pi_{lt}(m)t=\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})
.

Proposition 4.40

(store=propaslgrob-divcharacterization, note=Divisibility characterization of pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
and an ideal
I\subseteq A
, a subset
G\subseteq I
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis if and only if for all
f\in I
, there exists
g\in G
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
.

Proof.

Suppose
G
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis and
f\in I
. Then
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))\in LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
. Since by definition
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
is a standard monomial ideal that is generated by
\{\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)):g\in G\}
, by Lemma
4.1.2
, there is a
g\in G
such that
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))
divides
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))
. This is precisely the definition of
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
.

Conversely, suppose every
f\in I
has its leading term
\mid_{lt}
-divisible by the leading term of some
g\in G
. Let
m
be a standard monomial in
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
. There exists
f\in I
such that
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))=m
. By assumption, there is a
g\in G
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
. Thus
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))
is in
\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)):g\in G\rangle
. Since
m
was an arbitrary standard monomial in
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
and
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
is a standard monomial ideal, it follows that

LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)\subseteq\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)):g\in G\rangle.

The opposite inclusion is immediate from the definition of
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
, so
G
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis.
∎

Theorem 4.41

(store=thmmacaulay, note=Pseudo-ASL Analogue of Macaulay’s Basis Theorem)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
, let
I\subseteq A
be an ideal, and
\rho\colon A\to{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$I$}\right.}
the natural projection homomorphism. Let

B=\left\{\rho(m):m\text{ is a standard monomial and }\pi_{lt}(m)\notin LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)\right\}.

Then
B
is an
R
-linear basis of
{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$I$}\right.}
.

For pseudo-ASLs that are both over a field and monomial, this result is mentioned already in Green
[
Gre99
, p. 36]
. As the proof in our setting follows the proof for ordinary Gröbner bases very closely, we relegate it to Appendix
A
.

4.1.5.
Compatibility, Standard Expressions, and Remainders

The key new concept we need in comparison with ordinary Gröbner bases is the following notion of “compatibility” of two standard monomials. Nearly all the subsequent definitions and results we use will rely in some way on the notion of compatibility.

Definition 4.42

(store=defncompatible, note=Compatible)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
, we say two standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A
are
compatible (with respect to
A_{lt}
)
if
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})\neq 0
.
11
11

11

We tried defining compatibility instead as “if
mm\neq 0
then
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})\neq 0
”, with the intuition that if
A
is not a domain, we ought only to care whether
m
and
m^{\prime}
are “more compatible” in
A
than in
A_{lt}
. That definition led to a number of issues that were sidestepped with our current definition. While there might be a way to get this alternative definition to work, we did not find it.
For arbitrary
f,g\in A
, we say that
f
and
g
are compatible (with respect to
A_{lt}
) if every standard monomial occurring in
f
is compatible with every standard monomial occurring in
g
.

In terms of compatibility, we can rephrase the condition in the definition of algebra of leading terms (Definition
4.1.3
) as: if
m,m^{\prime}
are compatible (with respect to
A_{lt}
), then
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime}))
. Note that, by the defining condition in
A_{lt}
, if two standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A
are compatible, then
mm^{\prime}\neq 0
in
A
as well.

Definition 4.43

(store=defnstandardexpression,note=Standard expression)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
and a list of elements
g_{1},\dotsc,g_{k},f\in A
, a
standard expression
for
f
relative to
g_{1},\dotsc,g_{k}
is a list
r,h_{1},\dotsc,h_{k}\in A
such that:

(1)

We have

(4.11)

f=r+\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}g_{i},

(2)

for any standard term
t
occurring in
r
,
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})\nmid_{lt}t
for all
i\in[k]
,

(3)

\text{LM}_{sm}(f)\succeq\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}g_{i})
for all
i
, and

(4)

for every
i=1,\dotsc,k
,
h_{i}
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
.

When this happens, we say that
r
is
a remainder
of
f
relative to
\{g_{1},\dotsc,g_{k}\}
, or that
f
reduces to
r
relative to
\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{k}\}
.

In general, remainders are not unique; however, the next proposition shows that they are unique when the
g_{i}
form a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis.

Proposition 4.44
.

Let
I\subseteq A
be an ideal and let
G\subseteq I
be an pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis of
I
with respect to
(\preceq,A_{lt})
. Then for every
f\in A
, there exists remainder
r
of
f
relative to
\{g_{1},\dotsc,g_{k}\}
, and this remainder is unique. Furthermore, the remainder is 0 if and only if
f
is in
I
.

In the case where
A
is a monomial pseudo-ASL over a field, and the product of standard monomials is a standard monomial (not just a standard term), this proposition follows directly from Green’s
[
Gre99
, Prop 2.7]
. In our setting, we must carefully use both
A
and
A_{lt}
(which is always a monomial pseudo-ASL), but the basic idea of the proof is otherwise similar.

Proof.

[Existence]. We will show this by induction on
\text{LM}_{sm}(f)
(which works because
\preceq
is a well-order, by Proposition
4.1.3
). The base case is
\text{LM}_{sm}(f)=0
, which is trivial. For the inductive step, as long as there is a
g\in G
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
, we proceed as follows. In this case, there exists a standard term
h\in A_{lt}
such that
h\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))
. Setting
h^{\prime}=\pi_{lt}^{-1}(h)
, we have

(4.12)

\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))=h\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))=\pi_{lt}(h^{\prime})\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(h^{\prime}g)),

where the third equality is due to Definition
4.1.3
, since we have that
h^{\prime}
and
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
are compatible, since
\pi_{lt}(h^{\prime})\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))\neq 0
.

Now consider
f^{\prime}=f-h^{\prime}g
. By Equation (
4.12
),
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(h^{\prime}g))
, and since
\pi_{lt}
is
R
-linear and is the identity on standard monomials,
\text{LT}_{sm}(f)=\text{LT}_{sm}(h^{\prime}g)
, implying that
\text{LT}_{sm}(f^{\prime})\prec\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
. Therefore, this procedure can be applied inductively till we are left with
r
such that either
r=0
(which we will show happens iff
f\in I
), or
\forall g\in G,\;\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\nmid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(r)
(which we will show happens iff
f\not\in I
). Consequently, we will have
f=r+\sum_{g\in G}h_{g}g
, where
h_{g}\in A
.

If
r=0
, the proposition is trivially true, so we only have to consider the case where
r\neq 0
. We can thus continue the procedure with lower terms of
r
as needed, starting with the greatest standard term appearing in
r
, say
t
, such that for some
g\in G
,
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}t
. As long as we proceed with greatest-first in this fashion, the induction continues, thus producing for us an
r
such that every standard term
t
appearing in
r
is not
\mid_{lt}
-divisible by
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
for any
g\in G
.

The
h_{g}
are compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
by construction. Furthermore, every standard monomial
m
we added to
h_{g}
has the property that
\text{LT}_{sm}(mg)
is either one of the terms of
f
, so we have
\text{LT}_{sm}(mg)\preceq\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
, or one of the terms of a previously-added
m^{\prime}g^{\prime}
, in which case we have
\text{LT}_{sm}(mg)\preceq\text{LT}_{sm}(m^{\prime}g^{\prime})\preceq\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
(by induction “upward towards
\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
”). Thus the above produces a standard expression for
f
relative to
G
.

[Uniqueness]. To see uniqueness, suppose we have two such decompositions:

f=r+\sum_{g\in G}h_{g}g=r^{\prime}+\sum_{g\in G}h^{\prime}_{g}g.

Then we have that

r-r^{\prime}=\sum_{g\in G}(h_{g}-h^{\prime}_{g})g\in I.

Thus there must exist a
g\in G
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(r-r^{\prime})
by Proposition
4.1.4
, since
G
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
. But
\text{LM}_{sm}(r-r^{\prime})
appears in either
r
or
r^{\prime}
, so by construction of
r
and
r^{\prime}
, there cannot exist a
g\in G
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LM}_{sm}(r-r^{\prime})
. This in turn means that there cannot be any non-zero terms in
r-r^{\prime}
, so we have
r-r^{\prime}=0
, as desired.

[
r=0\Leftrightarrow f\in I
] Each step of the above algorithm preserves the coset
f+I
, because we only ever add elements of
I
(of the form
h^{\prime}g
) to
f
. In particular,
r+I=f+I
. Thus if
r=0
, then
f
is in
I
. Conversely, if
f
is in
I
, then so is every intermediate
f^{\prime}
produced at every step of the above algorithm, since we have
f+I=f^{\prime}+I
. By Proposition
4.1.4
, there is thus some
g\in G
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(f^{\prime})
, so the algorithm can continue to the next stage. The only way this terminates—which it must since the leading term is decreasing every time and
\preceq
is a well-order—is when
r=0
.
∎

4.2.
Reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases

Definition 4.45

(store=defnreducedgb, note=Irredundant/Reduced Gröbner Basis)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
, a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis
G
is
irredundant
if there is no pair
g,g^{\prime}\in G
of distinct elements such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(g^{\prime})
, and
G
is
reduced
if for every
g\in G
, for each standard monomial
m
appearing in
g
, there is no distinct
g^{\prime}\in G
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g^{\prime})\mid_{lt}m
, and furthermore,
\text{LC}_{sm}(g)=1
for all
g\in G
.

Being irredundant follows from being reduced, as the condition for irredundancy only depends on the leading monomials of the elements of
G
, while the condition for being reduced is similar but applies to all standard monomials appearing in any element of
G
. Nonetheless, conceptually it is often easier to split into two steps: first make a Gröbner basis irredundant, and then make it reduced.

The next result and its proof follow the standard treatment; we include the full details in Appendix
A
to ensure no subtlety is overlooked.

Theorem 4.46

(store=thmreduced, note=Existence, uniqueness, and algorithm for reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
and an ideal
I\subseteq A
, there is a unique reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
with respect to
(\preceq,A_{lt})
.

Furthermore, there is an algorithm that takes as input a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis
G
and produces as output a reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis.

One might attempt to bound the runtime of this algorithm in terms of the total number of standard monomials appearing in every element of
G
, but we have found this tricky without knowing more details about the straightening law in the pseudo-ASL one is working with. The issue is that when the algorithm uses
tg_{j}
to cancel out a monomial
m
in
g_{i}
, the straightening law might result in
tg_{j}
having more monomials than
g_{j}
itself, thus increasing the total number of monomials in some steps.

Next we will show that the unique reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
relative to
(\preceq,A_{disc})
actually only depends on the leading ideal
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
, and not on any other information about the pseudo-ASL term order. This will be useful for proving existence of universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases in Theorem
4.4
below.

Lemma 4.47
.

Given an ideal
I\subseteq A
in a pseudo-ASL, and two pseudo-ASL term orders
\preceq,\preceq^{\prime}
, suppose that
(LI_{sm}^{lt})_{\preceq}(I)=(LI_{sm}^{lt})_{\preceq^{\prime}}(I)
in
A_{disc}
. Then the reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases for
I
relative to
(\preceq,A_{disc})
and
(\preceq^{\prime},A_{disc})
are equal.

Proof.

Let
G
be the unique reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
relative to
(\preceq,A_{disc})
, and similarly
G^{\prime}
relative to
(\preceq^{\prime},A_{disc})
. It suffices to show that
G\subseteq G^{\prime}
, for then by symmetry, applying the same argument with the roles reversed, we automatically get
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
.

Let
g\in G
and
m=\text{LT}_{sm}^{\preceq}(g)
; then
m
is in
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
. Therefore, by Proposition
4.1.4
, there is some element
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}^{\preceq^{\prime}}(g^{\prime})
divides
m
in
A_{disc}
. We will show that
g^{\prime}=g
.

By Lemma
4.1.2
,
m
is minimal under divisibility in
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)\subseteq A_{disc}
, and thus we must have
\text{LM}_{sm}^{\preceq^{\prime}}(g^{\prime})=m
. Since
G^{\prime}
is reduced, we in fact have

\text{LT}_{sm}^{\preceq^{\prime}}(g^{\prime})=m=\text{LT}_{sm}^{\preceq}(g).

If
g\neq g^{\prime}
, then there is at least one monomial in the support of
g-g^{\prime}
, and it must be different than
\text{LM}_{sm}(g)=\text{LM}_{sm}(g^{\prime})
. Let
m^{\prime}
be the
\preceq
-leading monomial of
g-g^{\prime}
. If
m^{\prime}
occurs in
g
, then, because
m^{\prime}
is the
\preceq
-leading monomial of an element of
I
(namely,
g-g^{\prime}
), we have that there is some
g_{2}\in G
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}^{\preceq}(g_{2})
divides
m^{\prime}
in
A_{disc}
, contradicting the fact that
G
was reduced. On the other hand, if
m^{\prime}
occurs in
g^{\prime}
, then since
(LI_{sm}^{lt})_{\preceq}(I)=(LI_{sm}^{lt})_{\preceq^{\prime}}(I)
in
A_{disc}
,
m^{\prime}
also occurs as the
\preceq^{\prime}
-leading monomial of some element of
I
(not necessarily
g-g^{\prime}
, since it need not be the case that
\text{LM}_{sm}^{\preceq}(g-g^{\prime})=\text{LM}_{sm}^{\preceq^{\prime}}(g-g^{\prime})
). And thus there is some
g^{\prime}_{2}\in G^{\prime}
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}^{\preceq^{\prime}}(g^{\prime}_{2})
divides
m^{\prime}
in
A_{disc}
, contradicting the fact that
G^{\prime}
was reduced. Thus, it must be the case that
g=g^{\prime}
, completing the proof.
∎

4.3.
Relations between different algebras of leading terms

In this subsection we prove several results clarifying the relationships between various algebras of leading terms and their associated pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases. In addition to being a natural question on its own, we will use the results of this section to show the existence of universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases in the next section.

Given
(A,\preceq)
, we say that two algebras of leading terms for
(A,\preceq)
, say
A_{lt}
and
A^{\prime}_{lt}
, are
isomorphic as algebras of leading terms
for
(A,\preceq)
if the bijection
\pi_{lt}\circ(\pi^{\prime}_{lt})^{-1}
(which is the identity on standard monomials) is an isomorphism of algebras
A^{\prime}_{lt}\to A_{lt}
; we denote this by
A_{lt}\cong_{sm}A^{\prime}_{lt}
.

Proposition 4.48

(Characterization of
A_{gen}
in terms of compatibility)
.

Given
(A,\preceq)
, all pairs of standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A
such that
mm^{\prime}\neq 0
are compatible with respect to
A_{lt}
if and only if

A_{lt}\cong_{sm}A_{gen}.

Proof.

Suppose
A_{lt}
is isomorphic to
A_{gen}
as in the statement. By the definition of
A_{gen}
,
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})=0
iff
mm^{\prime}=0
in
A
, thus, all pairs
(m,m^{\prime})
such that
mm^{\prime}\neq 0
in
A
are compatible relative to
A_{gen}
.

Conversely, suppose that all pairs of standard monomials whose products in
A
are nonzero are compatible with respect to
A_{lt}
. For any two standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A
, if
mm^{\prime}\neq 0
, then by the assumption of compatibility we have that
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})\neq 0
. But then by Definition
4.1.3
, we have
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})=\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime}))
. Since the latter holds for any pair of standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A
such that
mm^{\prime}\neq 0
, these are precisely the defining relations of
A_{gen}
, and thus
A_{lt}
is isomorphic to
A_{gen}
as an algebra of leading terms.
∎

Proposition 4.49

(Relationship between pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases for different algebras of leading terms)
.

Suppose that
A_{lt}
and
A^{\prime}_{lt}
are two algebras of leading terms for the same
(A,\preceq)
, and suppose that, for all standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}
, if
mm^{\prime}\neq 0
in
A_{lt}^{\prime}
, then
mm^{\prime}\neq 0
in
A_{lt}
.

Then for any ideal
I\subseteq A
, the reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
with respect to
(\preceq,A_{lt})
is contained in the reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
with respect to
(\preceq,A^{\prime}_{lt})
.

The proof of this result is very similar to that of Lemma
4.2
. There, we fixed the algebra of leading terms to
A_{disc}
and allowed the term order to vary (as long as the leading ideal didn’t change). Here, we allow the algebra of leading terms to vary, but we fix the term order. In the present setting, note that the leading ideal also essentially stays the same: the leading ideals in the two algebras of leading terms will be spanned by the same set of standard monomials, and sent to one another bijectively by
\pi^{\prime}_{lt}\circ\pi_{lt}^{-1}
. (We note that our proof of existence of universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases below will use both Lemma
4.2
and Proposition
4.3
.)

Proof.

Let
G
be the reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
relative to
(\preceq,A_{lt})
and let
G^{\prime}
be the reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
relative to
(\preceq,A^{\prime}_{lt})
. Let
g\in G
; we will show that
g
must be in
G^{\prime}
as well.

Let
m=\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
; then
m
is in
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
in any algebra of leading terms relative to
\preceq
. In particular, the standard monomial
m
must occur in
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
in
A^{\prime}_{lt}
. Therefore, by Proposition
4.1.4
, there is some element
g^{\prime}\in G^{\prime}
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g^{\prime})
divides
m
in
A^{\prime}_{lt}
. We will show that
g^{\prime}=g
.

By Lemma
4.1.2
,
m
is minimal under divisibility in
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)\subseteq A_{lt}
. Now, since
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)\subseteq A_{lt}^{\prime}
consists of the same set of standard monomials as it does in
A_{lt}
, and for any two standard monomials
n,n^{\prime}
that if
n
divides
n^{\prime}
in
A_{lt}^{\prime}
then
n
divides
n^{\prime}
in
A_{lt}
(because the only difference between
A_{lt}
and
A_{lt}^{\prime}
is that more products of standard monomials in
A_{lt}^{\prime}
are zero than in
A_{lt}
), we also have that
m
is minimal under divisibility in
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
in
A_{lt}^{\prime}
. Thus we must have
\text{LM}_{sm}(g^{\prime})=m
. Since
G^{\prime}
is reduced, we in fact have

\text{LT}_{sm}(g^{\prime})=m=\text{LT}_{sm}(g).

If
g\neq g^{\prime}
, then
\text{LM}_{sm}(g-g^{\prime})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(g)
and
\text{LM}_{sm}(g-g^{\prime})\neq 0
. Let
m^{\prime}=\text{LM}_{sm}(g-g^{\prime})
;
m^{\prime}
must occur in either
g
or
g^{\prime}
. If
m^{\prime}
occurs in
g
, then, because
m^{\prime}
is the leading monomial of an element of
I
, we have that there is some
g_{2}\in G
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{2})
divides
m^{\prime}
in
A_{lt}
. But this contradicts the fact that
G
was reduced. On the other hand, if
m^{\prime}
occurs in
g^{\prime}
, then, by the same reasoning, we have that there is some
g^{\prime}_{2}\in G^{\prime}
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}(g^{\prime}_{2})
divides
m^{\prime}
in
A^{\prime}_{lt}
, contradicting the fact that
G^{\prime}
was reduced. Thus, it must be the case that
g=g^{\prime}
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 4.50
.

Given Proposition
4.3
, one might naturally wonder why bother with any algebras of leading terms other than
A_{gen}
, since any other algebras of leading terms can only yield larger reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases. Although the reduced Gröbner basis relative to
A_{gen}
will indeed be the smallest among Gröbner bases relative to all choices of
A_{lt}
, deciding divisibility and computing LCMs relative to other
A_{lt}
may be significantly simpler than in
A_{gen}
. For example, relative to
A_{disc}
the LCM, if it exists, is unique and is the one you would expect based on ordinary monomials (Observation
5.3
); in contrast, relative to
A_{gen}
LCM sets can be much more complicated, see, e.g., Theorem
7.3
for the case of bideterminants. We will also see that
A_{disc}
has certain theoretical advantages, for example, when it comes to universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases (§
4.4
),
A
-modules (§
5
) and computing Krull dimension (§
8
).

4.4.
Universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases

Definition 4.51

(store=defuniversalgb, note=Universal Gröbner basis)
.

Given a pseudo-ASL
A
and an ideal
I\subseteq A
, a set
G\subseteq I
is a
universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis
for
I
if
G
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
relative to all pairs
(\preceq,A_{lt})
consisting of a pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq
on
A
and an algebra of leading terms
A_{lt}
for
(A,\preceq)
.

The usual proof of existence of universal Gröbner bases relies on showing that there are only finitely many possible leading ideals for a given ideal. However, in our case, the leading ideals may reside in different algebras of leading terms, so some care must be taken. The following proof, however, does not rely on there being only finitely many algebras of leading terms, it instead uses the fact that
A_{disc}
is an algebra of leading terms for any order.

Theorem 4.52

(store=thmuniversal, note=Universal Gröbner bases)
.

Every ideal
I\subseteq A
in a pseudo-ASL has a finite universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis.

Green’s Proposition 2.11
[
Gre99
]
gives this result in the case of monomial pseudo-ASLs over a field, under the conditions that
A=A_{lt}
(which is allowed when
A
is a monomial pseudo-ASL) and subject to condition that the product of two standard monomials is a standard monomial (not merely a scalar multiple thereof).

We start by outlining the proof, then will give a key lemma, and give the full proof after the lemma.

Proof outline.

Note that
A_{disc}
is an algebra of leading terms for
A
relative to
all
pseudo-ASL term orders. Thus, for each fixed term order
\preceq
, by Theorem
4.3
, the unique reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis relative to
(\preceq,A_{disc})
is universal among pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases relative to algebras of leading terms for this particular order
\preceq
. This effectively reduces to the case of
A_{disc}
, and in
A_{disc}
, essentially the same proof as for ordinary Gröbner bases shows that each ideal has at most finitely many leading ideals in
A_{disc}
.
∎

Lemma 4.53

(store=lemuniversal)
.

Given a pseudo-ASL
A
and an ideal
I\subseteq A
, among all possible pseudo-ASL term orders,
I
has only finitely many leading ideals in
A_{disc}
.

Since the proof of the lemma is similar to the case of ordinary Gröbner bases, we relegate it to Appendix
A
. We note that in fact the same result holds with
A_{lt}
in place of
A_{disc}
, as long as we restrict to those term orders for which
A_{lt}
is in fact an algebra of leading terms; but we will only need to use the results for
A_{disc}
.

Proof of Theorem
4.4
.

We follow the above outline. Let
I\subseteq A
be an ideal. By Lemma
4.4
, among all possible pseudo-ASL term orders, there are only finitely many leading ideals for
I
in
A_{disc}
, call these
I_{1},\dotsc,I_{k}\subseteq A_{disc}
. For each pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq
, let us say
\preceq
has “type
j
” if the leading ideal of
I
relative to
(\preceq,A_{disc})
is
I_{j}
. By Lemma
4.2
, all term orders of type
j
have the same reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases; let us call this
G_{j}
. By Theorem
4.3
, if
\preceq
is a term order of type
j
and
A_{lt}
is an algebra of leading terms for
(A,\preceq)
, then the reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
relative to
(\preceq,A_{lt})
is contained in
G_{j}
. Thus all reduced pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases for
I
, relative to all choices of pseudo-ASL term order and algebra of leading terms, are contained in
\bigcup_{j=1}^{k}G_{j}
. As each
G_{j}
is finite,
\bigcup G_{j}
is thus a finite universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
.
∎

5.
Syzygies and pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases for modules

5.1.
Definitions and setup

Let
A
be a pseudo-ASL, and
M
a free
A
-module with basis
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{d}
, so in particular
M\cong A^{d}
. We define a
standard monomial
in
M
to be any element of the form
me_{i}
, where
m\in A
is a standard monomial, and similarly a
standard term
in
M
is any element of the form
te_{i}
for a standard term
t\in A
.

Definition 5.1

(pseudo-ASL term order for free modules)
.

Given a pseudo-ASL
A
and a free module
A^{d}
with basis
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{d}
, a
pseudo-ASL term order
on
A^{d}
is total ordering
\preceq
on the standard monomials of
A^{d}
such that

(
MATO-1
)

(Positivity)
e_{i}\preceq me_{i}
for all
i
and all standard monomials
m\in A
.

(
MATO-2
)

(Multiplicativity in leading terms when nonzero) For all
i,j
and all standard monomials
f,g,h\in A
, if
fe_{i}\prec ge_{j}
, then
12
12

12

Why not also include here
h\preceq k
and conclude
\text{LM}_{sm}(fhe_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gke_{j})
? Because we do not have a term order on
A
to use here! This leads us to
(MATO-3)
.

fh\neq 0\text{ and }gh\neq 0\Longrightarrow\text{LM}_{sm}(fhe_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(ghe_{j}).

(
MATO-3
)

(Restriction to each
Ae_{i}
is a pseudo-ASL term order) For all
i
, and all standard monomials
f,g,h,k\in A
, if
fe_{i}\prec ge_{i}
and
he_{i}\preceq ke_{i}
, then

fh\neq 0\text{ and }gk\neq 0\Longrightarrow\text{LM}_{sm}(fhe_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gke_{i}).

Remark 5.2
.

As in, e.g., Green’s approach to Gröbner bases for modules
[
Gre99
]
, the above definition can be gotten from Definition
4.1.1
by building a sort of “semi-direct product algebra” as follows. Suppose
(A,\preceq)
is a pseudo-ASL with pseudo-ASL term order. Then we can define a pseudo-ASL structure on the Abelian group
A\oplus A^{d}
as follows; we will denote the resulting algebra
A\ltimes A^{d}
. Let
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{d}
be a standard free basis for
A^{d}
as an
A
-module. The copy of
A^{d}
will be an ideal in
A\ltimes A^{d}
. Elements of the form
(a,0)
form a sub-ring isomorphic to
A
:
(a,0)(a^{\prime},0)=(aa^{\prime},0)
. Elements of the form
(a,0)
act on elements of the form
(0,*)
as
A
acts on the
A
-module
A^{d}
, viz.
(a,0)\cdot(0,me_{i})=(0,ame_{i})
. And finally, in the subring
A^{d}
, basis elements multiply according to
(0,me_{i})(0,m^{\prime}e_{j})=(0,mm^{\prime}\delta_{ij}e_{i})
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta (=1 if
i=j
and 0 otherwise). (In Green’s approach, in contrast, one would have
(0,*)(0,*)=(0,0)
, but using the Kronecker
\delta
here gives us the above definition—in particular,
(MATO-3)
—which is useful in our developments below.) The pseudo-ASL structure on
A\ltimes A^{d}
will be as follows. Let
H,\Sigma
be the given pseudo-ASL structure on
A
. For
i=0,\dotsc,d
, let
H_{i}
be a copy of
H
, with
H_{0}
generating the copy of
A
, while
H_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq d
generates the subring
\{(0,fe_{i}):f\in A\}
; let
\Sigma_{i}
be an isomorphic copy of
\Sigma
on
H_{i}
. Then we define the pseudo-ASL structure on
A\ltimes A^{d}
by

H^{\prime}:=H_{0}\sqcup\dotsb\sqcup H_{d}

and

\Sigma^{\prime}:=\left\langle\left(\bigcup_{i=0}^{d}\Sigma_{i}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{0\leq i<j\leq d}(H_{i}\cup\{1_{i}\})(H_{j}\cup\{1_{j}\})\right)\right\rangle,

as an ideal in the semigroup
\W^{H^{\prime}}
, where
1_{i}
denotes
e_{i}
if
i\geq 1
and
1_{i}
denotes the copy of
1\in A
if
i=0
(including those in the above corresponds to, e.g., the straightening law
(1,0)\cdot(0,e_{i})=(0,e_{i})
where the LHS is non-standard and the RHS is standard). It is readily verified that this gives a pseudo-ASL structure on
A\ltimes A^{d}
, in which the set of standard monomials is the disjoint union of the sets of standard monomials on each of the
d+1
copies of
A
.
13
13

13

If
A
is an ASL, this construction almost yields an ASL. The straightening rules for each
\Sigma_{i}
are isomorphic to those for
\Sigma
in
A
; the straightening rules on
H_{i}H_{j}
are zero, and the straightening rules on
H_{0}H_{i}
simply encode the action of
A
on
Ae_{i}
, viz.
a^{(0)}b^{(i)}=a^{(i)}b^{(i)}
. The reason this is not quite an ASL is that when we rewrite
a^{(0)}b^{(i)}
as
a^{(i)}b^{(i)}
, we are not guaranteed that for every
x\in H_{i}
there is some
y<x
in
H_{i}
occurring in
a^{(i)}b^{(i)}
. However, if we replace the strict
<
with
\leq
, then this weakened form of the axiom is satisfied, but the weakened form of the axiom is likely to break some inductive arguments about ASLs.

Many of the basic Gröbner-basis-related properties of modules over a pseudo-ASL will follow from the observation that a pseudo-ASL term order on a free module
A^{d}
restricts to an ordinary pseudo-ASL term order on each
1
-dimensional submodule
Ae_{i}
, under the identification
Ae_{i}\cong A
(isomorphism as
A
-modules, but under this isomorphism, we may treat
Ae_{i}
as a pseudo-ASL in order to apply, e.g., Definition
4.1.1
and others).

Proposition 5.3
.

Pseudo-ASL term orders on free modules
A^{d}
are well-orders.

Proof.

Suppose
\preceq
is a pseudo-ASL term order on
A^{d}
. Let
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{d}
be the standard free basis of
A^{d}
. The restriction of
\preceq
to each
Ae_{i}
gives a pseudo-ASL term order on
A
, which we’ll denote by
\preceq_{i}
. By Proposition
4.1.3
, each
\preceq_{i}
is a well-order on
Ae_{i}
. Now suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that
m_{1}\succ m_{2}\succ m_{3}\succ\dotsb
is a strictly decreasing chain of infinitely many standard monomials in
A^{d}
. Let
M_{i}=\{m_{j}:m_{j}\in Ae_{i}\}
. At least one of the
M_{i}
must be infinite, say
M_{i}
. But then
M_{i}
is an infinite strictly decreasing chain according to the pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq_{i}
on
A
, contradicting Proposition
4.1.3
.
∎

Definition 5.4

(store=defmlt, note=Algebra of leading terms for a module; module of leading terms)
.

Given a pseudo-ASL
A
, free module
A^{d}
with basis, a pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq
on
A^{d}
, let
\preceq_{i}
be the pseudo-ASL term order on
A
induced by restricting
\preceq
to
Ae_{i}
and then pulling back along the unique
A
-module isomorphism
Ae_{i}\cong A
. We say that
A_{lt}
is an
algebra of leading terms for
(A^{d},\preceq)
if
A_{lt}
is simultaneously an algebra of leading terms for all
(A,\preceq_{i})
,
i=1,\dotsc,d
. In this case, we say that
A_{lt}^{d}
is a
module of leading terms for
(A^{d},\preceq)
.

Since
A_{disc}
is an algebra of leading terms relative to all pseudo-ASL term orders on
A
, we know that for any free module
A^{d}
that admits a pseudo-ASL term order, there is at least one module of leading terms, namely
A_{disc}^{d}
.

Convention 5.5
.

Henceforth, whenever we write
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
, the notation is understood to mean that
A
is a pseudo-ASL,
A^{d}
is a free
A
-module with basis, and
A_{lt}^{d}
is an module of leading terms for
(A^{d},\preceq)
, and thereby that
A_{lt}
is an algebra of leading terms for
(A^{d},\preceq)
. Note that when
d=1
, this is consistent with our earlier convention about pseudo-ASLs (rather than modules over them).

The next observation justifies the terminology in Definition
5.1
, by completing the analogy with the definition of algebra of leading terms for
A
(Definition
4.1.3
).

Observation 5.6

(store=obsmlt, note=Module of leading terms)
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
, by slight abuse of notation let
\pi_{lt}\colon A^{d}\to A_{lt}^{d}
be the map that applies
\pi_{lt}\colon A\to A_{lt}
coordinate-wise. Then
\pi_{lt}
is an
R
-linear bijection that restricts to the identity map on standard monomials, and for all standard monomials
m\in A,m^{\prime}e_{i}\in A^{d}
, we have

\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime}e_{i})\in\{0,\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime}e_{i}))\}.

Proof.

All the parts except perhaps the last are immediate. For the final property, suppose
m\in A
is a standard monomial and
m^{\prime}e_{i}\in A^{d}
is a standard monomial. Then
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime}e_{i})=\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})e_{i}
, by the definition of
\pi_{lt}
as the coordinate-wise application of
\pi_{lt}
. But by the definition of algebra of leading terms, we have
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})\in\{0,\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime})\}
, and the result then follows from the fact that
\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime}e_{i})=\text{LT}_{sm}(mm^{\prime})e_{i}
.
∎

5.2.
Annihilators over monomial pseudo-ASLs

We start with a lemma about syzygies of standard monomial submodules (the module analogue of standard monomial ideal) of a free module
M
over a monomial pseudo-ASL
A^{\prime}
. (This is a pseudo-ASL analogue of, e.g.,
[
Eis95
, Lemma 15.1]
.)
It is readily verified that Lemma
4.1.2
extends to monomial submodules. Because our algebras of leading terms won’t in general be domains, we must introduce some new terminology and syzygies to take care of this.

Given standard monomials
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}\in(A^{\prime})^{e}
, let
\varphi\colon(A^{\prime})^{d}\to(A^{\prime})^{e}
be the module homomorphism that sends the standard basis elements
e_{i}
to the monomials
m_{i}
. Then the syzygy module
\text{Syz}(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d})
is by definition
\ker(\varphi)
.
There are two types of syzygies that will play an important role for us. We introduce the first type here as it will be useful in the next section for our Buchberger-like criterion; the other type is introduced in Definition
5.4
in a later section, when we get to our pseudo-ASL analogue of Schreyer’s Syzygy Theorem.

Definition 5.7

(Annihilator syzygies)
.

For any
s\in\Ann(m_{i})\subseteq A^{\prime}
, define

\alpha_{i}^{s}:=se_{i}.

Let
\text{LAM}(m_{i})
(for “Least Annihilating Monomials”) be the set of standard monomials
m\in\Ann(m_{i})
that are minimal under divisibility. Say a binomial in
\Ann(m_{i})
is a
proper binomial annihilator
if it is not the sum of two standard terms that are separately in
\Ann(m_{i})
. Define
\text{LAB}(m_{i})
(for ‘‘Least Annihilating Binomials’’) to be the set of normalized (=at least one coefficient is 1)
14
14

14

In the presence of a term ordering, it would make sense to say “leading coefficient 1” here; however, the way we have phrased it here allows these particular definitions and results to be independent of term ordering.
standard proper binomial annihilators in
\Ann(m_{i})
that are minimal under divisibility.

By the usual Noetherianity argument, both
\text{LAM}(m_{i})
and
\text{LAB}(m_{i})
are finite. If a monomial pseudo-ASL admits a pseudo-ASL term order, then by Observation
5.2
, we will see that
\text{LAB}(m_{i})
is in fact empty; we include it here for completeness, even though it won’t play any role in our theory of pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases.

Lemma 5.8

(Annihilators of monomials, cf.
[
ES96
]
)
.

Let
A^{\prime}
be a monomial pseudo-ASL, and let
m_{1}
be a standard monomial in
(A^{\prime})^{e}
. Then
\Ann(m_{1})

is generated by

\left\{\alpha_{1}^{s}:s\in\text{LAM}(m_{1})\cup\text{LAB}(m_{1})\right\}.

(Here we are slightly abusing notation by identifying the ring
A^{\prime}
with the 1-dimensional free module
A^{\prime}
, generated freely by an element we call
e_{1}
.)

Proof.

By Observation
4.1.2
, this follows directly from Eisenbud & Sturmfels
[
ES96
, Cor. 1.7]
.
∎

It follows from Lemma
4.1.2
(
mutatis mutandis
for modules) that in the presence of a pseudo-ASL term order, there are no proper binomial annihilators. We record this as a separate observation for ease of reference:

Observation 5.9
.

Let
A^{\prime}
be a monomial pseudo-ASL such that
(A^{\prime})^{e}
admits a pseudo-ASL term order, and let
m
be a standard monomial in
(A^{\prime})^{e}
. Then there are no proper binomial annihilators for
m
. In particular, it follows from Lemma
5.2
that the annihilator of
m
is generated by the annihilator syzygies for the least annihilating monomials alone.

5.3.
A Buchberger-like criterion for pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
and a submodule
M\subseteq A^{d}
, we would like to find a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis of
M
. In this section we will prove a Buchberger-like criterion for pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases, that will eventually lead to an algorithm in Section
6
.

In the polynomial ring, given two monomials, there is just one least common multiple. However, relative to an arbitrary algebra of leading terms associated to some pseudo-ASL, we need to define the notion of a set of least common multiples of two standard monomials in
A_{lt}
.

Definition 5.10

(Least common standard multiples)
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
and two standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A_{lt}^{d}
, the set of their least common standard multiples,
LCM_{sm}^{lt}(m,m^{\prime})\subseteq A_{lt}^{d}
, consists of the standard monomials that are common multiples of
m
and
m^{\prime}
that are minimal under divisibility. If
m,m^{\prime}\in A^{d}
are standard monomials, we also write
LCM_{sm}^{lt}(m,m^{\prime})
for
LCM_{sm}^{lt}(\pi_{lt}(m),\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime}))
.

Equivalently, we can rephrase this in terms of standard monomial submodules as follows. For a monomial pseudo-ASL
A^{\prime}
, call a submodule
M\subseteq(A^{\prime})^{d}
a
standard monomial (sub)module
if for all
f\in(A^{\prime})^{d}
,
f
is in
M
iff every standard monomial occurring in
f
is in
M
. The basic results on standard monomial ideals in
A^{\prime}
extend to standard monomial submodules
mutatis mutandis
. In particular, by the module analogue of Lemma
4.1.2
,
LCM_{sm}^{lt}(m,m^{\prime})
is the unique minimum generating set of standard monomials for the module
\langle m\rangle\cap\langle m^{\prime}\rangle
, which is standard monomial by the module analogue of Corollary
4.1.2
(whose proof is the same as the first proof of that result,
mutatis mutandis
).

For a concrete example of a pseudo-ASL where there can be multiple LCMs for a given pair of standard monomials, see Section
7.3
where in Theorem
7.3
, given two standard bideterminants, we provide an algorithm to compute all of their least common standard multiples relative to
A_{gen}
.

Observation 5.11
.

Relative to
A_{disc}
, every pair of standard monomials has at most one LCM. Equivalently, if
me_{i},m^{\prime}e_{j}\in A_{disc}^{d}
are standard monomials, then
\langle me_{i}\rangle\cap\langle m^{\prime}e_{j}\rangle
is either 0 or generated by a single element.

Proof.

Recall that the defining ideal of
A_{disc}
is just
\langle\Sigma\rangle\subseteq R[H]
, and hence
A_{disc}
is the quotient of
R[H]
by a monomial ideal. Thus the product of two standard monomials in
A_{disc}
is either zero, or is equal to their product in
\W^{H}
, since no (nonzero) rewriting can occur. In particular, if
m,m^{\prime}
are standard monomials with corresponding exponent vectors
e\colon H\to\W,f\colon H\to\W
, then
m\mid m^{\prime}
iff
e(h)\leq f(h)
for all
h\in H
. It follows that the LCM of two monomials
m
and
m^{\prime}
in
A_{disc}
is the same as their LCM in
R[H]
, namely the monomial whose exponent vector is
\ell(h)=\max\{e(h),f(h)\}
. If this LCM is 0 in
A_{disc}
, then the two monomials do not have any nonzero common multiples, and otherwise this LCM is the only one. For standard monomials in
A_{disc}^{d}
, say
me_{i}
and
m^{\prime}e_{j}
, if
i\neq j
then
\langle me_{i}\rangle\cap\langle m^{\prime}e_{j}\rangle=0
, and if
i=j
, then
\langle me_{i}\rangle\cap\langle m^{\prime}e_{i}\rangle=\langle LCM(m,m^{\prime})e_{i}\rangle
.
∎

Below we define a notion of S-pairs in a module over a pseudo-ASL similar to the notion of S-pairs in polynomial rings. However, since there could be multiple LCM’s (Definition
5.3
) at our level of generality, we could in turn have multiple S-pairs for just one single pair of elements
f,g\in A
. We first generalize divisibility of leading terms to the module setting. If
me_{i},m^{\prime}e_{i}\in A_{lt}^{d}
are standard monomials in
A_{lt}^{d}
supported on the same basis element, we say that
me_{i}
divides
m^{\prime}e_{i}
, and write
me_{i}\mid m^{\prime}e_{i}
, if there exists
t\in A_{lt}
such that
tme_{i}=m^{\prime}e_{i}
(or, equivalently,
tm=m^{\prime}
).

Definition 5.12

(Divisibility of leading terms in modules)
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
and
f,g\in A^{d}
. If
\pi_{lt}(f)\mid\pi_{lt}(g)
, we write
f\mid_{lt}g
. If
m,m^{\prime}\in A^{d}
are standard terms such that
m\mid_{lt}m^{\prime}
, we may further write
\frac{\,m^{\prime}\,}{\,m\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}
for the unique (by the module analogue of Lemma
4.1.2
) standard term
t\in A_{lt}
such that
\pi_{lt}(m)t=\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})
.

Definition 5.13

(S-sets over a pseudo-ASL)
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
,
f,g\in A^{d}
, and
l
a common multiple of their leading terms in
A_{lt}^{d}
, define their S-polynomial with respect to
l
:

S_{sm}^{l}(f,g):=\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(f)\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)\cdot f-\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)\cdot g,

Then define their S-set:

S_{sm}(f,g):=\left\{S_{sm}^{l}(f,g):l\in LCM_{sm}^{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f),\text{LT}_{sm}(g))\right\}\subseteq A^{d},

Observation
5.3
below records how, just like in the polynomial ring, the leading term of all the elements in the S-set of a pair of elements from
A
will be strictly smaller (by order
\preceq
) than the corresponding LCM.

Observation 5.14
.

For each each element of an S-set, by construction, we have,

\text{LT}_{sm}\left(\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(f)\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)\cdot f\right)=\text{LT}_{sm}\left(\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)\cdot g\right)=\pi_{lt}^{-1}(l),

thus

\text{LT}_{sm}\left(S_{sm}^{l}(f,g)\right)\prec\pi_{lt}^{-1}(l).

Proof.

Immediate consequence of Definition
5.3
.
∎

We now prove an analogue of Buchberger’s algorithm/criterion for Gröbner bases.

Definition 5.15

(store=defnsclosed, note=S-closed)
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
, a subset
G\subseteq A^{d}
is
S-closed
if for all pairs
g,g^{\prime}\in G
, every element of
S_{sm}(g,g^{\prime})
reduces to 0 relative to
G
(see Definition
4.1.5
).

In
A^{d}
, there are lifts of the annihilator syzygies from
A_{lt}^{d}
(Definition
5.2
), defined as follows (we will show in Lemma
5.5
the precise sense in which they are “lifts”):

Definition 5.16

(store=defncompatsyzygy, note=Compatibility syzygy)
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
and
G\subseteq A^{d}
, for any
g_{0}\in G
and any standard monomial
m_{0}
that is incompatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{0})
, if
\sum_{g}\ell_{g}g
is a standard expression for
m_{0}g_{0}
, then

m_{0}e_{g_{0}}-\sum_{g\in G}\ell_{g}e_{g}

is a
compatibility syzygy (for
(m_{0},g_{0})
relative to
G
)
.

For the next definition, we need a preparatory observation:

Observation 5.17
.

Suppose
A^{\prime}
is a monomial pseudo-ASL. If
(A^{\prime})^{d}
admits a psuedo-ASL term order, then
(\Ann(t):m)
is a standard monomial ideal in
A^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By definition, we have

(\Ann(t):m)=\{f\in A^{\prime}:fmt=0\}=\Ann(mt).

Since
A^{\prime}
is monomial, we have that
mt\in(A^{\prime})^{d}
is either 0 (in which case the colon ideal is the whole ring, which is technically a standard monomial ideal albeit not a proper one) or a standard term. In the latter case,
\Ann(mt)
is a standard monomial ideal by Observation
5.2
, since we have assumed
(A^{\prime})^{d}
admits a pseudo-ASL term order.
∎

Definition 5.18

(store=defannclosed,note=Ann-closed)
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
, a subset
G\subseteq A^{d}
is
Ann-closed
if there exists a set
S
of compatibility syzygies relative to
G
such that, for all
g\in G
:

(1)

for every
\pi_{lt}(s)\in\text{LAM}(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)))
,
S
contains a compatibility syzygy for
(s,g)
; and

(2)

for every standard monomial
m
that occurs in any
\ell_{g}
in any syzygy in
S
, and for every standard monomial
t
in the unique minimal generating set of the colon ideal
(\Ann(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))):\pi_{lt}(m))
,
S
contains a compatibility syzygy for
(\text{LM}_{sm}(tm),g)
.

In the preceding definition, we have used Observation
5.3
along with the unique minimal generating set from the module analogue of Lemma
4.1.2
.

Theorem 5.19

(store=thmaslbuchberger2, note=Pseudo-ASL analogue of Buchberger’s criterion)
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
, suppose that
G\subseteq A^{d}
has the property that for all
g\in G
,
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
is a non-zerodivisor. Then
G
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis if and only if it is S-closed and Ann-closed.

In particular, the condition about the leading terms in
G
being non-zerodivisors automatically holds when
A
is a domain, which is a common case of application (see also Proposition
4.1.1
).

Proof.

If
G
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis, then the fact that S-sets reduce to 0 and the existence of compatibility syzygies to satisfy the definition of Ann-closure are immediate from (the module analogue of) Proposition
4.1.5
, since it is clear that every element of
S_{sm}(g,g^{\prime})
is in
\langle G\rangle
, as is every element of the form
mg
(whether
m
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
or not).

Conversely, suppose
G
is S-closed and Ann-closed. We split this direction up into the following two lemmata, corresponding to the use of Ann-closure and S-closure, respectively.

Lemma 5.20
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
, suppose that
G\subseteq A^{d}
has the property that for all
g\in G
,
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
is a non-zerodivisor. If
G
is Ann-closed, then for every standard monomial
m
and every
g\in G
,
mg
has a standard expression relative to
G
with 0 remainder.

Lemma 5.21
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
, suppose that
G\subseteq A^{d}
has the property that for all
g\in G
,
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
is a non-zerodivisor. If
G
is S-closed and for every standard monomial
m\in A
and every
g\in G
,
mg
has a standard expression with 0 remainder relative to
G
, then so does every
f\in\langle G\rangle
.

Before proving these lemmata, let us see how the theorem follows. Let
f=\sum h_{i}g_{i}
be a standard expression for
f
relative to
G
. Since
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}g_{i})\preceq\text{LM}_{sm}(f)
for all
i
, we must have some
i
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}g_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(f)
. Since
h_{i}
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
, by Proposition
4.1.1
, we have

\text{LM}_{sm}(f)=\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}g_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i})\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{i})).

Furthermore, since
h_{i}
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
, we have

\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}))\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{i}))=\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f)).

Thus
\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{i}))\mid\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f))
, and we are done by the module analogue of Proposition
4.1.4
. Thus, all that remains to prove the theorem is to prove the two lemmata, which we do next.
∎

Remark 5.22
.

We note that although we have split the proof up into “first use Ann-closure, then use S-closure”, even the S-closure proof (Lemma
5.3
) differs from the classical case somewhat significantly, in that it must handle lower-order terms that are identically zero in the setting of ordinary Gröbner bases in a polynomial ring.

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

We induct over
\text{LM}_{sm}(mg)
, according to the given pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq
. Suppose inductively that
m^{\prime}g^{\prime}
has a standard expression with 0 remainder for all standard monomials
m^{\prime}
and all
g^{\prime}\in G
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}(m^{\prime}g^{\prime})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{0}g_{0})
. We will show that
m_{0}g_{0}
has a standard expression relative to
G
with 0 remainder.

We will proceed with our inner induction as follows. Let
S
be a set of compatibility syzygies as in the definition of Ann-closure. Given a pair
(m,g)
where
m
is a standard monomial such that
\pi_{lt}(m)\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))=0
, we define its
distance from compatibility relative to
S
as

d_{S}(m,g):=\min\left\{\frac{\,m\,}{\,m^{\prime}\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}:S\text{ has a compatibility syzygy for }(m^{\prime},g)\right\}.

(We will only apply this to pairs
(m,g)
such that the set on the RHS is non-empty.) Note that
d_{S}(m,g)=1
implies that there is a compatibility syzygy for
(m,g)
in
S
, and hence by definition
mg
is has a standard expression relative to
G
with zero remainder.

In order to show that
m_{0}g_{0}
has a standard expression in terms of
G
with 0 remainder, we proceed by induction on the distance from compatibility relative to
S
, starting with
d_{S}(m_{0},g_{0})
. Since
\pi_{lt}(m_{0})\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{0}))=0
, there is some
\pi_{lt}(s)\in\text{LAM}(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{0})))
such that
\pi_{lt}(s)\mid\pi_{lt}(m_{0})
, and thus
d_{S}(m_{0},g_{0})
exists. Let
t=\pi_{lt}^{-1}(d_{S}(m_{0},g_{0}))
, and let
m_{1}=\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,m_{0}\,}{\,t\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)
.
Then
S
has a compatibility syzygy for
(m_{1},g_{0})
, say

m_{1}g_{0}=\sum_{g\in G}\ell_{g}g.

Multiplying by
t
, we get

tm_{1}g_{0}=t\left(\sum_{g\in G}\ell_{g}g\right).

Now, as we only have
m_{0}=\text{LM}_{sm}(tm_{1})
, it may be the case that
tm_{1}
has additional lower-order terms than
m_{0}
. However, any such term
m_{2}\in tm_{1}
with
m_{2}\neq m_{0}
then has
m_{2}\prec m_{0}
. Thus, since we have assumed
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{0})
is a non-zerodivisor,
we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{2}g_{0})=\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{2}\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{0}))
by Prop.
4.1.1
, and similarly
\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{0}g_{0})=\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{0}))
. Thus by
(ATO-2)
we get

\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{2}g_{0})=\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{2}\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{0}))\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{0}))=\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{0}g_{0}).

Therefore, by our (outer) inductive assumption, all the lower-order terms
m_{2}\in tm_{1}
are such that
m_{2}g_{0}
has a standard expression with 0 remainder. And since
\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{2}g_{0})\preceq\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{0}g_{0})
, those standard expressions thus have all of their terms
\preceq\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{0}g_{0})
, so they may legitimately be part of a standard expression for
m_{0}g_{0}
as well. Thus,
tm_{1}g_{0}
has a standard expression with 0 remainder if and only if
m_{0}g_{0}
does.

If every standard term occurring in
t\ell_{g}
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
for all
g\in G
, then the expression
\sum_{j}(t\ell_{j})g_{j}
is already standard, and we are done. (While not needed for the proof, it may be helpful for intuition to see that this is automatically the case if
t=1
, which happens iff
S
already contains a compatibility syzygy for
(m_{0},g_{0})
.)

Otherwise, there is some
g\in G
, standard monomial
m_{2}\in\ell_{g}
and some standard monomial
m_{3}\in tm_{2}
such that
m_{3}
is incompatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
. We break the final part of the argument into cases.

Case 1 [
m_{3}=\text{LM}_{sm}(tm_{2})
]: In this case, we have that
\pi_{lt}(t)\pi_{lt}(m_{2})
is either 0 or
\pi_{lt}(m_{3})
, and in either case,
\pi_{lt}(t)\pi_{lt}(m_{2})
is in
\Ann(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)))
. Thus by Ann-closure, there is some
s_{2}
in the minimal generating set of
(\Ann(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))):\pi_{lt}(m_{2}))
such that
s_{2}\mid_{lt}t
and there is a compatibility syzygy in
S
for
(\text{LT}_{sm}(s_{2}m_{2}),g)
. Using the latter syzygy, since
s_{2}\mid_{lt}t
and
m_{3}=\text{LT}_{sm}(tm_{2})
, we find that

d_{S}(m_{3},g)\preceq\frac{\,m_{3}\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(s_{2}m_{2})\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}=\frac{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(tm_{2})\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(s_{2}m_{2})\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}=\frac{\,t\,}{\,s_{2}\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}.

We will now show that
d_{S}(m_{3},g)
is
strictly
less than
t
. From the preceding equation, this is equivalent to showing that
s_{2}\succ 1
. The latter follows since
m_{2}
is in
\ell_{g}
, so is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
by assumption, and therefore
1
is not in
(\Ann(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))):\pi_{lt}(m_{2}))
, so
s_{2}\neq 1
. Thus we have
d_{S}(m_{3},g)\prec t
, which implies, by our inner inductive assumption, that
m_{3}g
has a standard expression with zero remainder,
and we are done.

Case 2 [
m_{3}\neq\text{LM}_{sm}(tm_{2})
]: Then we have

(5.1)

m_{3}\prec\text{LT}_{sm}(tm_{2})\preceq\text{LT}_{sm}(t\ell_{g}).

Multiplying the first and last parts of this inequality by
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
, and using our assumption that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
is a non-zerodivisor,
(MATO-2)
implies that

(5.2)

\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3}\text{LT}_{sm}(g))\prec\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(t\ell_{g})\text{LT}_{sm}(g)).

Now, since
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
is a non-zerodivisor, by Prop.
4.1.1
the LHS here is equal to
\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3}g)
and the RHS is equal to
\text{LT}_{sm}(t\ell_{g}g)
, so we have

\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3}g)\prec\text{LT}_{sm}(t\ell_{g}g).

Finally, since
\sum_{g}\ell_{g}g
was a standard expression for
m_{1}g_{0}
with
m_{1}\mid_{lt}m_{0}
, we have
\text{LT}_{sm}(\ell_{g}g)\preceq\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{1}g_{0})
. Multiplying both sides of this equation by
t
, and since both
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
and
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{0})
are non-zerodivisors, we finally get
\text{LT}_{sm}(t\ell_{g}g)\preceq\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{0}g_{0})
. Combining with the above equation, this gives us
\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{3}g)\prec\text{LT}_{sm}(m_{0}g_{0})
, and thus by our (outer) inductive assumption,
m_{3}g
has a standard expression with 0 remainder. This completes the proof of Lemma
5.3
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

Write
G=\{g_{1},\dotsc,g_{s}\}
. Let
f\in\langle G\rangle
; say
f=\sum_{i\in[s]}h^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}
, where
h^{\prime}_{i}\in A
. By Lemma
5.3
, if
m
is a standard monomial occurring in
h^{\prime}_{i}
, then
mg_{i}
has a standard expression relative to
G
with 0 remainder.
Thus, term-by-term within each
h^{\prime}_{i}
, we may replace the preceding expression with

(5.3)

f=\sum_{i\in[s]}h_{i}g_{i},

where each
h_{i}
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
. Let
\delta=\max_{i\in[s]}\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}g_{i})
. (Note that while the preceding replacement process did not increase any of the leading terms relative to their initial values in the expression
\sum h^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}
, it is possible that some of those leading terms are in fact strictly larger than
\text{LM}_{sm}(f)
.) If
\delta=\text{LM}_{sm}(f)
, then we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}g_{i})\preceq\text{LM}_{sm}(f)
for all
i
, so the preceding is in fact a standard expression with 0 remainder
and we are done.

Thus, all that remains is to handle the case in which
\delta
is strictly greater than
\text{LM}_{sm}(f)
. We will handle this case by showing that
f
has another expression
\sum_{i\in[s]}h^{\prime\prime}_{i}g_{i}
with
h^{\prime\prime}_{i}
compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
for all
i
, and such that
\max_{i\in[s]}\text{LT}_{sm}(h^{\prime\prime}_{i}g_{i})
is strictly less than
\delta
. By induction on
\delta
, this procedure eventually gets to the base case where
\delta=\text{LM}_{sm}(f)
, completing the proof.

Toward that end, let
Q\subseteq[s]
be the set of all
q\in[s]
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})=\delta
;
Q
is non-empty by the definition of
\delta
. Then we can re-write Equation (
5.3
) as

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\in Q}h_{q}g_{q}+\sum_{i\in[s]\setminus Q}h_{i}g_{i}

(5.4)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{q\in Q}\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q}}_{\mathcal{A}}+\underbrace{\sum_{q\in Q}\left(h_{q}-\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})\right)g_{q}}_{\mathcal{B}}+\underbrace{\sum_{i\in[s]\setminus Q}h_{i}g_{i}}_{\mathcal{C}}.

We handle the three collections of terms
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
in reverse order:

•

By definition of
Q
, the leading standard monomials of all summands
h_{i}g_{i}
in
\mathcal{C}
are strictly smaller than
\delta
.

•

The leading standard monomials of all summands
(h_{q}-\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q}))g_{q}
of
\mathcal{B}
are also strictly smaller than
\delta
: for, since
h_{q}
is compatible with
\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{q})
, by Proposition
4.1.1
we have

\delta=\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})=\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{q}\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{q})),

and then we may apply Observation
5.3
stated below to
f_{1}=h_{q}
and
f_{2}=\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{q})
.

Observation 5.23

(store=claimlmsmlessthankappa)
.

Suppose
f_{1}\in A
is compatible with
f_{2}\in A^{d}
. Let
\text{tail}_{sm}(f_{1})=f_{1}-\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{1})
. Then

\text{LM}_{sm}\left(\text{tail}_{sm}(f_{1})f_{2}\right)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{1}f_{2}).

See Appendix
A
for the proof. (While it seems intuitively obvious and the proof is straightforward, one must use compatibility carefully in order to be able to apply Proposition
4.1.1
and
(MATO-2)
.)

The remainder of the proof is devoted to handling the terms in
\mathcal{A}
.

Claim 5.24

(store=claimrewriteAintermsofS)
.

We can rewrite
\mathcal{A}
as follows.
For any
q_{0}\in Q
, let
g_{0}:=g_{q_{0}}
, then we have:

(5.5)

\mathcal{A}=\sum_{q\in Q}\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})\cdot S_{sm}^{\delta}\left(g_{q},g_{0}\right).

Proof of claim.

Since
\text{LM}_{sm}(f)\prec\delta
, and all terms in
\mathcal{A}
have leading term
\delta
, and all terms in
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
have leading term
\prec\delta
, the
\delta
terms in
\mathcal{A}
must all cancel, i. e., we deduce that

(5.6)

\sum_{q\in Q}\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})=0.

(N.b.! Even if all
g_{q}
have
\text{LC}_{sm}(g_{q})=1
, the above equation need not be the same as
\sum_{q}\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{q})=0
, as happens in the case of ordinary Gröbner bases, because in a pseudo-ASL, even for two standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}
, we may have
\text{LC}_{sm}(mm^{\prime})\neq 1
.)

Now, let
q_{0}
be any element of
Q
,
g_{0}:=g_{q_{0}}
and
h_{0}:=h_{q_{0}}
for brevity and distinction. We can re-write
\mathcal{A}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\in Q}\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{q\in Q}\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q}\right)-\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})g_{0}}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{0}g_{0})}\cdot 0

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{q\in Q}\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q}\right)-\left(\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})g_{0}}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{0}g_{0})}\right)\cdot\sum_{q\in Q}\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})\qquad

[by (
5.6
)]

(5.7)
