\displaystyle=\sum_{q\in Q}\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})\left(\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q}}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})}-\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})g_{0}}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{0}g_{0})}\right).

We will now show that each summand in (
5.7
) is an (the) S-polynomial of
\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q}
and
\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})g_{0}
.
For since
\text{LM}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q})
is the same for all
q\in Q
(namely,
\delta
), they are all equal to their common LCM in
A_{lt}^{d}
, and that LCM is unique, namely
\pi_{lt}(\delta)
. Straightforward calculation shows that

S_{sm}^{\delta}(\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q},\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q_{0}})g_{q_{0}})=\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q}}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})}-\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})g_{0}}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{0}q_{0})}.

Finally, we show that
S_{sm}^{\delta}(\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q},\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})g_{0})
is in fact equal to
S_{sm}^{\delta}(g_{q},g_{0})
. We have:

S_{sm}^{\delta}(g_{q},g_{0})=\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,\delta\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{q})\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)g_{q}-\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,\delta\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{0})\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)g_{0}

and

S_{sm}^{\delta}(\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q},\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})g_{0})=\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q}}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})}-\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})g_{0}}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{0}g_{0})}.

In both cases, the coefficient of
g_{q}
is the unique (by Obs.
5.2
) standard monomial
m
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(mg_{q})=\pi_{lt}^{-1}(\delta)
, and similarly for
g_{0}
. Thus we must have

\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,\delta\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{q})\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)=\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{q}g_{q})}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,\delta\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{0})\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)=\frac{\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})}{\text{LC}_{sm}(h_{0}g_{0})},

showing that
S_{sm}^{\delta}(g_{q},g_{0})=S_{sm}^{\delta}(\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{q})g_{q},\text{LT}_{sm}(h_{0})g_{0})
. Plugging this into (
5.7
) finishes the proof of the claim.
∎

The remainder of the proof of Lemma
5.3
(and, hence, Theorem
5.3
) is then to show that the S-polynomials arising in Claim
5.3
can be rewritten in terms of S-polynomials of the
g_{q}
relative to their LCMs (rather than
\delta
as above, which is a common multiple but not necessarily a
least
common multiple) plus lower-order terms. (These lower-order terms are another place where this proof extends the usual proof; for ordinary Gröbner bases in a polynomial ring—or even in a monomial pseudo-ASL—the aforementioned lower-order terms that arise in our proof vanish identically and so need not be dealt with.)

At this point we already have that the leading term of each
S_{sm}^{\delta}\left(g_{q},g_{0}\right)
is strictly less than
\delta
. What remains is to show that each of the
S_{sm}^{\delta}\left(g_{q},g_{0}\right)
terms can be written as
\sum h^{\prime\prime}_{i}g_{i}
in which each
h^{\prime\prime}_{i}
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
and in which every summand
h^{\prime\prime}_{i}g_{i}
also has leading monomial strictly less than
\delta
.

By definition, for all
q\in Q
,
\pi_{lt}(\delta)
is in
\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{q}))\rangle\cap\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{0}))\rangle
, thus there exists

l_{q}\in LCM_{sm}^{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{q}),\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{0}))

such that
l_{q}\mid\pi_{lt}(\delta)
. For all
q\in Q
, let
\mu_{q}:=\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,\delta\,}{\,l_{q}\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)\in A
. We will attempt to rewrite each
S_{sm}^{\delta}(g_{q},g_{0})
in terms of
\mu_{q}S_{sm}^{l_{q}}(g_{q},g_{0})
, and see that the difference between the two only involves lower-order terms.

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
g,g^{\prime}\in A^{d}
,
l
a standard monomial in
\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))\rangle\cap\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g^{\prime}))\rangle
, and
\mu\in A
a standard monomial that is compatible with
l
. Then there exist
h,h^{\prime}\in A
such that

\mu S_{sm}^{l}(g,g^{\prime})=S_{sm}^{\pi_{lt}(\mu)l}(g,g^{\prime})+hg+h^{\prime}g^{\prime}

with
\text{LT}_{sm}(hg)
and
\text{LT}_{sm}(h^{\prime}g^{\prime})
both strictly less than
\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\pi_{lt}^{-1}(l))
.

Proof.

By definition, we have

\displaystyle\mu S_{sm}^{l}(g,g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)g-\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g^{\prime})\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)g^{\prime}\right)

At this point, since we will use them frequently throughout this derivation, we introduce the shorter notation
\alpha:=\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)
and
\beta:=\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g^{\prime})\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)
. Continuing with this new notation in hand, we have

\displaystyle\mu S_{sm}^{l}(g,g^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mu\alpha g-\mu\beta g^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\left(\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)+\text{tail}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)\right)g-\left(\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\beta)+\text{tail}_{sm}(\mu\beta)\right)g^{\prime}

(5.9)

\displaystyle=\left(\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)g-\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\beta)g^{\prime}\right)+\left(\text{tail}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)g-\text{tail}_{sm}(\mu\beta)g^{\prime}\right)

We claim that the first summand here is
S_{sm}^{\pi_{lt}(\mu)l}(g,g^{\prime})
, and that we may take
h=\text{tail}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)
and
h^{\prime}=-\text{tail}_{sm}(\mu\beta)
to satisfy the conclusion of the lemma. To prove both of these, we now analyze
\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)
and
\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\beta)
.

We have

\displaystyle\pi_{lt}\left(\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{lt}\left(\text{LT}_{sm}\left(\mu\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\pi_{lt}\left(\mu\right)\pi_{lt}\left(\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)\right)

[by Obs.
5.1
]

\displaystyle=\pi_{lt}(\mu)\cdot\frac{\,l\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}

\displaystyle=\pi_{lt}(\mu)\frac{l}{\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))}\qquad

[by Def.
5.3
]

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi_{lt}(\mu)l}{\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))}

It follows that
\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)g)=\pi_{lt}(\mu)l
. A similar calculation yields that
\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\beta)g^{\prime})=\pi_{lt}(\mu)l
. It follows that

\left(\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)g-\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\beta)g^{\prime}\right)=S_{sm}^{\pi_{lt}(\mu)l}(g,g^{\prime})

and
\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{tail}_{sm}(\mu\alpha)g)
and
\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{tail}_{sm}(\mu\beta)g^{\prime})
are both strictly less than
\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu\pi_{lt}^{-1}(l))
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Returning to our proof of Lemma
5.3
, we apply Lemma
5.3
with
g=g_{q},g^{\prime}=g_{0},l=l_{q},\mu=\mu_{q}
. As we have
\delta=\pi_{lt}(\mu_{q})l_{q}
, the conclusion of Lemma
5.3
gives us

\mu_{q}S_{sm}^{l_{q}}(g_{q},g_{0})=S_{sm}^{\delta}(g_{q},g_{0})+hg_{q}+h^{\prime}g_{0}

where
\text{LT}_{sm}(hg_{q})
and
\text{LT}_{sm}(h^{\prime}g_{0})
are both strictly less than
\pi_{lt}^{-1}(\delta)
. By our assumption, for each standard monomial
m
in
h
, the product
mg_{q}
can be rewritten in terms of a standard expression with 0 remainder; similarly for the monomials
m^{\prime}
occurring in
h^{\prime}
, and the product
m^{\prime}g_{0}
. Adding such expressions together gives us an expression in which all terms multiplied by
g_{i}
are compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
, and all summands have leading terms strictly less than
\delta
.

Since
G
is S-closed, there is a standard expression
S_{sm}^{l_{q}}(g_{q},g_{0})=\sum_{i\in[s]}k_{i}g_{i}
. Let
k^{\prime}_{i}
be the sum of those standard terms
t
in
k_{i}
such that
\mu_{q}t\neq 0
; then
\sum k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}
still has
k^{\prime}_{i}
compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
for all
i
, and we have

(5.11)

\mu_{q}S_{sm}^{l_{q}}(g_{q},g_{0})=\sum_{i\in[s]}\mu_{q}k_{i}g_{i}=\sum_{i\in[s]}\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}.

Claim 5.26
.

For all
i
, if
\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}\neq 0
, then
\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i})\prec\pi_{lt}^{-1}(\delta)
.

Proof of claim.

Assume
\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}\neq 0
. Since every standard term in
k^{\prime}_{i}
occurs in
k_{i}
by construction, we get
\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i})\preceq\text{LT}_{sm}(k_{i}g_{i})\preceq\text{LT}_{sm}(S_{sm}^{l_{q}}(g_{q},g_{0}))\prec l_{q}
(the latter by Observation
5.3
). We now multiply both ends of this inequality by
\mu_{q}
.

Since
k^{\prime}_{i}
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
, we have
\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})\neq 0
hence
\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i})=\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i}))\neq 0
by (
4.2
). Multiplying both sides by
\mu_{q}
and taking leading terms, we get

(5.12)

\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}))=\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i}))).

Next, by Lemma
4.1.3
, we have that if
\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})=0
, then
\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})=0
. But as we have assumed
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
is not a zerodivisor, the latter does not occur, and thus
\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})\neq 0
. Then by (
4.4
), we get

\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})))=\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})).

Combining the preceding with (
5.12
) gives

\displaystyle\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}))

\displaystyle=\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i}))

\displaystyle=\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i}))\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})),

where the last equality follows by (
4.2
) applied to
f=\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})
(which is nonzero by construction of
k^{\prime}_{i}
) and
g=\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
. Continuing, by construction of
k^{\prime}_{i}
we have
\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i})\neq 0
, hence
\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i}))=\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i})
by (
4.2
). Combining with the preceding, we then have

\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}))=\text{LT}_{sm}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i})\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})).

Since
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})
is assumed to not be a zerodivisor, we may apply (
4.2
) with
f=\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i}
and
g=g_{i}
to see that the preceding is equal to
\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i})
. Putting together the previous several paragraphs, we then we have

\displaystyle\pi_{lt}^{-1}(\delta)=\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}l_{q})

\displaystyle\succ\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}\text{LT}_{sm}(k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i}))\qquad

[by
(ATO-2)
]

\displaystyle=\text{LT}_{sm}(\mu_{q}k^{\prime}_{i}g_{i})

This completes the proof of Claim
5.3
.
∎

Finally, putting everything all together, we have

(5.13)

\displaystyle S_{sm}^{\delta}(g_{q},g_{0})

\displaystyle=\mu_{q}S_{sm}^{l_{q}}(g_{q},g_{0})-hg_{q}-h^{\prime}g_{0},

where we have shown how to write the RHS as an
A
-linear combination of elements of
G
such that every summand has all terms strictly less than
\delta
. Claim
5.3
shows that
\mathcal{A}
can be written as an
R
-linear combination of terms of the form
S_{sm}^{\delta}(g_{q},g_{0})
, and by (
5.13
), we can rewrite each such term in terms of
A
-linear combinations of
G
in which each summand is strictly less than
\delta
. By induction, the
f
we started with thus has a standard expression with zero remainder, completing the proof of Lemma
5.3
(thus completing the proof of Theorem
5.3
).
∎

Corollary 5.27

(of Theorem
5.3
)
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq,A_{lt}^{d})
, suppose that
A_{lt}\cong_{sm}A_{gen}^{\preceq_{i}}
for all
i
, where
\preceq_{i}
is the restriction of
\preceq
to
Ae_{i}\subseteq A^{d}
and
A_{gen}^{\preceq_{i}}
denotes the generic algebra of leading terms relative to
(A,\preceq_{i})
. If
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
is a non-zerodivisor for all
g\in G
, then
G
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis if and only if it is S-closed.

Proof.

By assumption, the leading terms of
g\in G
are not zerodivisors in
A
, hence the same is true in
A_{gen}
. Thus the annihilating ideals of all standard monomials in
A_{gen}
are 0, so
G
is vacuously Ann-closed.
∎

By contrasting the preceding corollary with Observation
5.3
, we see again a tradeoff in using different algebras of leading terms, viz.: relative to
A_{gen}
, S-closure suffices to be a Gröbner bases but LCMs need not be unique, while relative to
A_{disc}
LCMs are unique, but one must have both S-closure and Ann-closure to be a Gröbner basis.

5.3.1.
Towards dropping the nonzerodivisor condition

It is plausible to us that the condition in Theorem
5.3
that the leading terms of
G
be non-zerodivisors could be dropped with some additional work. Although we are not as yet able to prove this, here we develop some preparatory lemmata that are headed in this direction, and end this section with a remark sketching some ideas and obstacles towards such a more general result.

Lemma 5.28
.

Given
(A^{d},\preceq
), if
m\in A^{d}
is a standard monomial, then
\Ann_{A}(m)\subseteq A
is a “standard monomial ideal”, in the sense that for all
f\in A
, we have
f\in\Ann_{A}(m)
iff every standard monomial
m^{\prime}
occurring in
f
is also in
\Ann_{A}(m)
.

Proof.

Suppose
f\in\Ann_{A}(m)
. Write
f
as a sum of standard terms:
f=\sum_{i}t_{i}
. Consider
\delta:=\max_{i}\text{LM}_{sm}(t_{i}m)\in A^{d}
. If
\delta=0
then we are done, since then each
t_{i}
annihilates
m
. Otherwise, among all
i
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}(t_{i}m)
, we must have that the sum of the coefficients of
\delta
vanish, and thus there must be at least two
i
, say
i
and
i^{\prime}
, such that
\delta=\text{LM}_{sm}(t_{i}m)=\text{LM}_{sm}(t_{i^{\prime}}m)
. But the latter contradicts Observation
5.2
: let
e_{i}
be the vector supporting
m
(and hence also
\delta
), let
\preceq_{i}
be the term order restricted to
Ae_{i}
, and let
A_{gen}
be the generic algebra of leading terms relative to
(A,\preceq_{i})
. Write
m=m^{\prime}e_{i}
where
m^{\prime}\in A
. Then in
A_{gen}
we have
\pi_{gen}(t_{i})\pi_{gen}(m^{\prime})=\pi_{gen}(t_{i^{\prime}})\pi_{gen}(m^{\prime})
, contradicting Observation
5.2
. Thus it must be the case that for each
i
, we have
t_{i}m=0
, and thus
t_{i}\in\Ann_{A}(m)
.
∎

In particular, we note that in the proof of Lemma
5.3
we only use that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
times any standard monomial is nonzero; Lemma
5.3.1
shows that such a condition is equivalent to
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
being a non-zerodivisor.

Lemma 5.29
.

Given
(A,\preceq)
(a not-necessarily-monomial psuedo-ASL and term order), suppose that
I\subseteq A
is a standard monomial ideal in the sense of Lemma
5.3.1
. Then there is a finite set
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{s}
of standard monomials in
I
such that for all standard monomials
m
, we have
m\in I
iff
\exists i\in\{1,\dotsc,s\}
such that
m_{i}\mid m
(n. b.: divides in
A
).

In particular, this shows that “standard monomial ideals” in the sense of Lemma
5.3.1
are also generated by standard monomials. The converse (if an ideal in a pseudo-ASL is generated by standard monomials, then it is a “standard monomial ideal” in the sense of Lemma
5.3.1
) is false in general, see Example
6.13
.

Proof.

We will build an auxiliary monomial pseudo-ASL
A^{\prime}
as follows (it will be similar to, but not quite the same as, an algebra of leading terms). Suppose the pseudo-ASL structure on
A
is given by
(H,\Sigma)
. For each
\sigma\in\Sigma
, suppose
f\in R[H]
is the straightening law
\sigma-\sum_{i}t_{i}
, where the
t_{i}
are standard terms. If the RHS of the straightening law for
\sigma
consists of a single standard monomial—that is,
f=\sigma-t
—then in
A^{\prime}
the same straightening law applies; if the straightening law for
\sigma
consists of 0 or at least 2 standard monomials, then in
A^{\prime}
we mod out by
\sigma
. (This has the effect of allowing “incompatible” products of standard monomials so long as their product in
A
is still a standard monomial, but making all other incompatible products equal to
0
.)

By construction, it is clear that
A^{\prime}
is a monomial pseudo-ASL that is also governed by the same
(H,\Sigma)
. Let
\pi\colon A\to A^{\prime}
be the unique
R
-linear map that is the identity on standard monomials.

We claim that
\pi(I)
is an ideal in
A^{\prime}
. Since
A^{\prime}
is a monomial pseudo-ASL, for this it suffices to show that for any standard monomial
m\in\pi(I)
and any standard monomial
m^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}
, we have
mm^{\prime}\in\pi(I)
. If
mm^{\prime}=0
, then certainly
mm^{\prime}
is in
\pi(I)
. Otherwise, let
n=\pi^{-1}(m),n^{\prime}=\pi^{-1}(m^{\prime})
; we have
n\in I
. Since
mm^{\prime}\neq 0
, by construction of the straightening rules in
A^{\prime}
this is only possible if either
mm^{\prime}
was already standard in
\W^{H}
, in which case the product
nn^{\prime}
in
A
also involves no straightening, or if
mm^{\prime}\in\W^{H}
is non-standard, and the straightening rule in
A
(sic!) has
nn^{\prime}=t
for some standard term
t
. In either case, we have
nn^{\prime}
is a standard monomial in
A
and
\pi(nn^{\prime})=mm^{\prime}
. Since
n
is in
I
,
nn^{\prime}
is also in
I
, and thus
mm^{\prime}
is in
\pi(I)
.

Since an element of
A
is in
I
iff all its standard monomials are, the same is true of
\pi(I)
. Since
A^{\prime}
is a monomial pseudo-ASL by construction, we thus have that
\pi(I)
is a standard monomial ideal in a monomial pseudo-ASL, that is, in our usual sense.

Finally, by Lemma
4.1.2
, let
m^{\prime}_{1},\dotsc,m^{\prime}_{s}
be the unique minimum generating set of
\pi(I)
. Let
m_{i}=\pi^{-1}(m^{\prime}_{i})
for
i=1,\dotsc,s
. We show that
\{m_{1},\dotsc,m_{s}\}
satisfies the conclusion of the lemma. Suppose
m\in I
is a standard monomial; then
\pi(m)
is divisible by some
m^{\prime}_{i}
by construction; let
t
be a standard term such that
m^{\prime}_{i}\pi(t)=\pi(m)
, which exists by Lemma
4.1.2
. Then since
m^{\prime}_{i}\pi(t)\neq 0
, by construction of the straightening rules for
A^{\prime}
we must have
m_{i}t=m
, and thus
m_{i}\mid m
in
A
, as claimed.
∎

Remark 5.30

(Towards dropping the non-zerodivisor condition from Thm.
5.3
)
.

Lem.
5.3.1
suggests that one could fruitfully modify the first condition in the definition of Ann-closure to use the set of standard monomials from Lemma
5.3.1
in place of merely the minimal generating set of
\Ann(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)))\subseteq A_{lt}
. One would then want to extend a similar idea to the colon ideals, using
(\Ann_{A}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)):m)
in place of
(\Ann_{A_{lt}}(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))):\pi_{lt}(m))
; an obstacle here is that we have not been able to prove that the former colon ideal is standard monomial in the sense of Lemma
5.3.1
, and thus we do not know what should play the role of its “minimum generating set of standard monomials.” Even with all this in place, the induction in the proof of Lemma
5.3
would also need to change (an instance of the the key issue is that, in the notation of that proof,
\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{2}g_{0})
could be greater than
\text{LM}_{sm}(m_{0}g_{0})
, if for example
m_{2}
annihilates fewer of the largest terms of
g_{0}
than
m_{0}
does; a similar issue arises near the end of the proof as well). We note that Green’s approach
[
Gre99
]
gets around this issue by only considering “uniform” elements in Gröbner bases, that is, elements of the form
\sum t_{i}
where the
t_{i}
are standard terms such that for all standard monomials
m
,
mt_{i}=0\Leftrightarrow mt_{j}=0
. But intuitively the idea is that if
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
is a zerodivisor, then to get a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis “all one should need” is to multiply
g
by a suitable set of monomials that annihilate
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
, and ensure that each such product has a standard expression relative to
G
(reduce the product to its remainder, and if the remainder is nonzero, add the remainder to
G
).

5.4.
Syzygies of standard monomials

Heading towards the pseudo-ASL analogue of Schreyer’s Syzygy Theorem (Theorem
5.5
below), in this section we analyze the syzygies of standard monomial modules
\langle m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}\rangle\in(A^{\prime})^{e}
over a monomial pseudo-ASL
A^{\prime}
. A syzygy
\sum_{i=1}^{d}h_{i}e_{i}
is called
homogeneous
(of grade
\delta
) if there is a standard monomial
\delta
or
\delta=0
such that for all
i=1,\dotsc,d
and all standard monomials
m
appearing in
h_{i}
,
mm_{i}
is a scalar multiple of
\delta
. (This definition aligns with the grading on
A
by the standard monomial monoid, see Remark
4.1.2
.) We denote the
R
-submodule of syzygies that are homogeneous of grade
\delta
by a subscript, e.g.
\text{Syz}(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d})_{\delta}
or
\ker(\varphi)_{\delta}
.

Definition 5.31

(Divided Koszul syzygies)
.

For all
i,j\in\{1,\dotsc,d\}
, and for each
m\in LCM(m_{i},m_{j})
, let
t_{i},t_{j}
be standard terms such that
m_{i}t_{i}=m=m_{j}t_{j}
. Define

\sigma^{m}_{ij}:=t_{i}e_{i}-t_{j}e_{j}\in(A^{\prime})^{d}.

By Observation
5.2
, in the presence of a pseudo-ASL term order, the choices for
t_{i},t_{j}
are unique, but for the moment we continue our development without that assumption.
In general, although there may be multiple choices for the
t_{i},t_{j}
, any two such choices differ by a binomial annihilator of
m_{i}
(resp.,
m_{j}
), and we may choose any
t_{i},t_{j}
that satisfy the definition without affecting the subsequent development. This is proved more carefully within the proof of the following lemma.

Lemma 5.32

(Syzygies of monomial submodules)
.

Let
A^{\prime}
be a monomial pseudo-ASL, and let
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d}
be standard monomials in
(A^{\prime})^{e}
. Then
{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$\text{Syz}(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d})$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\bigoplus_{i}\Ann(m_{i})e_{i}$}\right.}
is generated by the divided Koszul syzygies

\displaystyle\left\{\sigma^{m}_{ij}:i,j\in[d],m\in LCM(m_{i},m_{j})\right\}.

Furthermore, if
(A^{\prime})^{e}
admits a pseudo-ASL term order, then

\left(\bigcup_{i}\text{LAM}(m_{i})\right)\cup\left\{\sigma^{m}_{ij}:i,j\in[d],m\in LCM(m_{i},m_{j})\right\}

is a universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
\text{Syz}(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d})
.

The proof here follows the same general outline as
[
Eis95
, Section 15.1]
, but we must additionally deal with the fact that in our setting LCMs need not be unique.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\colon(A^{\prime})^{d}\to(A^{\prime})^{e}
be the
A^{\prime}
-module homomorphism that sends the standard basis element
e_{i}
to
m_{i}
for all
i=1,\dotsc,d
, so that
\text{Syz}(m_{1},\dotsc,m_{d})=\ker(\varphi)
.

We claim that
\ker(\varphi)=\bigoplus_{n}\ker(\varphi)_{n}
, where the sum is over all standard monomials
n
or
n=0
such that
\ker(\varphi)_{n}\neq 0
. Indeed, suppose
\sum s_{i}e_{i}\in\ker(\varphi)
. For each
n
that occurs as a standard monomial in any
s_{i}m_{i}
, define
s_{i,n}
to be the sum of standard terms
t
occurring in
s_{i}
such that
tm_{i}
is a scalar multiple of
n
. In particular, we have
s_{i}=\sum_{n}s_{i,n}
, and
s_{i,n}m_{i}
is a scalar multiple of
n
for all
i,n
. Since
\sum s_{i}e_{i}
was a syzygy among the
m_{i}
, we have
\sum s_{i}m_{i}=0
, and hence each of these scalar multiples of
n
must in fact be zero, that is, we must have
\sum_{i}s_{i,n}m_{i}=0
for all
n
. In particular, this means that
\sum_{i}s_{i,n}e_{i}\in\ker(\varphi)
as well. We have thus written our syzygy
\sum s_{i}e_{i}
as a sum of elements of
\ker(\varphi)_{n}
for various
n
, and it is clear by construction that this expression is unique. This completes the proof of the claim that
\ker(\varphi)=\bigoplus_{n}\ker(\varphi)_{n}
.

(Although it is not needed in the proof, it is worth realizing that

\Ann(m_{i})e_{i}\cap\ker(\varphi)_{n}=\Ann(m_{i})_{n}e_{i}.
)

Thus, to show the first part of the lemma, it suffices to show that each
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\ker(\varphi)_{n}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\bigoplus_{i}\Ann(m_{i})e_{i}$}\right.}
is generated by the
\sigma_{i,j}^{m}
. We proceed by induction on the number of standard terms in our syzygy
\sum s_{i}e_{i}\in\ker(\varphi)_{n}
. Since we need only show the result modulo
\bigoplus\Ann(m_{i})e_{i}
, we may assume without loss of generality that none of the
s_{i}
include any tuple of terms
t_{1},\dotsc,t_{k}

(k\geq 1)
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}t_{i}\in\Ann(m_{i})
. Thus there must be at least two distinct indices
i,j
with
s_{i},s_{j}
both nonzero. Let
t_{i}
be a nonzero term of
s_{i}
and
t_{j}
a nonzero term of
s_{j}
. As our syzygy is in
\ker(\varphi)_{n}
, by definition there are nonzero constants
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}\in R
such that
t_{i}m_{i}=\alpha_{i}n
and
t_{j}m_{j}=\alpha_{j}n
.

What we would like is to find an
m\in LCM(m_{i},m_{j})
and standard terms
r_{i},r_{j}
such that
r_{i}m_{i}=r_{j}m_{j}=m
and
r_{i}\mid t_{i}
and
r_{j}\mid t_{j}
, as then we could subtract off a multiple of
\sigma_{ij}^{m}
to reduce the number of terms of our syzygy. However, because of the potential non-uniqueness of division of standard monomials, this may not happen. What we will show is that we can arrange for this to happen by adding certain elements from
\Ann(m_{i})_{n}e_{i}\oplus\Ann(m_{j})_{n}e_{j}
.

Towards that end, as
n
is a common multiple of
m_{i},m_{j}
, there is some
m\in LCM(m_{i},m_{j})
such that
m\mid n
(we may use any such
m
). Let
r_{i},r_{j}
be standard terms such that
\sigma_{ij}^{m}=r_{i}e_{i}-r_{j}e_{j}
(in particular,
r_{i}m_{i}=r_{j}m_{j}=m
). Let
q
be a standard term such that
qm=n
. Then
qr_{i}m_{i}=qr_{j}m_{j}=n
. Thus
\alpha_{i}qr_{i}m_{i}=t_{i}m_{i}=\alpha_{i}n
, so
(\alpha_{i}qr_{i}-t_{i})e_{i}
is an element of
\Ann(m_{i})_{n}e_{i}
. Similarly
(\alpha_{j}qr_{j}-t_{j})e_{j}\in\Ann(m_{j})_{n}e_{j}
. By adding these two elements to our syzygy, we maintain the total number of terms exactly, but we have replaced the term
t_{i}e_{i}
by
\alpha_{i}qr_{i}e_{i}
, and replaced the term
t_{j}e_{j}
by
\alpha_{j}qr_{j}e_{j}
. If we now subtract
\alpha_{i}q\sigma_{ij}^{m}
from our syzygy, then the two terms
\alpha_{i}qr_{i}e_{i}+\alpha_{j}qr_{j}e_{j}
get replaced by the one term
(\alpha_{j}+\alpha_{i})qr_{j}e_{j}
. Thus we have reduced the number of terms by 1, by adding a multiple of one of the divided Koszul syzygies and some multiples of annihilating syzygies. By induction on the number of terms, this completes the proof of generation.

To see the “furthermore,” suppose that
(A^{\prime})^{e}
admits a pseudo-ASL term order. We will show that the least annihilating monomials and divided Koszul syzygies together form a universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis. We will show that, relative to any term order, the leading term of any syzygy must be divisible by the leading term of either a least annihilating monomial or a divided Koszul syzygy; the result will then follow from the module analogue of Proposition
4.1.4
(whose proof is the same,
mutatis mutandis
).

As above, suppose that
\sum s_{i}e_{i}\in\ker(\varphi)
, and suppose the leading monomial of
\sum s_{i}e_{i}
is
t_{i}e_{i}
. Let
n=\text{LM}_{sm}(t_{i}m_{i})
; then we have that
t_{i}e_{i}
has degree
n
, since
A^{\prime}
is a monomial pseudo-ASL, that is, the projection of
\sum s_{i}e_{i}
onto
\ker(\varphi)_{n}
includes
t_{i}e_{i}
as one of its terms. If
t_{i}\in\Ann(m_{i})
, then by Lemma
5.2
,
t_{i}
is divisible by some least annihilating
s\in\text{LAM}(m_{i})
, hence
t_{i}e_{i}
is divisible by
\alpha_{i}^{s}=se_{i}
. If
t_{i}\notin\Ann(m_{i})
, then, by Observation
5.2
, we must be in the case above where there is another term
t_{j}e_{j}
with
\text{LM}_{sm}(t_{j}m_{j})=n
and
j\neq i
. Let
m,r_{i},r_{j},\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},q
be as above. Above we showed that

(\alpha_{i}qr_{i}-t_{i})e_{i}\in\Ann(m_{i}),\text{ and }(\alpha_{j}qr_{j}-t_{j})e_{j}\in\Ann(m_{j}).

But again by Observation
5.2
, we must have that the latter two are identically zero (they cannot be proper binomial annihilators, and if we have
\alpha_{j}qr_{i}-t_{i}
being a scalar multiple of
t_{i}
, then we would have
t_{i}\in\Ann(m_{i})
, contrary to our assumption), and hence
\alpha_{i}qr_{i}=t_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}qr_{j}=t_{j}
. We now claim that
\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma_{ij}^{m})=r_{i}e_{i}
. For if
r_{i}e_{i}\prec r_{j}e_{j}
, then multiplying by
q
, we know that
qr_{i}e_{i}
is a scalar multiple of
t_{i}
, so it is not zero, and similarly neither is
qr_{j}e_{j}
. Thus we have
qr_{i}e_{i}\prec qr_{j}e_{j}
. But since the latter two differ from
t_{i}e_{i}
, resp.
t_{j}e_{j}
, by scalar multiples, we would have
t_{i}e_{i}\prec t_{j}e_{j}
, contradicting our asusmption that
t_{i}e_{i}
was the leading term of our syzygy. Thus
\text{LT}_{sm}(\sum s_{i}e_{i})=t_{i}e_{i}
is divisible by
\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma_{ij}^{m})=r_{i}e_{i}
, and we are done.
∎

5.5.
Syzygies over general pseudo-ASLs

In the following definition, we will need the concept of “leading vector”: given
(A^{e},\preceq)
and
f\in A^{e}
, we define the
leading vector
of
f
, denoted
\text{LV}_{sm}(f)
, to be the basis element
e_{i}
of
A^{e}
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}(f)
is an
A
-multiple of
e_{i}
.

Definition 5.33
.

Given
(A^{e},\prec_{0})
, a free
A
-module
A^{d}
with basis
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{d}
, and an
A
-module homomorphism
\varphi\colon A^{d}\to A^{e}
, let
f_{i}=\varphi(e_{i})
for
i=1,\dotsc,d
. We say that a pseudo-ASL term order
\prec
on
A^{d}
is
good
for
(\prec_{0},\varphi)
if, for all
i,j
(not necessarily distinct), and for all standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A
,

(1)

if
mf_{i}\neq 0
and
m^{\prime}f_{j}\neq 0
, then

\text{LM}_{sm}(mf_{i})\prec_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(m^{\prime}f_{j})\Rightarrow me_{i}\prec m^{\prime}e_{j}.

(2)

if
i=j
,
mf_{i}\neq 0
,
m^{\prime}f_{i}\neq 0
, and
\text{LM}_{sm}(mf_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(m^{\prime}f_{i})
, then

m\text{LV}_{sm}(f_{i})\prec_{0}m^{\prime}\text{LV}_{sm}(f_{i})\Leftrightarrow me_{i}\prec m^{\prime}e_{i}.

If
A
is furthermore a domain, we can show that a good term order exists, analogous to the usual case of polynomial rings:

Proposition 5.34

(store=obsgooddomain)
.

Given
(A^{e},\prec_{0})
, a free
A
-module
A^{d}
with free basis
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{d}
, and an
A
-module homomorphism
\varphi\colon A^{d}\to A^{e}
, if
\varphi(e_{i})
is a non-zerodivisor for all
i=1,\dotsc,d
, then there exists a pseudo-ASL term order on
A^{d}
that is good for
(\prec_{0},\varphi)
.

We do not yet know whether a good term order exists for all
A
, though we have checked at least one non-domain case and seen that it indeed still exists.

Proof.

Let
f_{i}=\varphi(e_{i})
for
i=1,\dotsc,d
. Since each
\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i})
is not a zerodivisor,
the definition of “good” almost completely specifies the order, except in the case of ties, that is, when
\text{LM}_{sm}(mf_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(m^{\prime}f_{j})
but
i\neq j
.
In such cases, we may choose to break ties by defining
me_{i}\prec m^{\prime}e_{j}
if
\text{LM}_{sm}(mf_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(mf_{j})
and
i<j
. This order is good for
(\prec_{0},\varphi)
by construction. We will show that it is indeed a pseudo-ASL term order.

First, let
m
be any standard monomial. Then we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})\prec_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(mf_{i})
, as
m\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
nonzero since
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
is not a zerodivisor, so we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(mf_{i})=m\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
. Thus, by Def.
5.5
(
1
), we have
e_{i}\prec me_{i}
, showing positivity
(MATO-1)
.

Next, we will show that
(MATO-2)
holds. Let
f,g,h\in A
be standard monomials and suppose that
fe_{i}\prec ge_{j}
. If
fh\neq 0
and
gh\neq 0
, then we need to show that
\text{LM}_{sm}(fhe_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(ghe_{j})
. We split into three cases based on the reason that
fe_{i}\prec ge_{j}
.

[Case 1]: Suppose the reason that
fe_{i}\prec ge_{j}
is because of Def.
5.5
(
1
). That is, we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(ff_{i})\prec_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(gf_{j})
. Then, since
fh\neq 0
by assumption and
\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i})
is not a zerodivisor, we have
fh\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i})\neq 0
, and thus by (
4.4
), we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(fhf_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(h\text{LM}_{sm}(ff_{i}))
. Similarly we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(ghf_{j})=\text{LM}_{sm}(h\text{LM}_{sm}(gf_{j}))
. Since we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(ff_{i})\prec_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(gf_{j})
, by the definition of pseudo-ASL term order for
\prec_{0}
, we have

\text{LM}_{sm}(fhf_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(h\text{LM}_{sm}(ff_{i}))\prec_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(h\text{LM}_{sm}(gf_{j}))=\text{LM}_{sm}(ghf_{j}),

and therefore by Def.
5.5
(
1
) we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(fhe_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(ghe_{j})
, as desired.

[Case 2]: Suppose
fe_{i}\prec ge_{j}
because of Def.
5.5
(
2
). We will show that in fact this cannot occur. In this case we have
i=j
and
\text{LM}_{sm}(mf_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(m^{\prime}f_{i})
. Since
\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i})
is a non-zerodivisor, we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(mf_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(m\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}))
, and similarly
\text{LM}_{sm}(m^{\prime}f_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(m^{\prime}\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}))
. As
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
lives in a copy of
A
inside
A^{e}
, we may work entirely in that one copy of
A
. Let
\preceq^{\prime}
denote the restriction of
\preceq
to that copy of
A
, and let
A_{gen}
be the generic algebra of leading terms relative to
\preceq^{\prime}
. Then in
A_{gen}
, we get the binomial annihilator

(\pi_{gen}(m)-\pi_{gen}(m^{\prime}))\pi_{gen}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}))=0,

contradicting Obesrvation
5.2
. Thus this case cannot occur.

(Although not needed for the proof, it is instructive to think about other assumptions under which this case could possibly occur: it can only occur when we have, e.g.,
m^{\prime}\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})=0
even though
m^{\prime}f_{i}\neq 0
.)

[Case 3]: Suppose neither case 1 nor 2 holds; then the reason
fe_{i}\prec ge_{j}
must be that
\text{LM}_{sm}(ff_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(gf_{j})
and
i<j
. Then by the same argument as before we get
\text{LM}_{sm}(fhf_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(ghf_{j})
, but we still have
i<j
, and thus
\text{LM}_{sm}(fhe_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(ghe_{j})
by construction, as desired.

Finally, we show
(MATO-3)
. Let
f,g,h,k
be standard monomials and suppose that
fe_{i}\prec ge_{i}
and
he_{i}\preceq ke_{i}
, and
fh\neq 0
and
gk\neq 0
. We need to show that
\text{LM}_{sm}(fhe_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gke_{i})
. By the argument above we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(fhe_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(ghe_{i})
(NB:
gh
not
gk
). (We note that here we are again crucially using the fact that
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
is not a zerodivisor, since nothing else in our assumptions would tell us that
ghf_{i}\neq 0
.) If
h=k
then we are done. Otherwise we have
he_{i}\prec ke_{i}
(strict inequality). By applying the argument above, but this time multiplying
he_{i}\preceq ke_{i}
by
g
on both sides, we get that
\text{LM}_{sm}(hge_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(kge_{i})
. Putting these two together, we get
\text{LM}_{sm}(fhe_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gke_{i})
.
∎

The following observation about domains may be useful in light of Proposition
5.5
.

Observation 5.35
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
,
A_{lt}
is a domain if and only if
A
is a domain and
A_{lt}\cong A_{gen}
.

Proof.

(
A_{gen}
is a domain if and only if
A
is.) If
A
is a domain, then since a product of two standard monomials in
A
is always nonzero, the same is true in
A_{gen}
. Thus by Prop.
4.1.1
,
A_{gen}
is a domain. Conversely, if
A
is not a domain, then by Prop
4.1.1
there is a pair of standard monomials whose product in
A
is zero. By construction of
A_{gen}
, their product in
A_{gen}
is also zero, so
A_{gen}
is not a domain.

(Other
A_{lt}
’s are not domains.) Note that the way
A_{lt}
is constructed, if
A_{lt}
is not isomorphic to
A_{gen}
, then there is some pair of standard monomials whose product becomes
0
in
A_{lt}
, since, by the definition of algebra of leading terms, a product of standard monomials is either
\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(mm^{\prime}))
or
0
. Thus,
A_{lt}
is not a domain.
∎

To set up the pseudo-ASL analogue of Schreyer’s Theorem, we will use the compatibility syzygies and the S-remainder syzygies, which we define as follows. For all
i,j\in[d]
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
and
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{j})
involve the same basis element of
A^{d}
, and for all
m\in LCM_{sm}^{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}),\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{j}))
, choose a standard expression (Def.
4.1.5
) for

\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,m\,}{\,\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i}))\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)f_{i}-\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,m\,}{\,\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{j}))\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)f_{j},

say as
\sum_{k=1}^{d}h_{k}f_{k}
, and define:

\tau_{ij}^{m}:=\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,m\,}{\,\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i}))\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)e_{i}-\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,m\,}{\,\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{j}))\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)e_{j}-\sum_{k=1}^{d}h_{k}e_{k}.

Recall the divided Koszul syzygies
\sigma_{ij}^{m}
(Def.
5.4
) and annihilating syzygies
\alpha_{i}^{m}
in a monomial pseudo-ASL (Def.
5.2
); these correspond, respectively, to the S-remainder syzygies and compatibility syzygies in a general pseudo-ASL, in a sense made precise in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.36
.

Given
(A^{e},\prec_{0},A_{lt}^{e})
, suppose that
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis of the submodule
M\subseteq A^{e}
. Let
\varphi\colon A^{d}\to A^{e}
be the
A
-module map defined by
\varphi(e_{i})=f_{i}
for
i=1,\dotsc,d
. Let
\prec
be any pseudo-ASL term order on
A^{d}
that is good for
(\prec_{0},\varphi)
(Def.
5.5
). Let

•

\alpha_{i}^{s}
be the annihilating syzygies of
\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}))
,

•

\beta_{i}^{s}
be a compatibility syzygy for
(s,f_{i})
in
A^{d}
,

•

\sigma_{ij}^{m}
be the divided Koszul syzygies of
\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})),\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{j}))
in
A_{lt}^{d}
, and

•

\tau_{ij}^{m}
be the S-remainder syzygies of
f_{i},f_{j}\in A^{d}
.

Then

(1)

\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\beta_{i}^{s}))=\text{LT}_{sm}(\alpha_{i}^{s}),
and

(2)

\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\tau_{ij}^{m}))=\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma_{ij}^{m})
.

Proof.

(1) If
sf_{i}=0
, then
\pi_{lt}(\beta_{i}^{s})=\alpha_{i}^{s}
, so they certainly have the same leading term.

Otherwise, suppose
\beta_{i}^{s}=se_{i}-\sum_{k}h_{k}e_{k}
. Then since
\sum h_{k}f_{k}
is a standard expression for
sf_{i}
, we have that
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{k}f_{k})\preceq_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(sf_{i})
for all
k=1,\dotsc,d
. Since
\prec
is assumed to be good for
(\prec_{0},\varphi)
, this implies that
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{k}e_{k})\preceq\text{LM}_{sm}(se_{i})
for all
k=1,\dotsc,d
. Now, for
k\neq i
, the two cannot be equal since they involve different basis elements, so we necessarily have the strict inequality
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{k}e_{k})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(se_{i})
. For
k=i
, if
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}f_{i})\prec_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(sf_{i})
, then we again have
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}e_{i})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(se_{i})
, as desired.

Now instead suppose that
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}f_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(sf_{i})
. Since
h_{i}
is compatible with
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
, we have
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i})\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})\neq 0
. Next, we will show that we must have
s\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})=0
in
A^{e}
(since
\beta_{i}^{s}
is a compatibility syzygy, by definition we have
\pi_{lt}(s)\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}))=0
in
A_{lt}^{e}
; our claim here is that the same happens in
A^{e}
). For if not, then by Proposition
4.1.1
we have

\text{LM}_{sm}(\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i})\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}))=\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}f_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(sf_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(s\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})).

Now, since
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}f_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(sf_{i})
occurs in some copy of
A\subseteq A^{e}
, say
Av
, we may focus our attention only on those terms in
f_{i}
in the coordinate
v
. Let
\preceq^{\prime}
denote the ordering on
A
that agrees with the restriction of
\preceq_{0}
to
Av\subseteq A^{e}
.

Let
A_{gen}
be the generic algebra of leading terms relative to
\preceq^{\prime}
. Then applying
\pi_{gen}
to the equation above, we get

\pi_{gen}(\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}))\pi_{gen}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}))=\pi_{gen}(s)\pi_{gen}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})).

As
h_{i}
is compatible with
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
in
A^{e}
(relative to
A_{lt}^{e}
)—since
\sum h_{i}f_{i}
is a standard expression—and
s
is not, we must have
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i})\neq s
, and thus the preceding centered equation gives us a nontrivial binomial annihilator of
\pi_{gen}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}))
in
A_{gen}
, contradicting Observation
5.2
. Thus we have that
s\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})=0
in
A^{e}
.

We claim, finally, that
h_{i}\prec^{\prime}s
. For suppose not; then
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i})\succeq^{\prime}s
. But now let
m
be the largest monomial in
f_{i}
such that
sm\neq 0
. By the preceding argument,
m
is not the leading monomial of
f_{i}
, so in particular we have
m\prec^{\prime}\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
. But then, since
\prec^{\prime}
is a pseudo-ASL term order on
A
, and we have
s\preceq^{\prime}\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i})
, and
m\prec^{\prime}\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
, we get

\text{LM}_{sm}(sm)\prec^{\prime}\text{LM}_{sm}(\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i})\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i}))
, since both sides here are nonzero. However, the LHS is (by the definition of
m
) equal to
\text{LM}_{sm}(sf_{i})
and the RHS is equal to
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}f_{i})
. The strict inequality between the two sides contradicts our assumption that
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{i}f_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(sf_{i})
, and thus we have
h_{i}\prec^{\prime}s
.

Since
\prec^{\prime}
was by definition the restriction of
\prec
to standard monomial multiples of
\text{LV}_{sm}(f_{i})
,
h_{i}\prec^{\prime}s
is the same as
h_{i}\text{LV}_{sm}(f_{i})\prec s\text{LV}_{sm}(f_{i})
. Thus, by Definition
5.5
(
2
), this implies that
h_{i}e_{i}\prec se_{i}
.

Thus
\text{LT}_{sm}(\beta_{i}^{s})=\text{LT}_{sm}(se_{i})
, so

\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\beta_{i}^{s}))=\text{LT}_{sm}(\pi_{lt}(s)e_{i})=\text{LT}_{sm}(\alpha_{i}^{s}).

(2) We will first show that one of
t_{i}e_{i}
or
t_{j}e_{j}
is the leading term of
\tau_{ij}^{m}
. Let
t_{i}=\pi_{lt}^{-1}\left(\frac{\,m\,}{\,\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i}))\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}\right)
, and analogously for
t_{j}
. By construction we thus have

\text{LT}_{sm}(t_{i}f_{i})=\text{LT}_{sm}(t_{j}f_{j})=m,

and by Observation
5.3
we have

m\succ_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(S_{sm}^{m}(f_{i},f_{j})).

Next, by the definition of standard expression and the definition of
\tau_{ij}^{m}
, we have

\text{LM}_{sm}(S_{sm}^{m}(f_{i},f_{j}))\succeq_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{k}f_{k})

for all
k=1,\dotsc,d
. Combining these together we have

m=\text{LM}_{sm}(t_{i}f_{i})=\text{LM}_{sm}(t_{j}f_{j})\succ_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{k}f_{k})

for all
k=1,\dotsc,d
.

Now, as
t_{i}
is compatible with
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{i})
by construction, and every standard monomial in
h_{k}
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{k})
by the definition of standard expression, we have that
\text{LM}_{sm}(h_{k})e_{k}\prec t_{i}e_{i}
, since
\prec
is good for
(\prec_{0},\varphi)
(similar to the proof of (1) above). The same holds for
t_{j}e_{j}
. Thus one of
t_{i}e_{i},t_{j}e_{j}
must be the leading term of
\tau_{ij}^{m}
.

As
\sigma_{ij}^{m}=t_{i}e_{i}-t_{j}e_{j}
, one of
t_{i}e_{i},t_{j}e_{j}
is the leading term of
\sigma_{ij}^{m}
, and since the ordering on standard monomials is the same in
A^{d}
and
A_{lt}^{d}
, we have
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\tau_{ij}^{m}))=\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma_{ij}^{m})
, as claimed.
∎

Theorem 5.37

(store=thmaslschreyer, note=Pseudo-ASL analogue of Schreyer’s Syzygy Theorem, cf.
[
Eis95
, Theorem 15.10]
)
.

Given
(A^{e},\prec_{0},A_{lt}^{e})
, suppose that
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{d}
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i})
is a non-zerodivisor for all
i
. Let
\varphi\colon A^{d}\to A^{e}
be the
A
-module homomorphism defined by
\varphi(e_{i})=f_{i}
for
i=1,\dotsc,d
. Let
\prec
be any pseudo-ASL term order on
A^{d}
that is good for
(\prec_{0},\varphi)
and suppose that
A_{lt}
is an algebra of leading terms for
(A^{d},\prec)
as well.

Then the following S-remainder syzygies and compatibility syzygies are a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for the syzygies on the
f_{i}
relative to
(\prec,A_{lt}^{d})
:

\left\{\tau_{ij}^{m}:i,j\in[d],m\in LCM_{sm}^{lt}(\pi_{lt}(f_{i}),\pi_{lt}(f_{j}))\right\}\cup\left\{\beta_{i}^{s}:(s,f_{i})\text{ as in Definition~\ref{def:ann-closed}}\right\}.

Proof.

Let
\sigma=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\ell_{k}e_{k}
be a syzygy, that is,
\sum\ell_{k}f_{k}=0
. We will show that
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma))
is divisible by the leading term of one of the S-remainder syzygies or compatibility syzygies in the statement of the theorem. For
k=1,\dotsc,d
, let
t_{k}e_{k}=\text{LT}_{sm}(\ell_{k}e_{k})
. Since the terms
t_{k}e_{k}
for distinct values of
k
involve different basis elements, they cannot cancel one another, so there is some
i
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma)=t_{i}e_{i}
.

If
\pi_{lt}(t_{i})\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i}))=0
, then
\pi_{lt}(t_{i})e_{i}
is divisible by an annihilating syzygy of the form
se_{i}
for some
s\in\text{LAM}(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i})))
. By Lemma
5.5
, we then have that the corresponding compatibility syzygy has its leading term lt-dividing
t_{i}e_{i}=\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma)
, and we are done.

On the other hand, if

\pi_{lt}(t_{i})\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{i}))=\pi_{lt}(m)\neq 0,

then we first proceed to eliminate other incompatible terms. For any
k
such that

\pi_{lt}(t_{k})\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{k}))=0,

we use the compatibility syzygies as in Lemma
5.3
to replace those terms by other terms consisting of standard monomials
m_{k}
times a basis vector
e_{k}
such that
m_{k}
is compatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{k})
. (We note that, as in Lemma
5.3
, our current proof of this uses the non-zerodivisor assumption. This is the only new place in this proof, beyond its use in previous lemmata, that we use that assumption.) Because the compatibility syzygies involved standard expressions, this does not increase the terms
t_{k}e_{k}
, and since none of those were the leading term
t_{i}e_{i}
, we get another syzygy where
t_{i}e_{i}
is still the leading term, and now every
k
satisfies
\pi_{lt}(t_{k})\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{k}))\neq 0
.

Next, let

I=\left\{j\in[d]:\text{LM}_{sm}(\pi_{lt}(t_{j})\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{j})))=\pi_{lt}(m)\right\}.

By Definition
5.5
(
1
), we have that
m\succeq_{0}\text{LM}_{sm}(t_{j}f_{j})
for all
j
, and hence the terms
\{\text{LT}_{sm}(t_{j}\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{j})):j\in I\}
must cancel one another. Thus we have that

\sum_{j\in I}\pi_{lt}(t_{j})\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{j}))=0,

so
\sigma^{\prime}:=\sum_{j\in I}\pi_{lt}(t_{i})e_{j}
is a syzygy among the
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{j}))
, over
A_{lt}
. By Lemma
5.4
, either a divided Koszul syzygy or an annihilating syzygy has the property that its leading term divides
\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma^{\prime})
. Finally, by Lemma
5.5
, if a divided Koszul syzygy’s leading term divides
\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma^{\prime})
, then the leading term of the corresponding S-remainder syzygy lt-divides
m=\text{LM}_{sm}(\sigma)
, and similarly, if an annihilating syzygy divides
\text{LT}_{sm}(\sigma^{\prime})
, then the corresponding compatibility syzygy’s leading term lt-divides
\text{LM}_{sm}(\sigma)
. And thus we conclude.
∎

6.
Algorithms

In this section we show that pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases can be computed by algorithms that are only slightly more complicated than the algorithms for computing ordinary Gröbner bases in a polynomial ring.

We assume that our pseudo-ASL is specified to the algorithms by giving:

•

R
, a field in which the basic field operations are computable

•

the finite set
H

•

A finite, minimal set of generators of the semigroup ideal
\Sigma\subseteq\W^{H}

•

An algorithm for multiplying standard monomials in
A
.

•

An algorithm for deciding the term order
\prec
, that is, the algorithm, given two standard monomials
m,m^{\prime}\in A
, returns true or false according to whether
m\prec m^{\prime}
or not.

•

A_{lt}
is specified analogously to
A
above, using the same
H,\Sigma
as for
A
, with the further stipulation that we are given generators for the kernel of the map
R[H]\to A_{lt}
, such that the generators all have at most two monomials.
(For
A_{lt}
we in fact need binomial generators of the defining ideal, and not merely a black box for multiplication in
A_{lt}
.)

Monomials, that is, elements of
\W^{H}
(which may be thought of as living in one or more of
\W^{H}
,
R[H]
,
A
, or
A_{lt}
), are specified in the usual way by a list of their exponents. Polynomials, i.e. elements of
R[H]
, are given in their usual sparse representation as a list of monomials together with their coefficients. Elements of
A
are also given as polynomials, the same as though they were in
R[H]
, but with the caveat that the only monomials allowed are the standard monomials. We assume a weak sort of type system, in which the data structure for a polynomial specifies whether it lives in
A
,
A_{lt}
, or
R[H]
(so that, for example, if
f,g\in A
are two polynomials, and we write
fg
, then the data structure implies that that means the product in
A
, rather than the product in
R[H]
or
A_{lt}
; similarly if
f,g\in A_{lt}
or
f,g\in R[H]
, and it is considered a type error to multiply a polynomial in
A
by a polynomial in
A_{lt}
, etc.).

Throughout this section we present our algorithms for
A,A_{lt}
for notational and linguistic simplicity (we never have to mention “the vector supporting the leading term” and so on), but the results and algorithms extend to
A^{d},A_{lt}^{d}
without any difficulty.

6.1.
Computing in pseudo-ASLs

Proposition 6.1
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
. There is an algorithm that halts in finite time such that, given two standard terms
t,t^{\prime}\in A_{lt}
as input, either outputs the unique (by Observation
5.2
) standard term
t/t^{\prime}
, or a special symbol
\bot
if
t^{\prime}
does not divide
t
.

The proof in fact does not use
(A,\prec)
at all, though it does use Observation
5.2
, which depends on the existence of a term order on
A_{lt}
.

Proof.

We represent monomials in this algorithm by their exponent vectors. Let
e,e^{\prime}
be the exponent vectors corresponding to
t,t^{\prime}
, respectively.

Suppose the defining ideal of
A_{lt}
as a quotient of
R[H]
is given by a generating set consisting of
B\cup M
, where
B
consists of binomials and
M
consists of monomials, that is,

A_{lt}={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\langle B\cup M\rangle$}\right.}.

For each binomial
b\in B
, let
e_{b}
be the difference of the exponent vectors of the two monomials in
b
. For
i=1,\dotsc,|H|
, let
e_{i}
denote the
i
-th standard basis vector, with a 1 in the
i
-th position and 0s elsewhere; equivalently, the exponent vector of the variable
x_{i}\in H
. Then the exponent vectors in
\W^{H}
that yield (not necessarily standard) monomials
m
such that
t^{\prime}m
and
t
are supported on the same monomial are precisely the solution to the following system of integer linear (in)equations:

e_{t^{\prime}}+\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{|H|}m_{i}e_{i}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{multiply by an}\\
\text{arbitrary monomial}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\sum_{b\in B}c_{b}e_{b}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{work}\\
\text{modulo }B\end{subarray}}=e_{t}\qquad m_{i}\in\Z_{\geq 0},c_{b}\in\Z\\

(Equivalently, this could be written as
\sum_{b\in B}c_{b}e_{b}\geq e_{t}-e_{t^{\prime}}
with
c_{b}\in\Z
, but the
m_{i}
above are the exponents of the monomial we want to solve for.)
This is an instance of the well-known Integer Linear Programming problem, which is in the complexity class
\mathsf{NP}
, hence is solvable in exponential time. This gives us some monomial
m\in\W^{H}
such that
t^{\prime}m
and
t
are supported on the same standard monomial modulo
\langle B\rangle
. Thus, there is a coefficient
r\in R
such that
t^{\prime}rm=t
modulo
B
. That is,
t^{\prime}rm\in t+\langle B\rangle
.

However, there are two remaining issues: (1) we have not yet accounted for the monomials
M
that become zero in
A_{lt}
, and (2)
m
need not be standard. For the first issue, suppose that our (not necessarily standard) monomial
m\in\W^{H}
in fact becomes zero in
A_{lt}
. This happens if and only if
m
is in
\langle B\cup M\rangle
. But then our containment
t^{\prime}rm\in t+\langle B\rangle
tells us that in fact
t
must be in
\langle B\rangle+\langle B\cup M\rangle=\langle B\cup M\rangle
, contradicting the fact that
t
is a nonzero standard monomial in
A_{lt}
. Thus,
m\neq 0
in
A_{lt}
(that is, even taking into account all the defining relations of
A_{lt}
, not just the binomial ones).

Finally, suppose
m
is not standard. By Observation
5.2
, if
t^{\prime}\mid t
, then there is a unique standard monomial
m^{\prime}
such that
t^{\prime}m^{\prime}
is supported on the same monomial as
t
. Using our assumptions, we can algorithmically rewrite
m
as a linear combination of standard monomials: since we are given (by assumption) both
H
and an algorithm for multiplication in
A_{lt}
, suppose
m=h_{1}^{e_{1}}h_{2}^{e_{2}}\dotsb h_{k}^{e_{k}}
where
H=\{h_{1},\dotsc,h_{k}\}
, then using the assumed algorithm we compute the product
h_{1}^{e_{1}}h_{2}^{e_{2}}\dotsb h_{k}^{e_{k}}
. By assumption, the output of that algorithm is then the standard monomial representation of
m
. Finally, at least one of those standard monomials occurring in the expression for
m
, say
m^{\prime}
, must have the property that
t^{\prime}m^{\prime}
is supported on the same monomial as
t
, so we simply check each one in turn until one is found.
∎

Corollary 6.2
.

There is an algorithm that halts in finite time for computing
standard expressions
. More specifically, given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
, as well as
f,g_{1},\dotsc,g_{k}\in A
, the algorithm outputs
r,h_{1},\dotsc,h_{k}\in A
such that
(r,h_{1},\dotsc,h_{k})
is a standard expression for
f
with respect to
(g_{1},\dotsc,g_{k})
.

Proof.

Initially, set

h_{1}=\dotsb=h_{k}=0.

Repeatedly test
\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f))
for divisibility by
\pi_{lt}(\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{i}))
for each
i
, using Proposition
6.1
. If it is found to be divisible, say
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(f))=m\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i}))
for some standard term
m
, then replace
f
by
f-\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)g_{i}
and continue, and add
\pi_{lt}^{-1}(m)
to
h_{i}
. At the end, we are left with a remainder
r
; for the remainder, we must do the same procedure but with all terms of
r
, not only its leading term.
∎

6.2.
Computing S-closures

Fix
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
. An
S-closure
of a set
G=\{g_{1},\dotsc,g_{k}\}\subseteq A
is a set
G^{\prime}
such that
G\subseteq G^{\prime}\subseteq\langle G\rangle
such that
G^{\prime}
is S-closed (Definition
5.3
). Computing S-closures is of course quite similar to Buchberger’s original algorithm in polynomial rings; the main difference in our setting is that LCMs of monomials need not be unique, and we need to show that we can compute them.

Algorithm 1

Computes S-closure of a finite set with respect to
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})

1:

(A,\preceq,A_{lt})

2:

procedure

S-closure
(
f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}
(elements of
A
))

3:

G\leftarrow\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}\}

4:

repeat

5:

G_{prev}\leftarrow G

6:

for
each
p,q\in G_{prev}

do

7:

R\leftarrow\{\textsc{Remainder}(s,G^{\prime}):s\in S_{sm}(p,q)\}\backslash\{0\}

8:

G\leftarrow G\cup R

9:

end

for

10:

until

G=G_{prev}

11:

return

G

12:

end

procedure

Theorem 6.3

(store=thmcomputablesclosure)
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
, there is an algorithm (Algorithm
1
) that halts in finite time and returns an S-closure of the given set
\{f_{1},\dotsc,f_{k}\}
of elements of
A
.

Lemma 6.4
.

In
A_{lt}
, the function
(M,N)\mapsto LCM_{sm}^{lt}(M,N)
(see Definition
5.3
) is computable.

Proof.

Let
\pi\colon R[H]\to A_{lt}
be the defining map, with kernel
K
. Then, in
R[H]
, compute generators of the ideal intersection

I:=(K+\left\langle M\right\rangle)\cap(K+\left\langle N\right\rangle).

Since
A_{lt}
is a monomial pseudo-ASL, it follows that
K
is a binomial ideal and
\pi(I)
is a standard monomial ideal (Cor.
4.1.2
). Map the generators of
I
into
A_{lt}
using
\pi
, then compute the unique minimum generating set of
\pi(I)
as follows. By Lemma
4.1.2
, this is the subset of the generators of
I
that are
\mid_{lt}
-division-minimal. We can test division-minimality among the given generating set, after mapping it into
A_{lt}
using
\pi
, using the algorithm of Proposition
6.1
or using standard Gröbner bases in
R[H]
as in
[
ES96
]
. By Lemma
4.1.2
the minimum generating set is unique, and this is precisely
LCM_{sm}^{lt}(M,N)
.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

The procedure in Lemma
6.2
and other algorithms in this section are written for arbitrary pseudo-ASLs. Although the above algorithm does work in
R[H]
, rather than intrinsically in
A
, we note that it only works with binomial ideals in
R[H]
. However, if one knew more about the particular pseudo-ASL we are working in, we can expect that the algorithms can be streamlined to take advantage of the structure of the pseudo-ASL. For instance, in Section
7.3
, we give a specifically tailored algorithm to compute the set of LCMs in the ASL structure imposed on the polynomial ring by the basis of standard bideterminants, and that algorithm is entirely using combinatorics of bitableaux, not appealing to calculations in
R[H]
at all.

Lemma 6.6
.

The function
(p,q)\mapsto S_{sm}(p,q)
is computable.

Proof.

To compute
S_{sm}(p,q)
, in looking at the definition we see it suffices to be able to:

(1)

Compute
LCM_{sm}^{lt}(p,q)

(2)

Divide an element of
A
by its leading coefficient

(3)

Multiply and subtract two elements of
A

(4)

Compute
a/b
for two standard monomials
a,b\in A_{lt}

We note that, while technically
\pi_{lt}
and
\pi_{lt}^{-1}
are used in the definition, computationally these come “for free”, as we may assume our elements of
A
and
A_{lt}
are given as lists consisting of standard monomials and their coefficients. Thus, in terms of the underlying data structure, a standard monomial
m
in
A
is represented by the
same
data structure as
\pi_{lt}(m)
, and vice versa.

Now, (1) is computable by Lemma
6.2
.

(2) is computable as we have assumed that arithmetic in
R
is computable.

(3) is computable by assumption. Subtraction is nearly trivial, as elements of
A
are represented by lists of standard monomials and their coefficients.

Finally, (4) is computable by Proposition
6.1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.

Since Algorithm
1
only ever adds elements to
G
, the output of the algorithm contains its input as a subset. Furthermore, if the algorithm halts, then we have
G=G_{prev}
(following notation in Algorithm
1
), whence we must have had
R=\emptyset
. But the latter is only possible if all S-remainders reduced to 0, i.e., if
G_{prev}=G
was S-closed. Therefore, if the algorithm halts, then it outputs an S-closure of its input.

For the algorithm to be computable, it is necessary and sufficient that each individual step be computable, and that the loop halts after finitely many iterations. Most of the steps are computable in standard fashion, with the possible exceptions of
Remainder
and
S_{sm}(p,q)
, which are computable by Corollary
6.1
and Lemma
6.2
, respectively.

Finally, it remains to show that the main loop exits after finitely many iterations. The key idea here is to use Noetherianity in
A_{lt}
. Suppose
r\in R
is a nonzero remainder to get added to
G
in Algorithm
1
. As
r
is a remainder (of something) relative to
G_{prev}
, it follows that for all
g\in G_{prev}
,
g\nmid_{lt}r
. As
I:=\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)):g\in G_{prev}\rangle
is a standard monomial ideal in
A_{lt}
, this means that
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(r))
is not in
I
. Thus, by adding
r
to
G
, we have added new elements to
\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)):g\in G\rangle
. As
A_{lt}
is Noetherian, this can only happen finitely many times. When it fails to happen, we will necessarily have
R=\emptyset
, hence
G=G_{prev}
and the algorithm will terminate.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

The computational complexity of the algorithm (such as runtime, space, completeness for various complexity classes, etc.) will of course depend on that of the algorithm to compute
LCM_{sm}^{lt}(M,N)
and the algorithm to compute remainders, as well as the structure of the pseudo-ASL
A
and the complexity of multiplication—and in particular, the complexity of straightening—in
A
. Beyond those, we expect the complexity of this algorithm to be similar to that of Buchberger’s algorithm for ordinary Gröbner bases, and we expect our algorithm is also amenable to improvements analogous to those made to algorithms for ordinary Gröbner bases over the last 40 years, e. g.,
[
EF17
,
Hib18
,
Fau99
,
Fau02
]
.

In the nicest cases—which are indeed common in applications of (pseudo-)ASLs—already the “ordinary” Buchberger’s algorithm for S-closure as above solves the problem of computing pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases, by Corollary
5.3
:

Corollary 6.8
.

If
A_{lt}
is a domain (equivalently, by Obs.
5.5
,
A
is a domain and
A_{lt}\cong A_{gen}
), then Algorithm
1
correctly computes pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases in
A
.

6.3.
Computing Ann-closures and pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases

The “only” remaining difficulty in computing pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases in general is to be able to compute Ann-closure. As with S-closure, we say that
G^{\prime}
is an Ann-closure of
G
if
G\subseteq G^{\prime}\subseteq\langle G\rangle
and
G^{\prime}
is Ann-closed.

Theorem 6.9
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
and an oracle that decides if a set is Ann-closed, there is an algorithm (Algorithm
2
) that halts in finite time that, given a finite set
G
, computes an Ann-closure of
G
.

For discussion about the oracle, see Remark
6.3
below.

Algorithm 2

Computes Ann-closure of a finite set with respect to
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})

1:

(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
, Oracle
Is-Ann-Closed

2:

procedure

Ann-closure
(
f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}
(elements of
A
))

3:

G\leftarrow\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}\}

4:

Q\leftarrow\{(s,g):s\in LAM(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))),g\in G\}

5:

Done\leftarrow\{\}

6:

repeat

7:

Pick and remove
(s,g)
from
Q
, and add it to
Done

8:

Compute a standard expression
\pi_{lt}^{-1}(s)g=r+\sum_{\gamma\in G}h_{\gamma}\gamma

9:

if

r\neq 0

then

10:

G\leftarrow G\cup\{r\}

11:

Q\leftarrow Q\cup\{(s^{\prime},r):s^{\prime}\in LAM(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(r)))\}

12:

end

if

13:

Q\leftarrow Q\cup\{(\text{LM}_{sm}(tm),\gamma):m\in h_{\gamma},t\in LAM(\pi_{lt}(m\text{LT}_{sm}(\gamma))),\gamma\in G\}\backslash Done

14:

until

Q
is empty or
Is-Ann-Closed
(G)

15:

return

G

16:

end

procedure

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

To compute an Ann-closure of
G\subseteq A
, we follow the outline suggested by Definition
5.3
and Lemma
5.3
. The idea is to build the set
S
of compatibility syzygies from Definition
5.3
as follows. We maintain a set
Q
(we might call it a “queue”, but it doesn’t matter whether it is first-in, first-out or not) of pairs
(s,g)
where
s
is a standard monomial and
g\in G
such that
s
is incompatible with
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)
, and such that we want to have a compatibility syzygy for
(s,g)
in
S
. Initially,
Q
is comes from the least annihilating monomials of each
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))
(
g\in G
) (line
4
of Algorithm
2
). On subsequent rounds,
Q
gets appended by pairs coming from
(\Ann_{A_{lt}}(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))):m)
(line
13
), where
m
is a standard monomial occurring in some compatibility syzygy in
S
.

When attempting to compute a compatibility syzygy for
(s,g)
, it may be that the standard expression for
\pi_{lt}^{-1}(s)g
relative to the current
G
has some non-zero remainder
r
(line
8
; in this case,
G
is not a pASL Gröbner basis, and so is certainly not yet Ann-closed). In that case, we add
r
to
G
(line
10
) so that we then have a compatibility syzygy for
(s,g)
.

Having overviewed the algorithm, we now show that each of its steps is computable.
The only points whose computability requires a little work are computing the updates to
Q
(lines
4
and
13
) and computing a standard expression (line
8
). The latter is the content of Corollary
6.1
. Computing a minimal generating set of
\Ann(\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)))
can be done using standard Gröbner basis methods in
A_{lt}
, since we have its defining ideal by assumption and the latter is binomial; indeed, if
K
is the defining ideal of
A_{lt}
, then we get
\Ann(m)
in
A_{lt}
by taking the colon ideal
(K:m)
in
R[H]
, and taking those generators of
(K:m)
that are not in
K
; these are necessarily monomials that are standard relative to a leading ideal of
K

[
ES96
]
, and by Observation
4.1.2
, we can then convert those to standard monomials (relative to the given pASL structure) generating
\Ann(m)
in
A_{lt}
. Instantiating
m
as
\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g))
gives us an algorithm for line
4
, and instantiating it as
\pi_{lt}(m\text{LT}_{sm}(g))
(in the notation of Algorithm
2
) gives us an algorithm for line
13
.

Termination.
If
G
has stabilized (gotten to the point where line
10
will never be executed again), then we claim that
G
is Ann-closed. For the construction of
Q
on lines
4
and
13
is readily seen to be verifying the conditions of Ann-closure. If
G
has stabilized, then it means that line
10
will never be executed again, which in turn means that on every syzygy computed on line
8
going forward, the result has 0 remainder, and therefore that
G
is Ann-closed. Now, to see that this must eventually happen, note that when
G
is added to on line
10
, it is necessarily the case that the ideal
\langle\pi_{lt}(g):g\in G\rangle\subseteq A_{lt}
is strictly increased; by Noetherianity, the latter can happen at most finitely many times. And thus eventually either the
Q
is empty, or
G
is Ann-closed, in which case the oracle correctly reports that this is the case and the algorithm correctly terminates.
∎

Several remarks are in order about the use of the oracle in Algorithm
2
.

Remark 6.10

(Regarding the oracle assumption)
.

First, we remark on the difficulty that led us to use the oracle in the first place. As written, it is conceivable that the queue
Q
in Algorithm
2
never empties; we conjecture that this cannot happen, but have been unable to either prove it or find a counterexample.

Another natural approach to termination is to store the syzygies computed so far in a set
S
, and only add to
S
and
Q
the new syzygy from line
8
does not reduce to
0
relative to
S
(this requires something like a term order on the ambient module in which
S
lives, but that is not a significant issue, as for this purpose it seems any term order will do). Then one might hope to argue via Noetherianity that, once
G
is not further expanded,
S
can only be expanded finitely many times, thus ensuring termination. The issue here is that we are no longer assured that what we have at the end is Ann-closed, because the algorithm is no longer directly checking the definition of Ann-closure. Just because the new syzygy reduces to
0
relative to
S
does not necessarily mean that the second part of the Ann-closure definition is handled for all the coefficients of the new syzygy.

Second, we remark that there is a slight modification where the oracle can be replaced by an actual algorithm. Namely, if we don’t separate out the Ann-closure and S-closure computations, and instead try to compute a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis all at once by, for example, computing S-closure every time
G
is increased by the Ann-closure algorithm (line
10
of Algorithm
2
), then instead of an oracle to test Ann-closure we need an oracle to test whether a given set is a pASL Gröbner basis. But such an oracle can be replaced by an actual algorithm: by reverting to the parent polynomial ring, one can compute the (affine) Hilbert series of
I
and of
\langle\pi_{lt}(g):g\in G\rangle
;
G
is a Gröbner basis if and only if the two are equal. On the one hand, this shows that such an oracle is not strictly necessary, and hence that computing pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis is computable in finite time; on the other hand, it is very much anathema to the theme of our development here, which is to compute intrinsically in
A
(and perhaps
A_{lt}
) as much as possible. (We note that our other excursions into
R[H]
have been limited to binomial ideals.)

Finally, we mention another possible approach suggested by the first paragraph of this remark. Another issue with the procedure described above is that even if the new syzyg is in
\langle S\rangle
, that does not guarantee that it reduces to
0
modulo
S
. To ensure this reduction to
0
, one would then want to compute a pASL Gröbner bases for the syzygy module itself. This then leads to an iterative procedure computing syzygies, second syzygies, and so on, essentially computing a resolution “all at once.” We believe such an approach, analogous to that in La Scala & Stillman
[
LSS98
]
, can work “intrinsically in
A
.” This is quite a bit more complicated, however, and also would only terminate if the ideal being resolved has a resolution of finite length (which is no longer guaranteed when working with
A
-modules instead of modules over the polynomial ring, as in
[
LSS98
]
).

Algorithm 3

Computes a pseudo-ASL Gröbner of an ideal with respect to any
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})

1:

(A,\preceq,A_{lt})

2:

procedure

pseudo-ASL-Gröbner-Basis
(
f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}
)

3:

G\leftarrow\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}\}

4:

repeat

5:

G_{prev}\leftarrow G

6:

G\leftarrow\textsc{S-closure}(G)

7:

G\leftarrow\textsc{Ann-closure}(G)

8:

until

G=G_{prev}

9:

return

G

10:

end

procedure

Theorem 6.11
.

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
and a finite set
G\subseteq A
, there is an algorithm (Algorithm
3
) that computes a set
G^{\prime}
that is both an Ann-closure and an S-closure of
G
relative to
(\preceq,A_{lt})
. In particular, when
A
is a domain,
G^{\prime}
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
\langle G\rangle
.

Proof.

The correctness of
\textsc{S-closure}(\cdot)
is the content of Theorem
6.2
, and the correctness of
\textsc{Ann-closure}(\cdot)
is the content of Theorem
6.3
. Thus the set
G
that is returned by Algorithm
3
, whenever the algorithm terminates, is obviously both an S-closure and an Ann-closure of
G
, hence is a pASL Gröbner basis if
A
is a domain.

Denote the sequence of intermediate
G
’s that are computed at each iteration of the loop in Algorithm
3
as
G_{1},G_{2},\ldots
. Notice that

\left\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)):g\in G_{1}\right\rangle\subseteq\left\langle\pi_{lt}(\text{LT}_{sm}(g)):g\in G_{2}\right\rangle\subseteq\ldots,

is an increasing sequence of ideals in
A_{lt}
, and this is guaranteed to eventually stablize due to Noetherianity of
A_{lt}
. This in turn means that the loop terminates in finite time.
∎

Example 6.12
.

If
A
is a domain, then
\{f\}
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
\langle f\rangle
relative to
A_{gen}
, since in this case by Corollary
6.2
a set is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis if and only if it is S-closed, and a singleton is always S-closed.

More generally, even if
A
is not a domain, if
m
is a standard monomial, then
\{m\}
is a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
\langle m\rangle
relative to
A_{gen}
, since beyond S-closure (which holds vacuously because
G
is a singleton), we only need to check standard monomial annihilators of
m
in
A_{gen}
. But any such annihilator must also send
m
to zero in
A
, hence
\{m\}
is also Ann-closed.

However, relative to other
A_{lt}
, even singletons need not be pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases:

Example 6.13
.

We use as an example the bideterminant ASL structure on
2\times 2
matrices; this is isomorphic to the polynomial ring
R[x_{11},x_{12},x_{21},x_{22}]
. While the larger explanation for this ASL structure is covered in the next section, here it is small enough we can write it out explicitly. We have
H=\{x_{11},x_{12},x_{21},x_{22},d\}
, with
\Sigma=\langle x_{21}x_{21}\rangle
, and the straightening law
x_{12}x_{21}=x_{11}x_{22}-d
, let

A={\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\langle x_{12}x_{21}-(x_{11}x_{22}-d)\rangle$}\right.}.

Consider the ideal
I\subseteq A
generated by
x_{12}
. Let us compute a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
I
relative to
A_{disc}
. As a singleton, it is already S-closed. The annihilator of
x_{12}
in
A_{disc}
is
\langle x_{21}\rangle
, and when we multiply
x_{12}
by
x_{21}
in
A
, we get
x_{11}x_{22}-d
, and this cannot be further reduced by a standard expression in terms of
x_{12}
, so we must add
x_{11}x_{22}-d
to our Gröbner basis.

Depending on the term order, when we take the S-polynomial of
x_{12}
and
x_{11}x_{22}-d
, we either get
x_{12}x_{11}x_{22}
or
x_{12}d
, and either of these is
\mid_{lt}
-divisible by
x_{12}
, so the S-remainder is zero. Similarly, since both terms of
x_{11}x_{22}-d
are nonzerodivisors in
A_{disc}
, our set is now Ann-closed as well, and thus the pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis of
\langle x_{12}\rangle
relative to
A_{disc}
is
\{x_{12},x_{11}x_{22}-d\}
. In fact, since we were working with
A_{lt}=A_{disc}
and our argument worked regardless of which term order was used, this is the a universal pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for
\langle x_{12}\rangle
, which in this case is reduced (universally, i.e., relative to all choices of term order and algebra of leading terms), hence is the unique reduced universal pASL Gröbner basis.

7.
Application to bideterminants and Grassmannians

In this section we shall demonstrate an application of our theory of ASL Gröbner bases. Specifically we will show that there is a theory of Gröbner bases in the polynomial ring in the basis of what are called standard bideterminants.

Remark 7.1
.

Although we will not elaborate further on this point, it is standard that the same machinery will then apply to the coordinate rings of Grassmannians (see, e.g.,
[
dCEP80
,
Stu08
]
), which are generated by standard products of maximal minors.

7.1.
Background on the bideterminant basis for the polynomial ring

We begin by providing some background on the bideterminant basis for the polynomial ring. Our notation and definitions will — with some important modifications — mostly follow
[
DRS74
,
DKR78
,
DKR17
]
, though other important developments are in
[
dCEP80
,
BC03
,
BC01
]
.

7.1.1.
Basic definitions

Let
X
be an
n\times m
matrix, each of whose entries is a different indeterminate
x_{i,j}
. We refer to
X
as a “generic matrix.” A
bideterminant
(of
X
) is a minor of
X
(of any size) or a product thereof. To keep track of these, Young (bi)tableaux are a convenient and meaningful notation, which we build up to and recall here.

We will indicate the minor in rows
r_{1},\dotsc,r_{k}
and columns
c_{1},\dotsc,c_{k}
by the notation

\left(\,\left.r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}\;\right|\;c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\,\right):=\mathrm{det}\left(\begin{bmatrix}x_{r_{1},c_{1}}&\ldots&x_{r_{1},c_{k}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{r_{k},c_{1}}&\ldots&x_{r_{k},c_{k}}\end{bmatrix}\right).

When a bideterminant is a product of minors, in order to more clearly see the relationship between its minor factors, we use a column-wise notation as follows (note that this is the transpose of what some authors use). A single minor as above will be denoted by the pair of columns of natural numbers:

[R\;|\;C]=\left[\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$r_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\vdots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$r_{k}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\;\right|\;{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$c_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\vdots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$c_{k}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right],}}}}}}

When we wish to distinguish between the pair of columns as a combinatorial object versus the minor (a polynomial), we use square brackets
[R\;|\;C]
for the combinatorial object and parentheses
(R\;|\;C)
for the corresponding bideterminant, viz. with
R,C
as above we have
(R\;|\;C)=\left(\,\left.r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}\;\right|\;c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}\,\right)
.

For bideterminants that are products of minors, we extend the notation as follows. For a product of minors of sizes
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dotsc\geq\lambda_{p}
, we have

(7.1)

[R\;|\;C]=\left[\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$r_{1}^{(1)}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule
