depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$c_{\lambda_{2}}^{(2)}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$c_{\lambda_{1}}^{(1)}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right],}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}

as the combinatorial datum defining the bideterminant:

\left(\,\left.R\;\right|\;C\,\right):=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\left.r_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,r_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\,\right|\,c_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,c_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\right).

We refer to the combinatorial datum
[R\;|\;C]
as a
bitableaux
(whose columns we assume are always put in non-increasing order by height), corresponding to the bideterminant
(R\;|\;C)
.

(One may think of the
i
-th column of
R
as inextricably linked to the
i
-th column of
C
in this notation. In which case one might prefer to think of
R
and
C
as stacked “in front/behind” of one another, coming out of the page.)

Given a bideterminant
f
, with the above conventions the bitableaux giving rise to
f
is almost unique: the only possible choices are to re-order multiple columns of the same length.

Young tableaux formalize what kind of combinatorial object
R
and
C
are (hence the name “bitableaux” for the pair
[R\;|\;C]
). As is standard, we build up to Young tableaux starting from partitions of integers. For any
n\in\Z_{\geq 0}
,
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})
is defined as a
partition
of
n
if

\sum_{i=1}^{p}\lambda_{i}=n,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{p}>0.

We will use
\mu=(4,2,2,1)
as a running example;
\mu
is a partition of
9
.

For a partition of
n
,
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{p})
, we define its
shape
, denoted
sh(\lambda)
(or sometimes just
\lambda
when no confusion can arise) as the set of integer points
(i,-j)
in the plane, with
1\leq i\leq p
and
1\leq j\leq\lambda_{i}
. For example, the shape of
\mu
, i.e.
sh(\mu)
, is

{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\}}$}},}}}}}}}}}

with empty boxes in place of points just for easier visualization. We will say that the
size of the shape/partition

\lambda
, denoted as
|\lambda|
, is
n
. For example, the size of
\mu=(4,2,2,1)
, i.e.
|\mu|=9
.

A
Young tableau
of shape
\lambda
with values in a totally ordered set
E
is an assignment of an element of
E
to each point in the shape
\lambda
. For example,
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are two Young tableaux of shape
\mu
with values in
2\N
and
\N
, respectively:

Y_{1}={\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$4$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$8$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$4$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$4$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\}}$}}.}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}}

It should now be apparent that the combinatorial objects
R,C
arising in our indexing scheme for bideterminants are indeed Young tableaux, and a bitableaux
[R\;|\;C]
is any pair of Young tableaux of the same shape, which we also refer to as the shape of the bitableaux,
sh([R\;|\;C])=sh(R)=sh(C)
. Note that, for a bideterminant
f
, if
f
has multiple minor factors of the same size, there may be multiple distinct bitableaux that give rise to it, but they all have the same shape. Thus, if
f
is a bideterminant, we may speak of its shape
sh(f)
unambiguously as the shape of any bitableaux giving rise to
f
.

For
\lambda=sh([R\;|\;C])
, we have that the length of the largest column of
\lambda
is the degree of the largest minor factor of
(R\;|\;C)
. Also,
|\lambda|=\deg((R\;|\;C))
, i.e. the size of
\lambda
is the degree of
(R\;|\;C)
taken as a polynomial in the variables
x_{i,j}
. The number of parts of the partition
\lambda
is the number of minor factors of
(R\;|\;C)
, which is well-defined since minors are irreducible.

The convenience of Young tableaux as an indexing scheme for bideterminants will become more apparent as we recall their additional combinatorial properties. A Young tableau shall be called
normal
if the entries in each column are strictly increasing from top to bottom. A Young tableau shall be called
semi-standard
15
15

15

[
DKR78
]
define the notion of a “standard” tableau, which is the transpose of what we are calling semi-standard. We choose to use the term semi-standard because “standard” refers to something else in modern combinatorics parlance (as in
[
DKR17
]
).
if it is normal and the entries in each row are non-decreasing left to right. For example, both
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
above are normal;
Y_{1}
is semi-standard while
Y_{2}
is not, because of the
9,8
that are in decreasing order in the second row.

We shall call a bitableau
standard
if both the row and column tableaux are semi-standard, and the bideterminant corresponding to a standard bitableau will be called a
standard bideterminant
. These will end up being the standard monomials in the bideterminant ASL structure, so the nomenclature “standard” is consistent.
Also, by convention, we define
(\;|\;)=1
. Correspondingly, we will say
[\;|\;]
is a standard bitableau, and thus
1
is a standard bideterminant.

Using basic facts about the determinant, observe that if either the column
(r_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,r_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)})
or
(c_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,c_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)})
contains repeated entries, then
\left(\,\left.r_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,r_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\;\right|\;c_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,c_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\,\right)=0
. Also, for permutations
\pi_{r},\pi_{c}\in\mathfrak{S}_{\lambda_{i}}
, we have that

\left(\,\left.r_{\pi_{r}(1)}^{(i)},\ldots,r_{\pi_{r}(\lambda_{i})}^{(i)}\;\right|\;c_{\pi_{c}(1)}^{(i)},\ldots,c_{\pi_{c}(\lambda_{i})}^{(i)}\,\right)=\mathrm{sgn}\left(\pi_{r}\right)\cdot\mathrm{sgn}\left(\pi_{c}\right)\cdot(r_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,r_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\;|\;c_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,c_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}).

Remark 7.2
.

Although the correspondence between bitableaux and bideterminants is surjective but not injective (as remarked above where we defined
sh(f)
), the correspondence between standard bitableaux and standard bideterminants is bijective.

The
content of a minor

\left(\,\left.r_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,r_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\;\right|\;c_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,c_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\,\right)
is a pair of vectors
(\alpha,\beta)
,
\alpha\in\Z_{\geq 0}^{m}
, and
\beta\in\Z_{\geq 0}^{n}
, where
\alpha_{s}=1
if
s\in\left\{r_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,r_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\right\}
, and
\alpha_{s}=0
otherwise, and
\beta_{t}=1
if
t\in\left\{c_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,c_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\right\}
, and
\beta_{t}=0
otherwise. Note that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\alpha_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\beta_{j}
and these sums are equal to the degree of the minor
\left(\,\left.r_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,r_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\;\right|\;c_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,c_{\lambda_{i}}^{(i)}\,\right)
. The
content of a bideterminant
is the (vector) sum of the contents of the minors that make up the bideterminant (counting multiplicity, if the same minor occurs more than once). For a bideterminant
f
, we will use the notation
\chi(f)
to denote its content.
We can also define the
content of a bitableau
as the content of the corresponding bideterminant, noting that this is well-defined despite what is noted in Remark
7.1.1
. Although we will not make use of this fact, it may help some readers to know that the content of a bideterminant is the same as its weight when the polynomial ring
\F[X]
is viewed as a
\text{GL}_{n}\times\text{GL}_{m}
-representation under the action by left- and right-multiplication.

7.1.2.
Bideterminant ASL structure on the polynomial ring

N.B: While the machinery we use in this section is not new
[
DKR78
,
Dés80
]
, our exact notion of ordering differs in subtle but crucial ways from the ordering used there, in order to get not just an ASL structure (which goes back to
[
dCEP82
,
Eis80
]
), but also an ASL term order.

The straightening law says that every bideterminant, and in turn, every element of
\F[X]
, has a unique presentation as a
\F
-linear combination of standard bideterminants. Additionally, the expression of a non-standard bideterminant as a
\F
-linear combination of standard bideterminants possesses some additional structure that makes it an ASL with a viable ASL term order, and in turn admits a theory of Gröbner bases in the basis of standard bideterminants. To make a precise statement (Theorem
7.1.2
), we need to introduce a few more definitions.

Definition 7.3

(Partial ordering on minors)
.

We define a partial order
(\leq)
on minors as follows. Given two minors
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\;|\;b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})
and
(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\;|\;d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
,

(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}\;|\;b_{1},\ldots,b_{m})\leq(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\;|\;d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})\iff m\geq n\text{ and }\forall i\in[n],a_{i}\leq c_{i}\text{ \& }b_{i}\leq d_{i}.

There is an equivalent definition of standard bideterminant in terms of this order. Namely, a bidetermintant
f=\prod_{i=1}^{t}f_{i}
, where all
f_{i}
are minors, is a standard bideterminant if all the
f_{i}
are pairwise comparable in the above partial order. Equivalently,
f
is a standard bideterminant if there is some
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{t}
such that

f_{\sigma(1)}\leq\ldots\leq f_{\sigma}(t).

In fact, this equivalent definition makes Remark
7.1.1
even more clear because there is only one order of minors which will satisfy the requirement, which in turn indicates that there is a unique standard bitableau corresponding to this standard bideterminant.

The now-standard Theorem
7.1.2
establishes that there is an ASL structure in the polynomial ring in the basis of standard bideterminants, relative to the ordering of minors of Definition
7.1.2
:

Theorem 7.4

(
[
dCEP82
,
Eis80
]
)
.

Let
H\subseteq\F[X]
be the set of minors of
X
, and let
\phi:H\hookrightarrow\F[X]
be the identity map. Let
\Sigma\subseteq\W^{H}
be the set of non-standard bideterminants. Finally, let
\leq
be the order from Definition
7.1.2
. Then
A^{bd}:=(\F[X],H,\leq,\Sigma,\phi)
is a
\F
-ASL.

We will use the ordering on minors (or rather, its reverse) to build an ASL term order on bideterminants. As our orderings are slight modifications of known orderings, we define them here; we will show they give an ASL term order in Theorem
7.2
below.

Definition 7.5

(store=defshapeorder, note=Total order on shapes (
\prec
))
.

Given two shapes
\lambda
and
\mu
, we say
\lambda
is
smaller than

\mu
if
\lambda
is of smaller size than
\mu
, or if
\lambda
and
\mu
have the same size, and
\lambda
is lexicographically larger than
\mu
(when viewed as partitions), equivalently in symbols we define:

\lambda\prec\mu\iff[|\lambda|<|\mu|]\text{ or }[|\lambda|=|\mu|\text{ and }\lambda>_{lex}\mu].

Definition 7.6

(store=defnorderbitab, note=Total order on standard bitableaux/bideterminants
(\prec)
16
16

16

Abusing notation by reusing
\prec
.
)
.

For a standard bitableau
[R\;|\;C]
, let
\theta([R\;|\;C])
denote the sequence obtained by first reading off
R
row by row, from left to right within each row, followed by the same for
C
. Given two standard bitableaux or standard bideterminants
f
and
g
, we define

f\prec g\iff\left[sh(f)\prec sh(g)\right]\text{ or }\left[sh(f)=sh(g)\text{ and }\theta(f)>_{lex}\theta(g)\right].

Remark 7.7
.

Compared to the ordering on shapes defined in
[
DKR78
]
, in order to get an ASL term order where “greater” agrees with the desired notion in the standard theory of Gröbner bases, our ordering first orders by size, and among shapes of the same size is the reverse of the order on shapes from
[
DKR78
]
. In contrast, they do not first order by size, although
[
DKR17
]
states a caveat about comparing two shapes where one of them is a prefix of another. Nonetheless, because the straightening algorithm of
[
DKR78
, Theorem 1]
always produces tableaux of the same content, they necessarily have the same size, and in that context our ordering is simply the reverse of theirs, so we get that their straightening algorithm only produces bitableaux that are lower (in our ordering).

Also, the order on standard bitableaux of the same shape, i.e. the order on the
\theta
sequences of bitableaux, is the ‘transpose’ of the order suggested in the proof of
[
DKR78
, Theorem 1]
(simply because we use the transpose convention for bitableaux everywhere), and is the reverse of the order suggested in
[
Dés80
]
(again, in order to make ours an ASL term order).

Example 7.8
.

By Definition
7.1.2
,
(1,3,7\;|\;1,2,9)<(2,3\;|\;3,4)
, whereas
(1,3,7\;|\;1,2,9)
and
(12\;|\;34)
are incomparable. Note, however, that although
(1,3,7\;|\;1,2,9)<(2,3\;|\;3,4)
as minors, as 1-column bideterminants we have the opposite ordering relative to
\prec
of Definition
7.1.2
(this is similar to the reversal that happens with the grevlex ordering in ordering Gröbner bases).
By Definition
7.1.2
,
(5,3)\prec(5,2,2,2,1)
, whereas
(5,3)\succ(5,4,4,2,1)
.

For more complicated examples of Definition
7.1.2
, consider the bitableaux

T_{1}
has shape
sh(\lambda_{1})=(4,2,2,1)
, and
T_{2}
has shape
sh(\lambda_{2})=(4,3)
, so since
sh(\lambda_{1})<_{lex}sh(\lambda_{2})
,
sh(\lambda_{1})\succ sh(\lambda_{2})
, and consequently
T_{1}\succ T_{2}
. For the same reason
T_{3}\succ T_{2}
. Finally,
\theta(T_{1})=(2,2,4,8,4,\ldots,6,10)
, and
\theta(T_{3})=(2,2,3,9,4,\ldots,10)
, and since
\theta(T_{1})>_{lex}\theta(T_{3})
,
T_{1}\prec T_{3}
.

Theorem 7.9

(Straightening law
[
Hod43
,
DRS74
,
DKR78
]
, see also
[
dCEP80
,
ABW81
,
BC03
,
BC01
]
)
.

Let
\F
be any arbitrary commutative ring, and let
\F[X]:=\F[\{X_{ij}:i\in[n],j\in[m]\}]
.

(I)

Suppose for two minors
f,g\in\F[X]
, the product
fg
is not a standard bideterminant, then there is a unique representation of
fg
as the following finite sum

fg=\sum_{i}{r_{i}}\eta_{i,1}\eta_{i,2},

where for all
i
,
r_{i}\in\F
(in fact they are integers),
\eta_{i,1}\eta_{i,2}
is a standard bitableau, and for some
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{2}
,
\eta_{i,\sigma(1)}<f
,
\eta_{i,\sigma(1)}<g
,
f<\eta_{i,\sigma(2)}
, and
g<\eta_{i,\sigma(2)}
.

(II)

By successive application of
(I)
, any arbitrary bideterminant
(R\;|\;C)
can be uniquely expressed as follows:

(7.2)

(R\;|\;C)=\sum a_{R_{i},C_{i}}(R_{i}\;|\;C_{i}),

where
(R_{i}\;|\;C_{i})
are standard, and only finitely many
a_{R_{i},C_{i}}\in\F
are non-zero.

(III)

Equation (
7.2
) satisfies the additional property that, for all
i
with
a_{R_{i},C_{i}}\neq 0
,

sh{(R_{i}\;|\;C_{i})}\leq sh{(R\;|\;C)},\qquad\text{and}\qquad\chi((R_{i}\;|\;C_{i}))=\chi((R\;|\;C)).

(IV)

Since every monomial is trivially a bideterminant corresponding to a bitableau where each tableau has columns all of length one, and number of columns equal to the degree of the monomial, as a consequence of
(II)
, any polynomial
f\in\F[X]
may be written uniquely as follows:

(7.3)

f=\sum a_{R,C}(R\;|\;C),

where the bideterminants
(R\;|\;C)
are standard, and only finitely many
a_{R,C}\in\F
are nonzero. Thus the standard bideterminants form a
\F
-linear basis of the polynomial ring.

See Appendix
C
for the algorithmic details of Theorem
7.1.2
(I)
. For any
f\in\F[X]
, we say that a standard bideterminant
(R\;|\;C)

occurs
or
appears
in
f
if it is accompanied with a non-zero coefficient in the expression of
f
(see Equation (
7.3
)) in the basis of standard bideterminants. We are careful to only apply this term to standard bideterminants.

Given two bitableaux
[R\;|\;C]
and
[R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime}]
, we define
[R+R^{\prime}\;|\;C+C^{\prime}]
to be any bitableau corresponding to the bideterminant
(R\;|\;C)\cdot(R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime})
. This corresponds to
R+R^{\prime}
having its set of columns be the union of the sets of columns of
R
and
R^{\prime}
, and similarly for
C
and
C^{\prime}
(taking care to maintain the association between columns of
R
with columns of
C
, and columns of
R^{\prime}
with their corresponding columns in
C^{\prime}
). We may sometimes refer to this as “merging” the columns of
R
and
R^{\prime}
, and those of
C
and
C^{\prime}
, though the operation must be done simultaneously on both
R,R^{\prime}
and
C,C^{\prime}
.

Remark 7.10
.

(1)

[R+R^{\prime}\;|\;C+C^{\prime}]
is not well-defined usually because, if there are columns of the same height, all permutations of those columns are acceptable. For example, if

[R\;|\;C]=\left[\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox are valid representations of
[R+R^{\prime}\;|\;C+C^{\prime}]
.

then there is no candidate for
[R+R^{\prime}\;|\;C+C^{\prime}]
which is a standard bitableau.

(3)

Although
[R+R^{\prime}\;|\;C+C^{\prime}]
is not uniquely defined,
(R+R^{\prime}\;|\;C+C^{\prime})
is, and satisfies

(R+R^{\prime}\;|\;C+C^{\prime})=(R\;|\;C)\cdot(R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime}).

The following result gives the leading standard bitableau (or equivalently standard bideterminant) in the expression (
7.2
). For a
normal

[R\;|\;C]
, define
[^{(s)}(R)\;|\;^{(s)}(C)]
to be the bitableau obtained from
[R\;|\;C]
by writing the entries of each row of each of the tableaux in ascending order.

Remark 7.11
.

For those who are used to the transpose convention of ours, note that obtaining
[^{(s)}(R)\;|\;^{(s)}(C)]
does
not
merely correspond to rearranging the order of indices in each minor, and thereby correspond just to a sign change of the bideterminant; it is really (potentially) changing which minors are occurring. Also, the sorting operation is done separately to
R
and
C
, which again can change the bideterminant.

Theorem 7.12

(
[
Dés80
]
)
.

(I)

Let
[R\;|\;C]
be a normal bitableau. Then
[^{(s)}(R)\;|\;^{(s)}(C)]
is the largest standard bitableau (relative to our ordering
\prec
of Definition
7.1.2
) whose bideterminant appears in the decomposition of
(R\;|\;C)
as in expression (
7.2
), and has coefficient 1 in that expression.

(II)

Given two standard bitableaux
[R_{1}\;|\;C_{1}]
and
[R_{2}\;|\;C_{2}]
,
[^{(s)}(R_{1}+R_{2})\;|\;^{(s)}(C_{1}+C_{2})]
is a standard bitableau, and

LT_{\prec}((R_{1}\;|\;C_{1})(R_{2}\;|\;C_{2}))=(^{(s)}(R_{1}+R_{2})\;|\;^{(s)}(C_{1}+C_{2})).

Example 7.13

(Merging and sorting)
.

For example,

[R\;|\;C]

[R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime}]

[R+R^{\prime}\;|\;C+C^{\prime}]

\left[\,\left.{}^{(s)}(R+R^{\prime})\;\right|\;^{(s)}(C+C^{\prime})\,\right]

7.2.
bd-Gröbner bases in the polynomial ring

Theorem
7.2
below is the main theorem of this section, which establishes that there is a theory of ASL Gröbner bases in the basis of standard bideterminants in the polynomial ring.

Theorem 7.14

(store=thmbdgrobnerbasis)
.

Let
A^{bd}=(\F[X],H,\leq,\Sigma,\phi)
be as defined in Theorem
7.1.2
. Then the order
\preceq
in Definition
7.1.2
is an ASL term order on
A^{bd}
. Thus
(A^{bd},\preceq)
admits a theory of ASL Gröbner bases.

Proof.

Theorem
7.1.2
gives us that
A^{bd}
is indeed an ASL. All we have to do is to prove that
\preceq
is a valid ASL term order. Once this is in place, all the results of the prior sections may be applied in the setting of the standard bideterminant basis.

First observe that Axiom
(ATO-1)
is easily proven by noting that
1
corresponds to the bitableau
[\;|\;]
, which in turn is the only bitableau that has size
0
; Definition
7.1.2
first orders by size, which ensures that
1\preceq m
for all standard bideterminants
m
.

To establish
(ATO-2)
, first notice that the product of two standard bideterminants is never
0
. Since
A^{bd}
is a domain, by Remark
4.1.1
, it is necessary and sufficient to show that for standard bideterminants
f,g,h
, if
f\prec g
, then
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)
.

So we now show
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)
when
f\prec g
. We first prove this for the case where
h
is a single minor, and then use this to derive the general case below. So suppose
h
is a single minor of size
l_{h}
.

We need the following simple claim which is proved in Appendix
B
.

Claim 7.15

(store=claimlexicographicunion)
.

Given a multiset
A
with elements from
\N
, let
\mathcal{D}(A)
denote the list of elements of
A
in descending order, and let
\mathcal{U}(A)
denote the list of elements of A in ascending order.

(I)

Suppose
\mathcal{D}(A)<_{lex}\mathcal{D}(B)
. Then for any
\gamma\in\N
,
\mathcal{D}(A\cup\{\gamma\})<_{lex}\mathcal{D}(B\cup\gamma)
.

(II)

Suppose
\mathcal{U}(A)<_{lex}\mathcal{U}(B)
. Then for any
\gamma\in\N
,
\mathcal{U}(A\cup\{\gamma\})<_{lex}\mathcal{U}(B\cup\gamma)
.

[Case 1
sh(f)\neq sh(g)
]: Then since
f\prec g
,
sh(f)\prec sh(g)
. From Theorem
7.1.2
, we have that

|sh(\text{LM}_{sm}(fh))|=|sh(f)|+l_{h},\text{ and }|sh(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))|=|sh(g)|+l_{h}.

Thus, if
|sh(f)|<|sh(g)|
, then
|\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)|<|\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)|
, proving that
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)
.

It remains to consider the case where
|sh(f)|=|sh(g)|
. Since
f\prec g
, it must be true that
sh(g)<_{lex}sh(f)
. Now, by Theorem
7.1.2
, we have that the shape of the leading standard monomial that appears in
fh
is
sh(\text{LM}_{sm}(fh))=\mathcal{D}(sh(f)\cup\{l_{h}\})
,
17
17

17

We abuse notation here by treating
sh(f)
as a set while it is supposed to be a list.
where
l_{h}
is the height of the single column of the bitableau corresponding to
h
. Also, the shape
sh(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))=\mathcal{D}(sh(g)\cup\{l_{h}\})
. Thus by Claim
7.2
-
(I)
, we have that
sh(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))<_{lex}sh(\text{LM}_{sm}(fh))
, which implies that
sh(\text{LM}_{sm}(fh))\prec sh(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))
, and consequently,
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)
.

[Case 2
sh(f)=sh(g)
]: In this case, we must have
\theta(g)<_{lex}\theta(f)
. We shall first prove that
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)
when
f
(and of course
g
too because
sh(f)=sh(g)
) has exactly one row. Later, we will claim that this is without loss of generality, thus establishing
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)
for any
f,g,h
. Let the bitableaux be

f=\left[\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$f^{(r)}_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\ldots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$f^{(r)}_{s}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\;\right|\;{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$f^{(c)}_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$f^{(c)}_{s}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right],g=\left[\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$g^{(r)}_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\ldots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$g^{(r)}_{s}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\;\right|\;{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$g^{(c)}_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\ldots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$g^{(c)}_{s}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right],}}}}}}}}}}}}

and

h=\left[\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$h^{(r)}_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\vdots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$h^{(r)}_{t}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\;\right|\;{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$h^{(c)}_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\vdots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$h^{(c)}_{t}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right].}}}}}}

By Theorem
7.1.2
, we have that

\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(fh))=\left(\underbrace{\mathcal{U}(\{f^{(r)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r)}_{s},h^{(r)}_{1}\})}_{\mathcal{F}_{1}},h^{(r)}_{2},\ldots,h^{(r)}_{t},\underbrace{\mathcal{U}(\{f^{(c)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(c)}_{s},h^{(c)}_{1}\})}_{\mathcal{F}_{2}},h^{(c)}_{2},\ldots,h^{(c)}_{t}\right),

and

\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))=\left(\overbrace{\mathcal{U}(\{g^{(r)}_{1},\ldots,g^{(r)}_{s},h^{(r)}_{1}\})}^{\mathcal{G}_{1}},h^{(r)}_{2},\ldots,h^{(r)}_{t},\overbrace{\mathcal{U}(\{g^{(c)}_{1},\ldots,g^{(c)}_{s},h^{(c)}_{1}\})}^{\mathcal{G}_{2}},h^{(c)}_{2},\ldots,h^{(c)}_{t}\right).

By definition, we have

\theta(f)=(f^{(r)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r)}_{s},f^{(c)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(c)}_{s}),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\theta(g)=(g^{(r)}_{1},\ldots,g^{(r)}_{s},g^{(c)}_{1},\ldots,g^{(c)}_{s}).

Since
\theta(g)<_{lex}\theta(f)
, we can consider where the first mismatch in these two sequences occurs. It must either occur in the entries coming from the row tableaux or the column tableaux.

If the first mismatch comes from the row tableaux, that is, in
(f^{(r)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r)}_{s})
and
(g^{(r)}_{1},\ldots,g^{(r)}_{s})
, then we are certain to have a mismatch between
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}
. By Claim
7.2
-
(II)
, we have that

\mathcal{U}(\{g^{(r)}_{1},\ldots,g^{(r)}_{s},h^{(r)}_{1}\})<_{lex}\mathcal{U}(\{f^{(r)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(r)}_{s},h^{(r)}_{1}\}),

proving that
\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))<_{lex}\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(fh))
, and in turn,
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)
.

On the other hand, suppose that the first mismatch between
\theta(f)
and
\theta(g)
occurs in the column tableaux, that is, between
(f^{(c)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(c)}_{s})
and
(g^{(c)}_{1},\ldots,g^{(c)}_{s})
. Similar to before, comparing
\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(fh))
and
\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))
is equivalent to comparing
\mathcal{F}_{2}
and
\mathcal{G}_{2}
, and in this case, Claim
7.2
-
(II)
gives us that
\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))<_{lex}\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(fh))
, implying
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)
.

We have proved the claim for the case of single row bitableaux
f
and
g
, and it remains to be argued that this is without loss of generality. Since
sh(f)=sh(g)
, the first mismatch between
\theta(f)
and
\theta(g)
will occur in some row
\kappa
of both bitableaux. Even after multiplication by
h
, Theorem
7.1.2
implies that the mismatch between
\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(fh))
and
\theta(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))
will be continue to be in row
\kappa
of
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)
and
(\text{LM}_{sm}(gh))
, and thus the same argument above, applied to just the row
\kappa
, gives us
\text{LM}_{sm}(fh)\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(gh)
in full generality.
∎

7.3.
Algorithmic aspects of finding bd-Gröbner bases

In this section we present and prove Algorithm
1
, which takes two standard bideterminants as input and returns the set of least common standard multiples (Definition
5.3
) of these two standard bideterminants, relative to
A^{bd}_{gen}
. Note that it is completely straightforward to return least common multiples relative to
A_{disc}^{bd}
; it is exactly equivalent to taking LCMs by considering standard monomials as elements of
R[H]
(see Obs.
5.3
). While Lemma
6.2
shows that LCMs relative to any algebra of leading terms are computable in finite time, Algorithm
1
is tailored to the specific case of bideterminants relative to
A_{gen}^{bd}
and is not only more efficient than the generic one of Lemma
6.2
, but also yields more insight into the nature of LCMs in the bideterminant setting.

Convention 7.16
.

In this section, we will be dealing with multisets. Just to clarify, a multiset
A
containing elements from
\N
is naturally equivalent to a function
m_{A}:\N\rightarrow\W
. Given two multisets
A
and
B
,
A\cup B
will denote
m_{A\cup B}:\N\rightarrow\W
that takes
x\mapsto\max\left\{m_{A}(x),m_{B}(x)\right\}
.
A\setminus B
will denote
m_{A\setminus B}:\N\rightarrow\W
that takes
x\mapsto\max\left\{m_{A}(x)-m_{B}(x),0\right\}
.
|A|
will denote
\sum_{x\in\N}m_{A}(x)
, and we will say
A\subseteq B
if
m_{A}(x)\leq m_{B}(x)
for all
x\in\N
. Finally, instead of describing
A
by
m_{A}
, we will describe
A
by listing all the elements
x\in\N
for which
m_{A}(x)>0
, and in our description, each
x
will be listed
m_{A}(x)
number of times. For example,if
m_{A}
is such that
m_{A}(1)=1,m_{A}(2)=2,m_{A}(3)=1
, and
m_{A}(x)=0
for all
x>3
, then we will write
A=\{\!\!\{1,2,2,3\}\!\!\}
.

Notation 7.17
.

Given a tableau
T
, let
\mathcal{R}(T,i)
denote the multiset of elements of the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
T
(which will be empty if
T
has no
i^{\text{th}}
row).

Also, we will take for granted the following two combinatorial procedures:

(1)

get-admissible-shapes

\bm{\left(\Lambda,(r_{i})_{i\in[N]},(c_{i})_{i\in[N]}\right)}:
The first parameter
\lambda
is one single shape, and the second and third parameters, i.e.
(r_{i})_{i\in[N]}
and
(c_{i})_{i\in[N]}
, are lists of sizes. This routine returns all valid shapes
\lambda^{\prime}
such that

(a)

number of columns in
\lambda^{\prime}
exactly equals
\max(r_{1},\ldots,r_{N},c_{1},\ldots,c_{N})
,

(b)

number of columns in the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
\lambda^{\prime}
is at least
\max(r_{i},c_{i})
, and

(c)

treating
\lambda^{\prime}
and
\lambda
as multisets, it is true that
\lambda\subseteq\lambda^{\prime}
.

(2)

get-admissible-standard-bd

\bm{\left(\Lambda,(R_{i})_{i\in[N]},(C_{i})_{i\in[N]}\right)}:
The first parameter
\Lambda
is a set of shapes. The second and third parameters are both a list of sets of elements; each
R_{i}
contains a set of elements that must be present in the
i^{\text{th}}
row of the row tableau of any bitableau that is returned, and each
C_{i}
contains a list of elements that must be present in the
i^{\text{th}}
row of the column tableau of any bitableau that is returned. This routine returns the set of all standard bitableaux whose shape
\lambda
is in
\Lambda
, and which can be formed while ensuring that all elements of
R_{i}
are present in the
i^{\text{th}}
row of the row tableau and that all elements of
C_{i}
are present in the
i^{\text{th}}
row of the column bitableau.

Remark 7.18
.

Note that the content of the
i
-th row will not be exactly
R_{i}
(it will, by construction, be a super-multiset, but may properly contain
R_{i}
). There will in general be LCMs that realize this possibility, though there will also be at least one row of the row tableau whose content is exactly
R_{i}
or one row of the column tableau whose content is exactly
C_{i}
.

Theorem
7.3
below proves the correctness of Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

Algorithm to compute
LCM_{sm}^{lt}(\cdot)
for a specific
A_{lt}

1:

Bitableau
f=[R_{f}\;|\;C_{f}]
,
g=[R_{g}\;|\;C_{g}]
, with
sh(f)=(\lambda^{(f)}_{1},\ldots,\lambda^{(f)}_{r})
,
sh(g)=(\lambda^{(g)}_{1},\ldots,\lambda^{(g)}_{s})

2:

N=\max(\lambda^{(f)}_{1},\lambda^{(g)}_{1})

3:

for

i\leftarrow 1
to
N

do

4:

re_{i}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}(R_{f},i)\cup\mathcal{R}(R_{g},i)

\triangleright
Multi-set union, see Convention
7.3

5:

ce_{i}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}(C_{f},i)\cup\mathcal{R}(C_{g},i)

\triangleright
Multi-set union

6:

rd_{i}\leftarrow|re_{i}|

7:

cd_{i}\leftarrow|ce_{i}|

8:

end

for

9:

\Lambda^{(lcm)}\leftarrow\textsc{get-admissible-shapes}\left(sh(f)\cup sh(g),(rd_{i})_{i\in[N]},(cd_{i})_{i\in[N]}\right)

10:

\mathfrak{L}\leftarrow\textsc{get-admissible-standard-bd}\left(\Lambda^{(lcm)},(re_{i})_{i\in[N]},(ce_{i})_{i\in[N]}\right)

\triangleright
LCMs relative to
A_{gen}^{bd}

11:

return

\{(R\;|\;C)\in\mathfrak{L}:f\mid_{lt}(R\;|\;C)\text{ and }g\mid_{lt}(R\;|\;C)\}
.

Theorem 7.19

(store=thmlcmalgo)
.

Given two standard bideterminants
f=[R_{f}\;|\;C_{f}]
and
g=[R_{g}\;|\;C_{g}]
such that
\pi_{lt}(f)\pi_{lt}(g)\neq 0
, Algorithm
1
halts in finite time and returns the set of least common standard multiples of
f
and
g
relative to
A_{lt}^{bd}
.

Understanding how divisibility works in
A^{bd}_{gen}
is the key piece required in the proof of Theorem
7.3
. To get our more combinatorial understanding of divisibility in
A^{bd}_{gen}
, we use the following definition.

Definition 7.20

(
[R^{\prime}\setminus R\;|\;C^{\prime}\setminus C]
)
.

Let
[R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime}]
and
[R\;|\;C]
be standard bitableaux of shapes
\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\lambda^{\prime}_{s})
and
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{t})
respectively, such that
\lambda\subseteq\lambda^{\prime}
as multisets, and the multiset of elements of every row of
R
(resp.,
C
) is a sub-multiset of the elements of the same row in
R^{\prime}
(resp.,
C^{\prime}
).
Then
[R^{\prime}\setminus R\;|\;C^{\prime}\setminus C]
is defined to be the standard bitableau of shape
sh(\lambda^{\prime})\setminus sh(\lambda)
and

\mathcal{R}(R^{\prime}\setminus R,i)=\mathcal{R}(R^{\prime},i)\setminus\mathcal{R}(R,i),\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{R}(C^{\prime}\setminus C,i)=\mathcal{R}(C^{\prime},i)\setminus\mathcal{R}(C,i).

Example 7.21

(Formation of
[R^{\prime}\setminus R\;|\;C^{\prime}\setminus C]
)
.

[R\;|\;C]
and
[R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime}]

[R^{\prime}\setminus R\;|\;C^{\prime}\setminus C]

4. compactify to get
[R^{\prime}\setminus R\;|\;C^{\prime}\setminus C]

\downarrow

.

Lemma 7.22
.

Consider
(A^{bd},A^{bd}_{lt})
. Suppose
f=(R_{f}\;|\;C_{f})
and
g=(R_{g}\;|\;C_{g})
are both standard. If
f\mid_{lt}g
, then
[R_{g}\setminus R_{f}\;|\;C_{g}\setminus C_{f}]
is defined and satisfies

(R_{g}\setminus R_{f}\;|\;C_{g}\setminus C_{f})=\frac{\,g\,}{\,f\,}{}_{{\scriptscriptstyle lt}}.

Proof.

Immediate consequence of Theorem
7.1.2
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
7.3
.

Since we are working in the ring
\F[\{X_{i,j}\}_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}]
, the columns of our bitableaux can be at most
\min(m,n)
long, because any minor corresponding to a taller column is zero since it has a repeated row or repeated column. Thus both procedures
get-admissible-shapes
and
get-admissible-standard-bd
halt in finite time, as they are exploring finite sets for bitableaux satisfying certainly easily recognizable conditions. By Prop.
6.1
, the division checks in
A_{lt}
on line
11
are also computable in finite time. These together imply that Algorithm
1
indeed halts in finite time. It remains to establish that, relative to
A_{gen}^{bd}
, the algorithm returns all least common multiples (completeness), and returns nothing other than least common multiples (soundness). Once we show that it works correctly relative to
A^{bd}_{gen}
, we note that line
11
then ensures it works correctly relative to
A^{bd}_{lt}
: For if
m,m^{\prime}
are two standard monomials in any
A_{lt}
, then the presence of term ordering guarantees that if
m\mid_{lt}m^{\prime}
, then there is a unique standard term
m^{\prime}/m
(Lemma
4.1.2
); thus if we can compute
t=m^{\prime}/m
in
A_{gen}
, then we can simply check in
A_{lt}
whether
tm=m^{\prime}
(in which case
m^{\prime}
is indeed a multiple of
m
in
A_{lt}
) or
tg=0
(in which case it is not).

(Soundness.) In Line
10
, Algorithm
1
computes
\mathfrak{L}
which we claim is exactly equal to the set of LCMs of
f
and
g
relative to
A_{gen}^{bd}
. By the description of
get-admissible-standard-bd
, we can see that in every standard bideterminant returned in Line
10
, the
i^{\text{th}}
row of the row tableau contains both
\mathcal{R}(R_{f},i)
and
\mathcal{R}(R_{g},i)
as subsets, and the
i^{\text{th}}
row of the column tableau contains both
\mathcal{R}(C_{f},i)
and
\mathcal{R}(C_{g},i)
as sub-multisets. Also, by the description of
get-admissible-shapes
, the call in Line
9
ensures that the shapes of the standard bitableaux that are eventually returned by Algorithm
1
are such that they contain both
sh(f)
and
sh(g)
as subshapes. Thus, by Lemma
7.3
, we can see that every standard bideterminant that is returned by Algorithm
1
is certainly a common multiple of
f
and
g
.

To see that the common multiples returned are least, we have to show that for every standard bideterminant
l
that is returned by the algorithm, there is no
l^{\prime}\neq l
that is a common multiple of
f,g\in A^{bd}_{gen}
satisfying
l^{\prime}\mid_{gen}l
. Observe in the description of
get-admissible-shapes
that all shapes returned have length exactly equal to
\max(r_{1},\ldots,r_{N},c_{1},\ldots,c_{N})
. By Lemma
7.3
, for any such
l^{\prime}
to satisfy
l^{\prime}\mid_{gen}l
, we would need the length of the shape of
l^{\prime}
to be strictly smaller than the length of the shape of
l
(the length of the shape of
R\backslash R^{\prime}
is strictly less than that of
R
unless
R^{\prime}
is the empty tableaux). This is impossible because it would mean that the elements in some row of the row tableau of
l^{\prime}
is a strict sub-multiset of the corresponding row in
R_{f}
or
R_{g}
, or the elements in some row of the column tableau of
l^{\prime}
is a strict sub-multiset of the corresponding row in
C_{f}
or
C_{g}
. This in turn would mean that
l^{\prime}
cannot possibly be a common multiple of
f
and
g
, again by Lemma
7.3
. This proves the soundness of the algorithm.

(Completeness.) To establish completeness, we first argue that the fact that the length of shapes returned by Algorithm
1
is equal to
\max(r_{1},\ldots,r_{N},c_{1},\ldots,c_{N})
is a necessary condition for being a least common multiple. We have already argued that it is not possible for a least common multiple, say
l
, to have length strictly smaller than
\max(r_{1},\ldots,r_{N},c_{1},\ldots,c_{N})
. It is also not possible for
l
to have length strictly larger than
\max(r_{1},\ldots,r_{N},c_{1},\ldots,c_{N})
because in that case, we would be able to remove a column from
l
, and after replacing some elements if necessary (to ensure that
f\mid_{gen}l^{\prime}
and
g\mid_{gen}l^{\prime}
), we can form
l^{\prime}
which is also a common multiple of
f
and
g
; since it is true by construction that
l^{\prime}\mid_{gen}l
, this would mean that
l
was not a least common multiple to begin with. Thus it is clear that all
l
have to necessarily have length equal to
\max(r_{1},\ldots,r_{N},c_{1},\ldots,c_{N})
. It is also a necessary condition that any shape of a least common multiple must the contain the shapes of
f
and
g
as subshapes; this is guaranteed from the description of
get-admissible-shapes
. Hence, we can conclude that the shapes returned by
get-admissible-shapes
exactly equals the set of shapes in
LCM_{sm}^{gen}(f,g)
.

By Lemma
7.3
, it is clear that it is a necessary condition that the
i^{\text{th}}
row of the row tableau of any least common multiple
l
must contain both the elements of the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
R_{f}
and the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
R_{g}
, and it is also a necessary condition that the
i^{\text{th}}
row of the column tableau of
l
must contain both the elements of the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
C_{f}
and the
i^{\text{th}}
row of
C_{g}
. Looking at the description of
get-admissible-standard-bd
, it is clear that these conditions are satisfied. Finally, since we enumerate all possible standard bideterminants which satisfy the above necessary conditions, it is clear that the algorithm returns all least common multiples, thus establishing the completeness of the algorithm.
∎

Example 7.23

(Example run of Algorithm
1
)
.

Let the ambient ring be
\F[\{X_{i,j}\}_{i\in[3],j\in[3]}]
.
Let the input to Algorithm
1
be

rd_{1}=2,rd_{2}=3,rd_{3}=1,\qquad cd_{1}=2,cd_{2}=2,cd_{3}=1.

In Line

, we look for shapes that satisfy the conditions that the number of columns of the shape is equal to

are valid standard bitableaux (the boxes with thicker border contains elements of
re_{*}
or
ce_{*}
, and the remaining boxes can be freely set whilst ensuring that the resulting bitableaux is standard). All of the above standard bitableaux comprise
LCM_{sm}^{lt}(f,g)
.

7.4.
Universal bd-Gröbner bases for determinantal ideals

For
S\subseteq[m],T\subseteq[n]
, write
M_{S,T}
for the minor of the
m\times n
matrix
X
, whose rows are indexed by
S
and columns are indexed by
T
.

Theorem 7.24

(store=thmtminorsbdgb)
.

Let
m>n\geq r
, and consider the determinantal ideal
I_{r}\subseteq\F[\{X_{i,j}\}_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}]=A^{bd}
generated by the size-
r
minors of
X
. Then

G_{r}:=\left\{M_{S,T}:S\subseteq[m],T\subseteq[n],n\geq|S|=|T|\geq r\right\}

is a universal bd-Gröbner basis for
I_{r}
(relative to any
A_{lt}^{bd}
).

In some sense, this proof is just a restatement of the standard fact (which follows from the bideterminant straightening law) that
I_{r}
consists precisely of the polynomials each of whose standard monomials is a standard bideterminant of length
\geq r
. The details in the following proof are just to verify that all the ASL pieces fit together properly.

Proof.

By the proof of Theorem
4.4
, it suffices to show that
G_{r}
is a universal ASL Gröbner basis relative to arbitrary ASL term orders for
A^{bd}
and with algebra of leading terms
A^{bd}_{disc}
. We use the characterization of ASL Gröbner bases in terms of divisibility (Proposition
4.1.4
).

Since the elements of
G_{r}
are themselves standard monomials, they are equal to their own leading terms regardless of choice of term order. Next, by the straightening law (Theorem
7.1.2
and the algorithm described in Appendix
C
), a polynomial
f
is in
I_{r}
if and only if for every standard monomial
m
occurring in
f
there is a minor of size
\geq r
that divides
m
(divides in
A^{bd}
). The latter set of minors is precisely
G_{r}
. Suppose
f\in I_{r}
. Regardless of term order, its leading term is thus divisible (in
A^{bd}
) by some element of
G_{r}
; say the leading term is
m
and let
M_{S,T}\in G_{r}
be an element of
G_{r}
that divides
m
in
A^{bd}
. All that remains is to show that
\pi_{disc}(M_{S,T})\mid\pi_{disc}(m)
in
A^{bd}_{disc}
. But this is almost immediate: for
m/M_{S,T}
is another standard monomial
m^{\prime}
, and since the product
m^{\prime}M_{S,T}
is standard in
A^{bd}
, in any algebra of leading terms we must have
\pi_{lt}(m^{\prime})\pi_{lt}(M_{S,T})=\pi_{lt}(m)
. Thus
G_{r}
is an ASL Gröbner basis relative to any term order and any algebra of leading terms.
∎

Remark 7.25
.

In the case of Theorem
7.4
, we see that a bd-Gröbner basis can be much larger than an ordinary Gröbner basis. In fact, all standard bideterminants with at least one of the minors being one of
G_{r}
(as defined in Theorem
7.4
) are a
\F
-basis of
I_{r}

[
BCRV22
, Corollary 3.4.2]
. On the other hand, the proof of the theorem is extremely short and elementary.

Corollary 7.26

(store=thmmaximalminorsbdgb,note=of Theorem
7.4
)
.

The set maximal minors of a generic matrix is a universal bd-Gröbner basis for the ideal they generate.

Proof.

Immediate by setting
r=n
in Theorem
7.4
.
∎

Remark 7.27
.

It is worth noting how easy proofs of Corollary
7.4
and Theorem
7.4
were compared to analogous proofs using ordinary Gröbner bases. Indeed, since we are working in an ASL where each minor is a single variable, there was no choice for what the leading standard monomial was, whereas in the ordinary polynomial ring each such minor has
n!
terms and one must contend with the various possibilities for what the leading terms are.

7.5.
Relation to ordinary Gröbner bases

In this section we show that every bd-Gröbner basis relative to
A_{disc}^{bd}
is also an ordinary Gröbner basis relative to the diagonal term order, but the converse does not hold.

Let
<_{diag}
denote the so-called “diagonal ordering” on monomials in the polynomial ring
\F[\{x_{ij}\}_{i\in[n],j\in[m]}]
, that is,
<_{diag}
is the lexicographic ordering on monomials induced by the following order on variables:

x_{11}>_{diag}\dotsb>_{diag}x_{1m}>_{diag}x_{21}>_{diag}\dotsb>_{diag}x_{2m}>_{diag}\dotsb>_{diag}x_{n1}>_{diag}\dotsb>_{diag}x_{nm}.

We use
\text{LM}_{diag}
,
\text{LT}_{diag}
, etc. to denote the leading monomial, leading term, etc. with respect to the diagonal ordering.

The following lemma is the key to showing that bd-Gröbner bases relative to
A_{disc}^{bd}
are also ordinary Gröbner basis relative to the diagonal term order.

Lemma 7.28
.

(1)

If
f,g
are standard bideterminants with
f\leq g
, then
\text{LM}_{diag}(f)\leq_{diag}\text{LM}_{diag}(g)
.

(2)

For all polynomials
f
,
\text{LM}_{diag}(f)=\text{LM}_{diag}(\text{LM}_{sm}^{bd}(f))
.

(3)

Suppose
f,g
are two standard bideterminants with
g\mid_{lt}f
in
A_{disc}^{bd}
. Then
\text{LM}_{diag}(g)
divides
\text{LM}_{diag}(f)
.

Proof.

First, note that the leading monomial of a minor is precisely the product of its diagonal elements. (This can be proved by induction: if the rows involved are
r_{1}<r_{2}<\dotsb<r_{k}
and the columns are
c_{1}<c_{2}<\dotsb<c_{k}
, then the largest variable involved is
x_{r_{1},c_{1}}
. Now induct on the remaining
(k-1)\times(k-1)
minor.)

(1)

Let
f,g
be standard bideterminants with
f\leq g
. Suppose
f=[R\;|\;C]
and
g=[R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime}]
. If the bitableau first differ at
r=R_{ij}>R^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}=r^{\prime}
, then we have
\text{LM}_{diag}(g)
has at least one more variable from row
r^{\prime}
than
\text{LM}_{diag}(f)
, and thus
\text{LM}_{diag}(f)<\text{LM}_{diag}(g)
. If the first difference is in the column tableaux, the same proof works
mutatis mutandis
.

(2)

Since
\text{LM}_{sm}^{bd}(f)
is greater than all other standard bideterminants
b
appearing in
f
, we have
\text{LM}_{diag}(\text{LM}_{sm}^{bd}(f))>\text{LM}_{diag}(b)
, by part (1). Thus
\text{LM}_{diag}(\text{LM}_{sm}^{bd}(f))=\text{LM}_{diag}(f)
.

(3)

Let
f,g
be two standard bideterminants with
g
dividing
f
in
A_{disc}^{bd}
. Then the columns of the bitableaux for
g
are a subset of the columns of the bitableaux for
f
. Since leading monomials multiply (for ordinary monomials in the polynomial ring), the leading monomial for
g
in the diagonal ordering is thus a sub-product of the leading monomial for
f
. ∎

Theorem 7.29
.

If
G
is a bd-Gröbner basis for an ideal
I
relative to
A_{disc}^{bd}
, then it is also a Gröbner basis with respect to the diagonal ordering.

Proof.

Let
f\in I
. Then
\pi_{disc}(\text{LM}_{sm}^{bd}(f))
is divisible by
\pi_{disc}(\text{LM}_{sm}^{bd}(g))
in
A_{disc}^{bd}
for some
g\in G
, so by Lemma
7.5
(
3
),
\text{LM}_{diag}(f)
is divisible by
\text{LM}_{diag}(g)
. Thus
\{\text{LM}_{diag}(g):g\in G\}
generates
\text{LM}_{diag}(I)
.
∎

We remark that the divisibility-theoretic characterization of Gröbner and bd-Gröbner bases was very helpful here; if we instead used Buchberger’s criterion, it was not at all clear—at least to us!—that all the S-polynomials with respect the diagonal ordering would reduce to 0.

Finally, we close this section by showing that the converse does not hold; that is, the concept of bd-Gröbner basis is not equivalent to Gröbner basis in the diagonal order. In fact, Example
6.13
already does this, as it gives a principal ideal in the polynomial ring but whose smallest bd-Gröbner basis has size 2. Here we give a related example where the ordinary Gröbner basis is not a singleton, just for some variety:

Example 7.30

(A Gröbner basis in the diagonal ordering that is not a bd-Gröbner basis)
.

Let
n=m=2
, and consider
I=\langle x_{11},x_{12}\rangle
. Since
I
is a monomial ideal already, it is easy to see that
\Gamma=\{x_{11},x_{12}\}
is the unique universal reduced (ordinary) Gröbner basis for
I
. However,
\Gamma
is not a bd-Gröbner basis relative to
A_{disc}^{bd}
. For
I
contains the minor
x_{11}x_{22}-x_{12}x_{21}
, but in the bideterminant ASL structure the latter is the single variable
X_{12|12}
, yet no element of of
\Gamma
divides the variable
X_{12|12}
in
A_{disc}^{bd}
.

The failure here is due to the fact that
\Gamma
is not Ann-closed. For the annihilator of
x_{12}
in
A^{bd}_{disc}
is
\langle x_{21}\rangle
.
Since we have

x_{21}x_{12}=\left[\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\;\right|\;{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right]=\left[\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\;\right|\;{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right]-\left[\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule
