depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\;\right|\;{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt&\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right],}}}}}}}}}}}}

if we add
X_{12|12}
to our generating set, we get an Ann-closed and S-closed generating set (as in Example
6.13
), and thus a bd-Gröbner basis
\{X_{1|1},X_{1|2},X_{12|12}\}
.

8.
Hilbert–Poincaré series and Krull dimension in pseudo-ASLs

In this section we show that in the
\N
-graded case we can use pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases—in particular through the monomial pseudo-ASLs of leading terms
A_{lt}
—to compute dimension of quotients of pseudo-ASLs using an algorithm similar to that in the ordinary case of polynomial rings.

8.1.
Background and Preliminaries

We begin by recalling some standard definitions and results regarding Hilbert–Poincaré series. Let
A
be an
\N
-graded ring over a commutative ring
R
, that is,
A=\bigoplus_{s\in\N}A_{s}
, where
A_{i}\cdot A_{j}\subseteq A_{i+j}
, and further suppose that each
A_{s}
is a free
R
-module (if
R
is a field this is automatic). If
a\in A
lies entirely in
A_{s}
for some
s
then we say that
a
is
homogeneous of degree
\deg(a)=s
. An ideal in
I
is homogeneous relative to this grading if
I=\bigoplus_{s}(A_{s}\cap I)
, and if
I
is a homogeneous ideal then the quotient ring
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I$}\right.}
inherits the grading from
A
in the natural way
\left({\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I$}\right.}\right)_{s}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A_{s}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$(I\cap A_{s})$}\right.}
. The
Hilbert–Poincaré function
of
A
is
h_{A}(s):=\dim_{R}A_{s}
and its
Hilbert–Poincaré series
is

H_{A}(t):=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}h_{A}(s)t^{s}\in\Z[\![t]\!].

As we will be particularly interested in the situation where
A
is an
\N
-graded pseudo-ASL—meaning (Def.
4.1.1
) that
A
is
\N
-graded and all standard monomials are homogeneous—we focus on that situation; in this preliminary section we first focus on the case of an ordinary polynomial ring.

Given a grading on the polynomial ring
R[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}]
, if all monomials are homogeneous, then in particular the generating variables
X_{i}
are homogeneous, so there is a function
d\colon[n]\to\N
where
d(i)=\deg(X_{i})
, and the degree of a monomial in
R[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}]
is then

\mathrm{deg}_{d}(x_{1}^{c_{1}}\ldots x_{n}^{c_{n}})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\cdot d(i).

Since any function
d\colon[n]\to\N
defines such a grading, given
d
we refer to the
d
-degree,
d
-homogeneity, etc. as the corresponding concepts for the grading given by
d
as above.

The following theorem collects some known facts about Hilbert–Poincaré series in relation to (ordinary) Gröbner bases. Recall (Def.
4.1.1
) that a term order
<
on a
\Z
-graded polynomial ring
R[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}]
is called a
graded term order
if
\mathrm{deg}(m)<\mathrm{deg}(m^{\prime})\Rightarrow m<m^{\prime}
, where here the grading is given by the function
\deg\colon\W^{n}\to\Z
.

Theorem 8.1
.

(I)

(cf.
[
CLO15
, §9.3, Prop. 4]
) Let
<
be graded term order on monomials in
R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, relative to the grading given by
\mathrm{deg}_{d}
. Then for any
d
-homogeneous ideal
I\subseteq R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
,

H_{R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]/I}=H_{R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]/LI(I)}.

If
G
is a Gröbner basis for
I
relative to
<
, then

H_{R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]/I}=H_{R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]/\left\langle\{LT(g):g\in G\}\right\rangle}.

(II)

(See App.
D.1
below, cf.
[
CLO15
, §10.2, Thm. 6]
.) There is an algorithm which, given a monomial ideal
I\subseteq R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
, computes the Hilbert–Poincaré series of
{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$I$}\right.}
as a rational function.

(III)

(
[
Smo72
, Thm. 5.5]
; see also
[
Ben98
, Prop 5.3.2]
,
[
Sta78
, p. 58]
.) The Krull dimension of
R[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}]/I
is equal to the order of the pole at
t=1
of
H_{R[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}]/I}
.

8.2.
Moving to pseudo-ASLs

Our strategy to compute the Hilbert–Poincaré series of
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I$}\right.}
is:

(1)

Compute a pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis
G
for
I
. By Theorem
8.2
,
H_{A/I}=H_{A_{lt}/LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)}
.

(2)

Use Prop.
8.2
to lift
\langle\pi_{lt}(g):g\in G\rangle
to a binomial ideal in
R[H]
and compute there.

(3)

In particular, if we use
A_{lt}=A_{disc}
, then the straightening relations for
A_{disc}
are monomials, so when we lift
\langle\pi_{disc}(g):g\in G\rangle
to
R[H]
we get a
monomial
ideal, for which no further Gröbner basis computation is necessary, as at that point one can directly compute the Hilbert–Poincaré series and Krull dimension (as in, e.g., App.
D.1
below, cf.
[
CLO15
, §10.2, Thm. 6]
).

In the remainder of this section we develop the results that support this approach, namely Theorem
8.2
and Proposition
8.2
.

Recall the definition of graded pseudo-ASL and graded pseudo-ASL term order (Def.
4.1.1
). An
\N
-graded pseudo-ASL can be equivalently defined as: there is a function
d\colon H\to\N
, using which we define the
d
-degree of monomials
m\in\W^{H}
as above, i.e. (
\deg_{d}(m):=\sum_{h\in H}m(h)d(h)
), and such that the straightening relations in
A
are
d
-homogeneous. Observe that the homogeneity of the straightening relations is a necessary and sufficient condition for the
d
-degree of standard monomials to be well-defined.

Henceforth, we will refer to the grading induced by
d
as the
d
-grading on
A
. Since
A
is finitely generated by
H
, we have an
R
-algebra isomorphism
\phi:A\rightarrow{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}$}\right.}
, which takes

H\ni h\mapsto x_{h}\in R[H],

naturally extended, where the ideal
\mathcal{S}\subseteq R[H]
is the defining ideal of
A
(and includes the straightening relations). As in the case of Gröbner bases, one can compute the Hilbert–Poincaré series in
A
by passing to a polynomial ring, as in the next proposition; the goal of the remainder of this section is to develop theory to compute the Hilbert–Poincaré series of
A
“natively to
A
”, that is, without passing to the polynomial ring.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
d\colon H\to\N
be a function such that the ASL
A=R[H]/\mathcal{S}
is
\N
-graded via
d
. Let
\psi:R[H]\twoheadrightarrow{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$\mathcal{S}$}\right.}
be the defining homomorphism of
A
, and for a
d
-homogeneous ideal
I\subseteq A
let
I^{\prime}:=\psi^{-1}(I)
. Then

H_{A/I}=H_{R[H]/I^{\prime}}.

Proof.

Again, since we have stipulated that
d
is such that the straightening relations in
A
are homogeneous, if we give
x_{h}\in R[H]
a degree of
d(h)
,
\mathcal{S}
is a
d
-homogeneous ideal in
R[H]
, and thus
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}$}\right.}
inherits a
d
-grading. Since both
h
and
x_{h}
have the same degrees in
A
and
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}$}\right.}
respectively,
\phi
is in fact an isomorphism of graded
R
-algebras. By the third ring isomorphism theorem, the ideal
I^{\prime}\subseteq R[H]
is such that
I^{\prime}:=\mathcal{S}+I
(where here we view elements of
I
as linear combinations of standard monomials in
R[H]
), thus if
I
is
d
-homogeneous in
A
, then
I^{\prime}
is
d
-homogeneous in
R[H]
. Thus once again, under the map which takes

{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I$}\right.}\ni h+I\mapsto x_{h}+I^{\prime}\in{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I^{\prime}$}\right.},

{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I$}\right.}
and
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I^{\prime}$}\right.}
are isomorphic as
d
-graded
R
-algebras.
∎

Remark 8.3
.

While we restrict our presentation to homogeneous ideals, it is possible to consider affine Hilbert–Poincaré series in order to extend our presentation to arbitrary ideals, as in, e. g.,
[
KR05
, Section 5.6]
.

Remark 8.4
.

In general, since every finitely generated algebra
A
is isomorphic as an algebra to the quotient of a polynomial ring, say
A\cong{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I$}\right.}
, one could define the Hilbert–Poincaré function of
A
as the (affine) Hilbert–Poincaré series of
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[X_{1},\dotsc,X_{n}]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I$}\right.}
. However, in this case, the Hilbert–Poincaré series itself is
not
an invariant of
A
, nor even is the Hilbert–Poincaré polynomial: they both can depend on the generators chosen. See
[
Kem11
, Remark 11.3(b)]
for an example. However the Krull dimension of
A
is an invariant of
A
, and thus is independent of the chosen presentation of
A

[
KR05
, Theorem 5.6.36]
, so can be computed using the affine Hilbert–Poincaré series of any presentation of
A
as a quotient of a polynomial ring.

Now let
A_{lt}
be an algebra of leading terms for
(A,\preceq)
. Since
A_{lt}
is finitely generated by
H
, we have an
R
-algebra isomorphism
\phi_{lt}:A_{lt}\rightarrow{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$R[H]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{lt}$}\right.}
for some ideal
\mathcal{S}_{lt}\subseteq R[H]
.
\mathcal{S}_{lt}
is a binomial ideal for all
A_{lt}
, and specifically,
\mathcal{S}_{disc}
is a monomial ideal.

Theorem 8.5

(store=thmhilbertaslalt)
.

Given a pseudo-ASL
A
on
H
,
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
, and
d\colon H\to\N
such that
\deg_{d}
makes
A
into a graded pseudo-ASL and
\preceq
a graded pseudo-ASL term order, let
I\subseteq A
be a
d
-homogeneous ideal. Then defining
\deg_{d}
on
A_{lt}
such that it agrees with
\deg_{d}
on
H
makes
A_{lt}
into a graded pseudo-ASL such that
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)\subseteq A_{lt}
is
d
-homogeneous, and we have

H_{A/I}=H_{A_{lt}/LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)}.

Here, if
\pi_{lt}\colon A\to A_{lt}
is the
R
-module isomorphism between
A
and
A_{lt}
that is the identity map on standard monomials in
A
, then we are extending
\deg_{d}
to
A_{lt}
by defining

\deg_{d}(\pi_{lt}(h)):=d(h).

Proof.

To see that
A_{lt}
is
\N
-graded by
\deg_{d}
, it is necessary and sufficient to show that the straightening relations for
A_{lt}
are
d
-homogeneous. But since the straightening relations in
A_{lt}
are truncations of the straightening relations in
A
, and the latter are
d
-homogeneous, the same must be true of the straightening relations in
A_{lt}
.

Thus under the
d
-grading of
A_{lt}
defined above,
\pi_{lt}:A\rightarrow A_{lt}
is in fact a graded
R
-module isomorphism, so we have
H_{A}=H_{A_{lt}}
.

To complete the proof, we will show that for each
s\in\N
we have

\dim_{R}(I\cap A_{s})=\dim_{R}(LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)\cap(A_{lt})_{s}).

This proof is similar to the “direct” computational proof for ordinary Gröbner bases (that is, not using Gröbner degenerations). Namely, let
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{m}
be an
R
-linear basis for
I\cap A_{s}
, where
m=\dim_{R}(I\cap A_{s})
. Let
\{m_{1},\dotsc,m_{N}\}
be a list of the standard monomials of
d
-degree exactly
s
, which is, in particular, a basis for
A_{s}
(and also, under
\pi_{lt}
,
(A_{lt})_{s}
), and ordered such that
i<j
iff
m_{i}\prec m_{j}
. Let
M
be the
m\times\dim A_{s}
matrix over
R
whose
(i,j)
entry is the coefficient of
m_{j}
in
f_{i}
. Doing row operations on this matrix corresponds to taking
R
-linear combinations of the
f_{i}
, and if the row operations are invertible, then the polynomials corresponding to the rows still span
I\cap A_{s}
. Putting
M
into row echelon form, we find a new basis for
I\cap A_{s}
in which all pairs of basis elements have distinct leading monomials. Those leading monomials are then a basis for
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)\cap(A_{lt})_{s}
, which thus also has dimension
m=\dim(I\cap A_{s})
.
∎

8.3.
An example: rank-1 matrices

Fix any
n
and
m
. In this section, we shall use the approach of the previous section to (re)derive the well known fact that the dimension of the affine variety of rank
1

n\times m
matrices taken as elements of
\C^{n\times m}
is equal to
n+m-1
. Obviously this is not a new result, and we don’t claim that the combinatorial calculations are easier than known (geometric or algebraic) proofs either. However, our analysis uses Theorem
7.4
which gives a
bd
-Gröbner basis of the ideal of
2
-minors of
X
, i.e.
I_{2}
, relative to
A_{disc}
, to calculate the Krull dimension of
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\C[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I_{2}$}\right.}
. The main point in showing this calculation is that getting a
bd
-Gröbner basis of
I_{2}
relative to
A^{bd}_{disc}
was relatively easier, and that this notion is useful to calculate dimension. This suggests that in cases where obtaining a
bd
-Gröbner basis is easier, one could subsequently obtain dimension in a relatively easier fashion as well. Another point of this calculation is that we show that
A^{bd}_{disc}
(as opposed to just
A^{bd}_{gen}
) can be used in an important way.

Consider the ASL
A^{bd}
as defined in Theorem
7.1.2
, where
X
is now specifically a generic
n\times m
matrix. The dimension of the variety of rank
1

n\times m
matrices is, by definition, equal to the Krull dimension of
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A^{bd}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I_{2}$}\right.}
, where
I_{2}
is the ideal generated by the size
2
minors of
X
. From Theorem
7.4
, we have that the set of minors of size
\geq 2
forms a
bd
-Gröbner basis of
I_{2}
relative to
A^{bd}_{disc}
. We shall calculate the Krull dimension of
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A^{bd}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I_{2}$}\right.}
by

(1)

giving
A^{bd}
a grading by
d:H\rightarrow\N
, which takes a minor
h
and sets
d(h)
equal to the size of the minor, and

(2)

calculating
H_{A^{bd}/I_{2}}
. Specifically, we will use Theorem
8.2
and the strategy laid out at the beginning of Section
8.2
to calculate
H_{A^{bd}/I_{2}}
by calculating
H_{A^{bd}_{disc}/LI_{bd}^{disc}(I_{2})}
, whose calculation reduces (without any further Gröbner basis calculation) to calculating the Krull dimension of a polynomial ring modulo a monomial ideal.

(3)

Calculating the latter Krull dimension using the standard algorithm.

Define

\mathcal{R}:=\C\left[\left\{X_{(S\;|\;T)}\right\}_{S\subseteq[n],T\subseteq[m],|S|=|T|}\right].

Also, let

\phi_{disc}^{bd}:A_{disc}^{bd}\rightarrow{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{R}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{S}_{disc}^{bd}$}\right.}

be the
\C
-algebra isomorphism that maps
h\mapsto x_{h}
, where
h\in H
is a minor. Giving each
x_{h}\in\mathcal{R}
a degree of
d(h)
,
\phi_{disc}^{bd}
is in fact a graded
\C
-algebra isomorphism. The monomial ideal
\mathcal{S}^{bd}_{disc}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
has a structure that is easy to describe:

\mathcal{S}^{bd}_{disc}=\left\langle\left\{x_{(S_{1}\;|\;T_{1})}x_{(S_{2}\;|\;T_{2})}:(S_{1}\;|\;T_{1})(S_{2}\;|\;T_{2})\text{ is a non-standard bideterminant in }A^{bd}\right\}\right\rangle.

By Theorem
8.2
and Proposition
8.2
,

H_{A^{bd}/I_{2}}=H_{A^{bd}_{disc}/LI_{bd}^{disc}(I_{2})}=H_{\mathcal{R}/\left(\mathcal{S}_{disc}+\phi_{disc}\left(LI_{bd}^{disc}(I_{2})\right)\right)}.

Conveniently, we have

\mathcal{S}_{disc}+\phi_{disc}\left(LI_{bd}^{disc}(I_{2})\right)=\mathcal{I}_{n,m}+\left\langle\left\{x_{(S\;|\;T)}:\text{ $|S|\geq 2$}\right\}\right\rangle,

where for
b\in[n]
and
c\in[m]
,

(8.1)

\mathcal{I}_{b,c}:=\left\langle\left\{x_{i_{1},j_{1}}x_{i_{2},j_{2}}:1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq b,1\leq j_{2}<j_{1}\leq c\right\}\right\rangle,

where here and henceforth we will just write
x_{i,j}
for
x_{(i\;|\;j)}
. In other words,
\mathcal{I}_{b,c}\subseteq\mathcal{S}^{bd}_{disc}\subseteq\mathcal{R}
clearly denotes the ideal generated by all two variable products, where the subscripts of the variables correspond to
1
-minors of the top-left
b\times c
submatrix of
X
, such that the product of these
1
-minors is a non-standard bideterminant. For example,
\mathcal{I}_{2,3}=\left\langle x_{12}x_{21},x_{13}x_{21},x_{13}x_{22}\right\rangle
.

The key fact we are using here and below is that, in this particular case, because our bd-Gröbner basis consists of all minors of size
\geq 2
, after modding out by those, the only straightening rules that are left are those for variables corresponding to (products of pairs of)
1\times 1
minors. Using
\C[X]:=\C\left[\left\{x_{(i\;|\;j)}\right\}_{i\in[n],j\in[m]}\right]
for brevity, we see that this is encapsulated in the isomorphism:

{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{R}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\left(\mathcal{S}_{disc}+\phi_{disc}\left(LI_{bd}^{disc}(I_{2})\right)\right)$}\right.}\cong_{\text{graded $\C$-alg}}{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\C[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{I}_{n,m}$}\right.},

from which it follows that we just need to calculate the Hilbert–Poincaré series of
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\C[X]$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathcal{I}_{n,m}$}\right.}
. To have a grading consistent with the others, note that
\C[X]
is given the standard grading (every variable
x_{i,j}
has degree 1, as the variables generating
\C[X]
correspond to
1\times 1
minors).

To apply the inductive algorithm, we will show the following lemma:

Lemma 8.6
.

For any fixed
b,c
, suppose that
A\subseteq\{x_{i,j}:i\in[n],j\in[m]\}
is such that for all
x\in A
and for all
y\in\mathcal{I}_{b,c}
,
x\nmid y
. Then we have

(8.2)

g(A,\mathcal{I}_{b,c}):=H_{\C[X]/(\left\langle A\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c})}=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{\min(b,c)-1}\binom{b-1}{i}\binom{c-1}{i}t^{i}}{(1-t)^{nm-(b-1)(c-1)-|A|}}.

We note that the preceding equation is already in reduced form: since the coefficients in the numerator are all strictly positive,
1
is not a root of the numerator, so
(1-t)
does not divide the numerator.

First let us see that this gives the dimension:

Corollary 8.7
.

For all
1\leq n\leq m
we have

(8.3)

g(\emptyset,\mathcal{I}_{n,m})=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{i}\binom{m-1}{i}t^{i}}{(1-t)^{n+m-1}}.

In particular, the (affine) variety of rank
1

n\times m
matrices has dimension
n+m-1
.

Proof.

The result on
g(\emptyset,\mathcal{I}_{n,m})
follows by plugging in
A=\emptyset
to Lemma
8.3
. Since the expression above is in reduced form, the power of
(1-t)
in the denominator gives the Krull dimension (Thm.
8.1
(III) or
[
KR05
, Definition 5.4.1 + Theorem 5.6.36]
).
∎

We now come to the proof of the lemma.

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

We will prove Equation (
8.2
) by induction on
b+c
. For the base case, we will prove Equation (
8.2
) when
\min(b,c)=1
, or equivalently,
\mathcal{I}_{b,c}=0
. Without loss of generality, suppose
b=1
. For any
A\subseteq\{x_{1,j}\}_{j\in[c]}
, we get

g(A,\left\langle 0\right\rangle)=H_{\C[X]/\left\langle A\right\rangle}=\frac{1}{(1-t)^{nm-|A|}},

where the final equality is by Line
5
and Line
9
of Algorithm
1
. This proves the base case.

For the rest of this section, we shall use the
\sqcup
symbol to denote a union of sets that we claim are disjoint.

We now have
\min(b,c)=2
, and by the induction hypothesis, we are guaranteed that Equation (
8.2
) holds for all smaller values of
b+c
. To derive
g(A,\mathcal{I}_{b,c})
, we shall run Algorithm
1
on the monomial ideal
\left\langle A\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c}
. In Line
11
, we will select
x_{1,c}
(which occurs in the monomial
x_{1,c}x_{2,c-1}\in\mathcal{I}_{b,c}
; here we are using the fact that
\min(b,c)\geq 2
). The monomial ideal in the first recursive call in Line
12
is

I^{(1)}_{1}:=\left(\left\langle A\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c}+\left\langle x_{1,c}\right\rangle\right).

In Claim
8.3
, we will show that

I^{(1)}_{1}=\left(\left\langle A\sqcup\{x_{1,c}\}\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c}\right).

The monomial ideal in the second recursive call in Line
12
is

I^{(1)}_{2}:=\left(\left\langle A\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c}\right):\left\langle x_{1,c}\right\rangle.

In Claim
8.3
, we will show that

I^{(1)}_{2}=\left\langle A\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}}\right\rangle,

and that
I^{(1)}_{2}
can be handled by the induction hypothesis. In the recursive call with
I_{1}^{(1)}
, we will now choose
x_{2,c}
in Line
11
. After doing so, the monomial ideals in calls in Line
12
are

I^{(2)}_{1}:=I_{1}^{(1)}+\left\langle x_{2,c}\right\rangle\quad\text{and}\qquad I^{(2)}_{2}:=I_{1}^{(1)}:\left\langle x_{2,c}\right\rangle,

satisfying

H_{\C[X]/I_{1}^{(1)}}=H_{\C[X]/I_{1}^{(2)}}+t\cdot H_{\C[X]/I_{2}^{(2)}}.

Again, Claim
8.3
will show that

I^{(2)}_{1}=\left(\left\langle A\sqcup\{x_{1,c},x_{2,c}\}\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad I^{(2)}_{2}=\left\langle A\sqcup\{x_{1,c}\}\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}2<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{2,c-1},

and that
I^{(2)}_{2}
can be handled by the induction hypothesis.

This pattern will continue. Every time we choose
x_{j,c}
, for
j\in[b-1]
, in Line
11
, Claim
8.3
will show that
I_{2}^{(j)}
can be handled by the induction hypothesis. Finally, for
j=b-1
, Claim
8.3
will also show that
I_{1}^{(b-1)}
can be handled by the induction hypothesis as well, thus closing the recursion.

Claim 8.8

(store=claimcolonidealrecursivecall)
.

Let
I_{1}^{(0)}=\left\langle A\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c}
. For
j\in[b-1]
, define

(8.4)

I_{1}^{(j)}=I_{1}^{(j-1)}+\left\langle x_{j,c}\right\rangle\qquad\text{and}\qquad I_{2}^{(j)}=I_{1}^{(j-1)}:\left\langle x_{j,c}\right\rangle.

By definition

(8.5)

H_{\C[X]/I_{1}^{(j)}}=H_{\C[X]/I_{1}^{(j+1)}}+t\cdot H_{\C[X]/I_{2}^{(j+1)}}.

For all
j\in[b-1]
, we have

(8.6)

I_{1}^{(j)}=\left\langle A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j]}\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c},

and

(8.7)

I_{2}^{(j)}=\left\langle A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j-1]}\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{j,c-1}.

Consequently,

(8.8)

H_{\C\left[X\right]/I_{2}^{(j)}}=g(A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j-1]}\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}},\mathcal{I}_{j,c-1}),

and

(8.9)

H_{\C\left[X\right]/I_{1}^{(b-1)}}=g(A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[b-1]},\mathcal{I}_{b,c-1}).

We defer the proof of Claim
8.3
to Appendix
D.2
.

We deduce

\displaystyle g(A,\mathcal{I}_{b,c})=H_{\C[X],(\left\langle A\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c})}

\displaystyle\,=H_{\C[X],I_{1}^{(1)}}+t\cdot H_{\C[X],I_{2}^{(1)}}

[Line
12
of Algorithm
1
]

\displaystyle\,=H_{\C[X],I_{1}^{(1)}}+t\cdot g(A\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}},\mathcal{I}_{1,c-1})

[by Equation (
8.8
)]

\displaystyle\,=H_{\C[X],I_{1}^{(2)}}+t\cdot H_{\C[X],I_{2}^{(2)}}+t\cdot g(A\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}},\mathcal{I}_{1,c-1})

[by Equation (
8.5
)]

\displaystyle\,=H_{\C[X],I_{1}^{(2)}}+t\cdot g(A\sqcup\{x_{1,c}\}\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}2<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}},\mathcal{I}_{2,c-1})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+t\cdot g(A\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}1<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}},\mathcal{I}_{1,c-1})

[by Equation (
8.8
)]

\displaystyle\,=H_{\C[X],I_{1}^{(2)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+t\cdot\left(\sum_{j=1}^{2}g(A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j-1]}\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}},\mathcal{I}_{j,c-1})\right)

\displaystyle\,=H_{\C[X],I_{1}^{(b-1)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+t\cdot\left(\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}g(A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j-1]}\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}},\mathcal{I}_{j,c-1})\right)

[telescoping with (
8.5
) and (
8.8
)]

\displaystyle\,=g(A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[b-1]},\mathcal{I}_{b,c-1})

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+t\cdot\left(\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}g(A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j-1]}\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}},\mathcal{I}_{j,c-1})\right)

[by Equation (
8.9
)]

\displaystyle\,=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{\min(b,c-1)-1}\binom{b-1}{i}\binom{c-2}{i}t^{i}}{(1-t)^{nm-(b-1)(c-2)-|A|-(b-1)}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+t\cdot\left(\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{\min(j,c-1)-1}\binom{j-1}{i}\binom{c-2}{i}t^{i}}{(1-t)^{nm-(j-1)(c-2)-|A|-(j-1)-(c-1)(b-j)}}\right)

[induction hypothesis]

(8.10)

\displaystyle\,=\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{\min(b,c-1)-1}\binom{b-1}{i}\binom{c-2}{i}t^{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}\sum_{i=0}^{\min(j,c-1)-1}\binom{j-1}{i}\binom{c-2}{i}t^{i+1}}{(1-t)^{nm-(b-1)(c-1)-|A|}}.

The denominator of Equation (
8.10
) matches the denominator of the RHS of Equation (
8.2
), thus we only need to verify that the numerators match.

Claim 8.9

(store=claimnumeratorsmatch)
.

The numerators of Equation (
8.10
) and the RHS of Equation (
8.2
) match.

Claim
8.3
, whose proof is deferred to Appendix
D.2
, completes the proof.
∎

9.
Future directions and open questions

While we have laid much of the groundwork for this theory, much also remains to be done. We highlight here a few avenues we find particularly interesting and think should be approachable.

9.1.
Algorithms and complexity

As one of the motivations of our theory is potential computational advantages—both for theoretical, “by-hand” computations, and algorithms implemented in software—it would be natural, interesting, and useful to implement our ideas in a standard computer algebra system. This would be especially interesting if implemented in a way that allowed one to incorporate more efficient straightening algorithms for the underlying pseudo-ASL in a modular fashion.

Open Question 9.1
.

What is the complexity of computing pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases? Can they always be computed uses an exponential amount of space?

Because ordinary Gröbner bases are a special case of pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases, computing pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases in general is at least as hard as computing ordinary Gröbner bases. In particular, it is
\mathsf{EXPSPACE}
-hard
[
MM82
]
. A positive answer to the above question would show that this complexity bound gives a tight characterization. Nonetheless, it is also possible that for particular pseudo-ASLs, computing pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases might be easier, or strictly harder; are there nontrivial examples of this phenomenon?

In the case of the ASL structure given by the bideterminant basis, our work highlights the following question:

Open Question 9.2
.

What is the complexity of straightening bideterminants? We conjecture that it is
\mathsf{\#P}
-hard.

Here the input is a single, not necessarily standard, bitableau
[R\;|\;C]
, and one must compute the set of standard bideterminants occurring in the expansion of
(R\;|\;C)
in the basis of standard bideterminants, as well as their coefficients. One can ask more fine-grained, related questions. For example, consider the problem: given a (not necessarily standard) bitableaux
[R\;|\;C]
and a standard bitableau
[R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime}]
, compute the coefficient of
(R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime})
in the expansion of
(R\;|\;C)
in the basis of standard bideterminants. Is this problem in
\mathsf{GapP}
? Is computing the absolute value of the coefficient in
\mathsf{\#P}
and the sign in
\mathsf{FP}
? What is the complexity of deciding whether
(R^{\prime}\;|\;C^{\prime})
occurs in
(R\;|\;C)
with nonzero coefficient? Is it in
\mathsf{PSPACE},\mathsf{PH},\mathsf{NP},\mathsf{P}
, or
\mathsf{C_{=}P}
? Is it
\mathsf{NP}
-hard?

Our conjecture of
\mathsf{\#P}
-hardness is based partly on intuition from computing many examples and seeing the complicated structure, and partly on
\mathsf{\#P}
-hardness results for other related problems on Young tableaux (e. g.,
[
Nar06
,
BI08
,
FI20
,
ARY21
]
). Although there have been practical improvements in the complexity of straightening bideterminants
[
Dés80
,
SL18
]
, to our knowledge their formal complexity has not been analyzed.

A question of a similar flavor is the complexity of computing the set of LCMs of two standard bitableaux relative to
A_{gen}
; we have currently left our algorithms
get-admissible-shapes
and
get-admissible-standard-bd
above as naive exhaustive searches over combinatorial spaces that are easily seen to have exponential size. Is this necessary, that is, can a pair of standard bideterminants of an
n\times n
matrix and degree
n^{O(1)}
in general have
\geq\exp^{n^{\Omega(1)}}
distinct LCMs? If so, can those LCMs at least be enumerated by an algorithm with
n^{O(1)}
delay?

9.2.
Theory and geometry

In the theory of ordinary Gröbner bases, the leading ideal is a flat deformation of the original ideal, which implies that the two share many invariants, and reduces the computation of such invariants to the case of monomial ideals. We believe, especially in light of our results on Hilbert series and dimension (Section
8
), that a similar geometric picture should be true for pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases. In particular, we conjecture the following:

Conjecture 9.3

(Flatness for pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases)
.

(1)

Given
(A,\preceq)
,
A_{gen}
is a flat deformation of
A
.

(2)

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
,
A_{lt}
is a flat deformation of
A_{gen}
(through algebras of leading terms), and
A_{disc}
is a flat deformation of
A_{lt}
(through algebras of leading terms).

(3)

Given
(A,\preceq)
, if two algebras of leading terms are each flat deformations of the other, then the natural bijection between their standard monomials is in fact an isomorphism of algebras.

(4)

Given
(A,\preceq)
and two algebras of leading terms
A_{lt},A^{\prime}_{lt}
, let
Z
(resp.,
Z^{\prime}
) be the set of those pairs of monomials in
\W^{H}\times\W^{H}
whose products become
0
in
A_{lt}
(resp.,
A^{\prime}_{lt}
). If
Z\subseteq Z^{\prime}
, then
A^{\prime}_{lt}
is a flat deformation of
A_{lt}
.

(5)

Given
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
and an ideal
I\subseteq A
,
{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$A_{lt}$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)$}\right.}
is a flat deformation of
{\raisebox{2.04439pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-2.04439pt}{$I$}\right.}
.

As the proof of flat deformations for Gröbner bases that we are aware of uses the fact that every term order is a lexicographic product of weighted-degree orders
[
Rob85
]
, a satisfactory resolution of the following question may be useful for proving the preceding conjecture:

Open Question 9.4
.

Characterize pseudo-ASL term orders. In particular, is there a characterization in terms of weights similar to Robbiano’s characterization
[
Rob85
]
in the case of term orders on polynomial rings?

A short proof shows that every pseudo-ASL term order on
A_{gen}
is isomorphic to an pseudo-ASL term order on
A
(via Proposition
4.1.3
), and therefore it suffices to characterize pseudo-ASL term orders on
A_{gen}
, which may be easier since the standard monomials form a multiplicative basis (up to scalars) in
A_{gen}
.

Next, we note that Section
7.5
was an instance in which there were two pseudo-ASL structures, each with pseudo-ASL term orders, on the same (or isomorphic) rings, that were in some sense compatible with one another (as in Lemma
7.5
). It would be interesting to better understand the relationship between pseudo-ASL Gröbner bases for a single ideal relative to multiple different pseudo-ASL structures. We note that on a polynomial ring
A
with
n
variables, any factorization
n=ab
gives a bideterminant pseudo-ASL structure viewing it as the coordinate ring of
a\times b
matrices. In fact, even just permuting the entries of a matrix of fixed size can give a different bideterminant pseudo-ASL on the same polynomial ring.

Lastly, in terms of further theoretical developments, we ask:

Open Question 9.5
.

Is there a pseudo-ASL analogue of Diana Taylor’s complex for monomial ideals
[
Tay66
]
(see
[
Eis95
, Ex. 17.11]
for a more widely-accessible description), but now for standard monomial ideals in a monomial pseudo-ASL?

9.3.
Bipermanents

Despite the superficial similarity between the permanent and determinant polynomials, our understanding of determinantal varieties and ideals is vastly more developed than our understanding of their permanent-based counterparts (see
[
GHSW25
,
BCMV25
]
for some recent work on permanental ideals). We saw how easy it was to obtain bd-Gröbner bases of determinantal ideals in Theorem
7.4
. It is possible that a theory of “bp-Gröbner ” bases could aid in understanding permanental ideals. A starting point towards such a theory is that there are also straightening laws for bipermanents
[
Cla82
,
All99
]
, though we do not know if they actually give an ASL structure, or even a pseudo-ASL structure.

Open Question 9.6
.

Does the coordinate ring of
n\times m
matrices admit a pseudo-ASL structure in which the standard monomials are standard bipermanents, and which admits a pseudo-ASL term order?

A positive resolution of the preceding question would thereby give a theory of “bp-Gröbner bases.” One might then hope to resolve the question of the codimension of the singular locus of the permanent hypersurface (highlighted in
[
Lan17
, Open Question 6.3.3.7]
; we believe it originates implicitly in
[
vzG87
]
) along the following lines: find a bp-Gröbner basis of the ideal of
n-1
subpermaments (the ideal corresponding to the singular locus of the permanent hypersurface), and apply the results of Section
8
(specifically Theorem
8.2
and Proposition
8.2
) to compute its dimension.

9.4.
Extensions to other settings

It would be interesting to extend our theory from pseudo-ASL Gröbner basis for ideals to bases of subalgebras, so-called SAGBI bases, but now in pseudo-ASLs.

Finally, as with ordinary Gröbner bases, we expect that the theory here should be extendible to pseudo-ASLs in certain mildly noncommutative settings. In fact, this work first arose out of the authors’ attempt to calculate the algebraic de Rham cohomology of certain determinantal varieties using Weyl algebras; the authors are thus working to extend the theory developed in this paper to (pseudo)-ASL structures on Weyl algebras. Denoting the Weyl algebra on
n
variables as over a ring
R
as
W_{n}(R)
, since

W_{n}(R)\cong_{R\text{-mod}}R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]\otimes_{R}R[\partial X_{1},\ldots,\partial X_{n}],

we immediately get an
R
-linear “bideterminant” basis, consisting of standard bideterminats in the
x_{ij}
times standard bideterminants in the
\partial_{ij}
. A theory of bd-Gröbner bases in Weyl algebras could help in studying annihilating D-ideals, which are crucial towards computing algebraic de Rham cohomology.

Proof of Lemma
4.1.2
.

The equivalence of the two conditions is a direct consequence of
[
ES96
, Cor. 1.6(b)]
, by Observation
4.1.2
.

To see the “furthermore:” suppose
G=\{m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}\}
is a generating set of
I
consisting only of standard monomials, and
m
is an arbitrary standard monomial. If
m_{i}|m
then
m\in I
. Conversely, if
m\in I
, we can write
m=\sum_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}m_{i}
for some
f_{i}\in A^{\prime}
. Write each
f_{i}
as a sum of standard monomials, say
f_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}r_{ij}m_{ij}
with
r_{ij}\in R
. Then we have

m=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{i}}r_{ij}m_{ij}m_{i}.

Because the product of standard monomials is zero or a standard term, the same is true of each
m_{ij}m_{i}
. Since the standard monomials form an
R
-basis of
A^{\prime}
, some nonzero multiple of
m
must occur in the set
\{m_{ij}m_{i}:i\in[k],j\in[k_{i}]\}
. Thus
m
is divisible by some
m_{i}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1.4
.

In this proof we will use the additional notation

LI_{sm}(I):=\{\text{LT}_{sm}(f):f\in I\}\subseteq A.

(Note that
LI_{sm}(I)=\pi_{lt}^{-1}(LI_{sm}^{lt}(I))
; it is not an ideal, but it is an
R
-subspace.)

First we show that
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I$}\right.}=Span_{R}(B)
. Suppose
f
is a nonzero element of
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$A$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$I$}\right.}
. Then there is a nonzero element
\hat{f}\in A
such that
\hat{f}\equiv f\pmod{I}
. If
\hat{f}
contains any standard terms that are in
LI_{sm}(I)
, then we may add elements of
I
to
\hat{f}
to get another
\hat{f}^{\prime}
such that
\hat{f}^{\prime}\equiv f\pmod{I}
and no standard term occurring in
\hat{f}^{\prime}
is in
LI_{sm}(I)
. But the elements of
B
are precisely the standard monomials not occuring in
LI_{sm}(I)
, and thus
\hat{f}^{\prime}
can be written as an
R
-linear combination of elements of
B
, and the same is true of
f
.

Next we show linear independence. Suppose that there is some nontrivial
R
-linear dependence among the elements of
B
, say
\sum r_{i}m_{i}\equiv 0\pmod{I}
where
r_{i}
are nonzero elements of
R
, and
m_{i}\in B
for all
i
. Then
\sum r_{i}m_{i}\in I
. Without loss of generality suppose we have indexed these monomials so that
m_{1}\succ m_{2}\succ\dotsb\succ m_{k}
. Then
m_{1}=\text{LM}_{sm}\left(\sum r_{i}m_{i}\right)
, and therefore
m_{1}\in LI_{sm}(I)
. But by assumption we have
m_{1}\in B
, contradicting the fact that
m_{1}\in LI_{sm}(I)
. Thus the elements of
B
are
R
-linearly independent modulo
I
.
∎

Proof of Observation
5.3
.

Clearly
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{1}-\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{1}))\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{1})
. Since
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are compatible, multiplying both sides here by
\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{2})
results in nonzero elements on both sides, and thus, by
(MATO-2)

\text{LM}_{sm}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{1}-\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{1}))\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{2}))\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{1})\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{2})).

Next, since the relevant products are nonzero by compatibility, by the module analogue of Equation (
4.3
) of Proposition
4.1.1
, this implies that

\text{LM}_{sm}((f_{1}-\text{LT}_{sm}(f_{1}))f_{2})\prec\text{LM}_{sm}(f_{1}f_{2}),

as required.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Let
G
be an ASL Gröbner basis for
I
with respect to
(A,\preceq,A_{lt})
.

(Existence and algorithm) First we discard certain elements of
G
to make it irredundant, and then we make it reduced.

For the algorithmic result, we will use the fact that division of standard terms in
A_{lt}
is computable (Proposition
6.1
).

To make
G
irredundant, if
g_{j}\mid_{lt}g_{i}
for some
j\neq i
, then remove
g_{i}
from
G
(if
g_{i}
and and
g_{j}
have the same leading monomial, we may remove either one of them). We claim that
G^{\prime}:=G\backslash\{g_{i}\}
is still an ASL Gröbner basis for the same ideal. Let
f\in\langle G\rangle
. Then by Proposition
4.1.4
, there is some
g\in G
such that
g\mid_{lt}f
. If
g\neq g_{i}
, then
g
is in
G^{\prime}
, and thus there is some
g\in G^{\prime}
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
. If
g=g_{i}
, then we have
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{j})\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i})\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
, and thus
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{j})\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
.

To see that
\langle G^{\prime}\rangle=\langle G\rangle
, suppose not. Let
f\in\langle G\rangle
be such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
is minimal according to
\preceq
among the leading terms of elements that are in
\langle G\rangle
but not in
\langle G^{\prime}\rangle
. By what we have said above, there is some
g\in G^{\prime}
such that
g\mid_{lt}f
. Let
t
be a standard term such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(gt)=\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
. Then
f-gt
is in
\langle G\rangle
, and
f-gt
cannot be in
\langle G^{\prime}\rangle
(for otherwise
f
itself would be). But
\text{LT}_{sm}(f-gt)\prec\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
, contradicting the assumed minimality of
\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
. Thus
G
and
G^{\prime}
generate the same ideal. Finally, by what we have said above, for all
f\in I
, there is some
g\in G^{\prime}
such that
\text{LT}_{sm}(g)\mid_{lt}\text{LT}_{sm}(f)
, and thus by Proposition
4.1.4
,
G^{\prime}
is an ASL Gröbner basis for
\langle G\rangle
.

Repeat the above process, removing elements of
G
, until the resulting set is an irredundant ASL Gröbner basis.

Now let
G
be an irredundant ASL Gröbner basis. We will show how to make it reduced.

Rescale each element of
G
to have leading coefficient
1
.

Let
B_{i}
be the set of standard monomials
m
appearing in
g_{i}
such that some other
g_{j}\mid_{lt}m
, and let
B=\bigcup_{i}B_{i}
. We proceed by downward induction on
m=\max B
. If
B
is empty, then we are done. Otherwise, let
m=\max B
(maximum according to
\preceq
), and let

\mathcal{I}_{m}=\{i\in\{1,\dotsc,k\}:m\in B_{i}\}.

Say
g_{j}\mid_{lt}m
. Then for each
i\in\mathcal{I}_{m}
, let
t_{i}
be a standard term such that
m
does not appear in
g_{i}-t_{i}g_{j}
. Replace
g_{i}
by
g_{i}-t_{i}g_{j}
. (Note that there is no circularity here, as
j
cannot be in
\mathcal{I}_{m}
because we had previously made
G
irredundant.) After doing this for all
i\in\mathcal{I}_{m}
, we strictly reduce
\max B
, and by well-orderedness of ASL term orders (Proposition
4.1.3
), this can happen at most finitely many times before the resulting
G
is reduced. This completes the algorithm and proof of existence.

(Uniqueness) As
LI_{sm}^{lt}(I)
is a standard monomial ideal in
A_{lt}
, by Lemma
4.1.2
, it has a unique minimum generating set of standard monomials, say
M
. Let
M=\{m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}\}
where

m_{1}\preceq m_{2}\preceq\dotsb\preceq m_{k}.

Now, suppose
G,G^{\prime}
are two reduced ASL Gröbner bases for
I
with respect to
(\preceq,A_{lt})
. We proceed by induction on
i
. Let
0\leq i<k
. Suppose by induction that we have found elements
g_{1},\dotsc,g_{i}\in G
such that
\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{j})=m_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq i
and all
g_{j}
are also in
G^{\prime}
; when
i=0
this set is empty and the “such that” is vacuously true. Then there are elements
g_{i+1}\in G
and
g^{\prime}_{i+1}\in G^{\prime}
such that

\text{LM}_{sm}(g_{i+1})=\text{LM}_{sm}(g^{\prime}_{i+1})=m_{i+1}.

If
g_{i+1}\neq g^{\prime}_{i+1}
, then
g_{i+1}-g^{\prime}_{i+1}
is nonzero and has leading term
m
strictly smaller than
\text{LT}_{sm}(g_{i+1})
. Note that
m
must occur among the standard monomials in either
g_{i+1}
or
g^{\prime}_{i+1}
. Without loss of generality, by swapping the roles of
G
and
G^{\prime}
if needed, suppose that
m
is in
g_{i+1}
. Since
G
is an ASL Gröbner basis, there must be some
g\in G
such that
g\mid_{lt}m
(by Proposition
4.1.4
). But since
m
is in
g_{i+1}
, this contradicts the reducedness of
G
. Thus we have
g_{i+1}=g^{\prime}_{i+1}
, as desired. By induction, we thus conclude that
G=G^{\prime}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

Let

\mathcal{L}=\{(LI_{sm}^{lt})_{\preceq}(I):\preceq\text{ is an ASL term order on $A$}\},

where here each element of
\mathcal{L}
is an ideal in
A_{disc}
, and suppose for the sake of contradiction that
\mathcal{L}
is infinite. Let
f_{1}
be a nonzero polynomial in
I
. Then every leading ideal of
I
, regardless of term order, must contain at least one of the monomials of
f_{1}
. Since
\mathcal{L}
is infinite, there must be at least one monomial, say
m_{1}
, that occurs in
f_{1}
, and such that
m_{1}
is contained in infinitely many ideals in
\mathcal{L}
.

Now suppose by induction that we have found
k\geq 1
monomials
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}
such that there are infinitely many elements of
\mathcal{L}
containing
\{m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}\}
; call the resulting set of ideals
\mathcal{L}_{k}
. Let
\preceq
be a term order such that the leading ideal relative to
\preceq
occurs in
\mathcal{L}_{k}
, that is, contains
m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{k}
is infinite, in particular we have
|\mathcal{L}_{k}|\geq 2
, and thus there is some pseudo-ASL term order
\preceq
such that the leading ideal of
I
with respect to
(\preceq,A_{disc})
is in
\mathcal{L}_{k}
and is not equal to
\langle m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}\rangle
(just because the latter, if it is in
\mathcal{L}_{k}
at all, cannot be the only ideal in
\mathcal{L}_{k}
). Now, since
(LI_{sm}^{lt})_{\preceq}(I)\supsetneq\langle m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}\rangle
, there is some element
f_{k+1}\in I
whose
\preceq
-leading monomial is not in
\langle m_{1},\dotsc,m_{k}\rangle
. Among the finitely many standard monomials in
f_{k+1}
, every leading ideal must contain at least one of them. Since
\mathcal{L}_{k}
is infinite, there is some monomial
m_{k+1}
occurring in
f_{k+1}
such that infinitely many of the ideals in
\mathcal{L}_{k}
contain
m_{k+1}
.

By induction, this gives us a strictly increasing chain of (standard monomial) ideals in
A_{disc}
:

\langle m_{1}\rangle\subsetneq\langle m_{1},m_{2}\rangle\subsetneq\langle m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}\rangle\subsetneq\dotsb.

But this contradicts the fact that
A_{disc}
is Noetherian.
∎

Appendix C
Straightening algorithm of
[
DKR78
]

This section gives algorithmic details about Theorem
7.1.2
-
(I)
. Lemma
C
below from
[
DKR78
]
helps rewrite a bideterminant
(R\;|\;C)
of length
2
, i.e. a product of two minors, as a sum of two kinds of terms. The first kind contains bideterminants of length
2
, and of the same shape as that of
(R\;|\;C)
. The second kind contains bideterminants of length
3
, with a shape strictly smaller than that of
(R\;|\;C)
. All bideterminants have the same content; the only difference is that the elements in the tableaux are
shuffled
around.

Proof sketch of Theorem
7.1.2
-
(I)
.

Straightening of any arbitrary bideterminant is done by recursively applying Lemma
C
. If a bideterminant is non-standard, then it is sure to contain at least two minors that are incomparable according to the partial order on minors (c.f. Definition
7.1.2
). In symbols, suppose that without loss of generality,
s\geq t
,
i_{1}<\ldots i_{s}
,
j_{1}<\ldots<j_{s}
,
l_{1}<\ldots<l_{n}
, and
t_{1}<\ldots<t_{n}
, and that the two
clashing
minors are

\left(\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$i_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\vdots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$i_{s}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\vdots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$j_{s}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\left(\,\left.{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$l_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\vdots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$l_{n}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\;\right|\;{\hbox{=100$\vbox{\halign{&\mkcell{#}\cr\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$t_{1}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$\vdots$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\\\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss$t_{n}$\hss}\vss}\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\vbox to0.4pt{\hrule depth=0.4pt,height=0.0pt\vss\hbox to0.4pt{\vrule width=0.4pt,height=0.4pt\hss\vrule width=0.4pt}\vss\hrule height=0.4pt,depth=0.0pt}\kern-0.2pt}\kern-0.2pt\crcr}}$}}\,\right).}}}}}}}}}}}}

This means that, again without loss of generality, we have an index
r\leq n
such that
i_{\alpha}\leq l_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha<r
, and
i_{r}>l_{r}
. This in turn means we have a sequence
l_{1}<\ldots<l_{r}<i_{r}<i_{r+1}<\ldots<i_{s}
, which is a strictly increasing sequence of length
s+1
. Thus the hypotheses of Lemma
C
are satisfied, and we can rewrite the above product of two minors. In
[
DKR17
, Theorem 3.2]
it is shown that consistently repeating this procedure will eventually terminate in finding an expression that only contains standard bideterminants.
∎

Appendix D
Supplement to Section
8

D.1.
Algorithm to compute Hilbert–Poincaré series in the polynomial ring

Algorithm
1
computes the Hilbert–Poincaré series for a monomial ideal in the polynomial ring
R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
. Algorithm
1
works for any
\N
-grading reposed on
R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]
. The presentation is adapted from
[
CLO15
, Chapter 10 - §2, Theorem 6]
which is tailored to the standard grading.

Algorithm 1

Algorithm to compute
H_{R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}],I}
when
I
is a monomial ideal, where
\deg(X_{i})=d_{i}
for all
i

1:

A monomial ideal
I\subseteq R[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}]

2:

procedure

HPS
(
I
)

3:

T\leftarrow
minimal set of generators of
I

4:

if

T=\emptyset

then

5:

return

\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{(1-t^{d_{i}})}

6:

else

if

T=\{1\}

then

7:

return

0

8:

else

if

T
consists of variables
x_{i_{1}},\dotsc,x_{i_{p}}

then

9:

return

\prod_{i\notin\{i_{1},\dotsc,i_{p}\}}\frac{1}{(1-t^{d_{i}})}

10:

else

11:

Select
x_{i}
appearing in a monomial in
T
that is more than just a lone variable

12:

Return
\textsc{HPS}(I+\left\langle x_{i}\right\rangle)+t^{d_{i}}\cdot\textsc{HPS}(I:x_{i})

13:

end

if

14:

end

procedure

Proof of Claim
8.3
.

Equation (
8.5
) follows from Line
12
of Algorithm
1
.

Equation (
8.6
) is immediate from definition (
8.4
), and the fact that since no
x\in A
divides any
y\in\mathcal{I}_{b,c}
,
A\cap\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j]}=\emptyset
.

By definition

I_{2}^{(j)}=I_{1}^{(j-1)}:\left\langle x_{j,c}\right\rangle=\left(\left\langle A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j-1]}\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{b,c}\right):\left\langle x_{j,c}\right\rangle.

For any fixed
j
,
x_{j,c}
is incompatible with all of
\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}}
, thus all of them are present as degree
1
variables in
I_{2}^{(j)}
, which in turn eliminates all incompatibilities involving
\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}}
. In other words,
I_{2}^{(j)}
will not contain any incompatibilities from row
j+1
or higher. Also, since the degree
1
variables
\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j-1]}
are already present,
I_{2}^{(j)}
will not contain any incompatibilities involving
\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j-1]}
. Finally,
x_{j,c}
cannot be present in the list of incompatibilities because (a) no incompatibilities from row
j+1
or higher are present, and (b) nothing in row
1
to row
j
can be incompatible with
x_{j,c}
. Thus, we are left with all the incompatibilities in the top
j\times(c-1)
submatrix of
X
, i.e.
\mathcal{I}_{j,c-1}
. This completes the proof of Equation (
8.7
), i.e. that

I_{2}^{(j)}=\left\langle A\sqcup\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[j-1]}\sqcup\{x_{r,s}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}j<r\leq b\\
1\leq s\leq c-1\end{subarray}}\right\rangle+\mathcal{I}_{j,c-1}.

Equation (
8.8
) follows just by Equation (
8.7
) and by definition of
g
, i.e. (
8.2
).

Finally, note that (a)
I_{1}^{(b-1)}
contains
\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[b-1]}
are degree
1
variables, which in turn means all incompatibilities involving
\{x_{i,c}\}_{i\in[b-1]}
are no longer present in
I_{1}^{(b-1)}
, and (b)
x_{b,c}
is compatible with all elements of
X
. This in turn means that
I_{1}^{(b-1)}
contains
\mathcal{I}_{b,c-1}
, thus proving Equation (
8.9
).
∎

Proof of Claim
8.3
.

Without loss of generality, suppose that
b\leq c
. The numerator of Equation (
8.10
), say
P_{1}\in\Z[t]
, and the numerator of the RHS of Equation (
8.2
), say
P_{2}\in\Z[t]
, are both polynomials of degree exactly
b-1
. We will match the coefficients of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
. The coefficient of
t^{0}
in
P_{1}
is
\binom{b-1}{0}\binom{c-2}{0}=1
, which matches the coefficient of
t^{0}
in
P_{2}
.

Next we shall match the coefficients of
t^{b-1}
in
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
. If
b=c
, then the coefficient of
t^{b-1}
in
P_{1}
is equal to
\binom{b-2}{b-2}\binom{b-2}{b-2}=1
which matches the coefficient of
t^{b-1}
in
P_{2}
. Otherwise it means that
b<c
. Then the coefficient of
t^{b-1}
in
P_{1}
is equal to
\binom{c-2}{b-1}+\binom{b-2}{b-2}\binom{c-2}{b-2}=\binom{c-1}{b-1}
, which again matches the coefficient of
t^{b-1}
in
P_{2}
.

Finally, for any
b-2\geq d\geq 1
, the coefficient of
t^{d}
in
P_{1}
is

\displaystyle\binom{b-1}{d}\binom{c-2}{d}

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=d}^{b-1}\binom{j-1}{d-1}\binom{c-2}{d-1}

\displaystyle=\binom{b-1}{d}\binom{c-2}{d}+\binom{c-2}{d-1}\left(\binom{d-1}{d-1}+\binom{d}{d-1}+\ldots+\binom{b-2}{d-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\binom{b-1}{d}\binom{c-2}{d}+\binom{c-2}{d-1}\left(\binom{d}{d}+\binom{d}{d-1}+\ldots+\binom{b-2}{d-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\binom{b-1}{d}\binom{c-2}{d}+\binom{c-2}{d-1}\binom{b-1}{d}

\displaystyle=\binom{b-1}{d}\binom{c-1}{d},

which matches the coefficient of
t^{d}
in
P_{2}
.
∎
